Generalized cluster states in 2+1d: non-invertible symmetries, interfaces, and parameterized families
Kansei Inamura, Shuhei Ohyama

Abstract
We construct 2+1-dimensional lattice models of symmetry-protected topological (SPT) phases with non-invertible symmetries and investigate their properties using tensor networks. These models, which we refer to as generalized cluster models, are constructed by gauging a subgroup symmetry H ⊂ G H\subset G in models with a finite group 0-form symmetry G G . By construction, these models have a non-invertible symmetry described by the group-theoretical fusion 2-category 𝒞 ⁡ ( G , H ) \mathcal{C}(G;H) . After identifying the tensor network representations of the symmetry operators, we study the symmetry acting on the interface between two generalized cluster states. In particular, we will see that the symmetry at the interface is described by a multifusion category known as the strip 2-algebra. By studying possible interface modes allowed by this symmetry, we show that the interface between generalized cluster states in different SPT phases must be degenerate. This result generalizes the ordinary bulk-boundary correspondence. Furthermore, we construct parameterized families of generalized cluster states and study the topological charge pumping phenomena, known as the generalized Thouless pump. We exemplify our construction with several concrete cases, and compare them with known phases, such as SPT phases with 2 ​ Rep ​ ( ( ℤ 2 [ 1 ] × ℤ 2 [ 1 ] ) ⋊ ℤ 2 [ 0 ] ) 2\mathrm{Rep}((\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{[1]}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{[1]})\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{[0]}) symmetry.



I
Introduction and summary

I.1
Introduction

Symmetry-protected topological (SPT) phases are the simplest yet non-trivial phases of quantum many-body systems
Gu_2009
;
Pollmann:2009mhk
;
Pollmann_2010
;
Chen:2010zpc
;
Chen_2011_complete
;
Chen:2011bcp
;
Chen:2011pg
;
Schuch_2011
.
Despite their simplicity, they exhibit a surprisingly rich structure due to the symmetry constraints.
A defining feature of non-trivial SPT phases is the existence of protected edge modes.
The connection between SPT non-triviality and the presence of edge modes—often referred to as the bulk-boundary correspondence—has been established for various symmetries and spatial dimensions.
It is also well established that adiabatic and periodic driving of an SPT state can induce topological transport, known as the generalized Thouless pump, which has also been demonstrated for various symmetries and spatial dimensions

Thouless83
;
PhysRevB.82.115120
;
Kitaev2011SCGP
;
Kitaev2013SCGP
;
Kitaev2015IPAM
;
Kapustin:2020mkl
;
PhysRevResearch.2.042024
;
Tantivasadakarn:2021wdv
;
Shiozaki:2021weu
;
Hermele2021CMSA
;
Wen:2021gwc
;
Bachmann:2022bhx
;
Spodyneiko:2023vsw
;
Ohyama:2022cib
;
Inamura:2024jke
;
Jones:2025khc
;
Shiozaki:2025pyo
;
Li:2025wes
.

In a related but distinct context, recent studies have explored generalizations of symmetry
Gaiotto:2014kfa
.
As established in a seminal work by Wigner
Wigner1959
, conventional symmetries are represented by unitary or anti-unitary operators, which are invertible and form a group.
By contrast, within the framework of generalized symmetries, the invariance under the action of certain non-invertible operators is also regarded as a symmetry.
Correspondingly, the algebraic structure of such a symmetry, called non-invertible symmetry, is modeled by an appropriate (higher) category.
A notable example of a non-invertible symmetry is the Kramers-Wannier self-duality of the critical Ising model
KW1941
;
Frohlich:2004ef
;
Chang:2018iay
.
1
1

1

More precisely, the critical Ising model on the lattice is invariant under the Kramers-Wannier transformation followed by the lattice translation by half a site
Seiberg:2023cdc
;
Seiberg:2024gek
.

See
Cordova:2022ruw
;
McGreevy:2022oyu
;
Schafer-Nameki:2023jdn
;
Brennan:2023mmt
;
Bhardwaj:2023kri
;
Luo:2023ive
;
Shao:2023gho
;
Carqueville:2023jhb
;
Iqbal:2024pee
;
Costa:2024wks
for recent reviews of non-invertible symmetries.
Given this generalization, it is natural to investigate the properties of SPT phases in the presence of non-invertible symmetries.
There are various recent studies along this line
Thorngren:2019iar
;
Inamura:2021wuo
;
Inamura:2021szw
;

;
Fechisin:2023odt

;
ParayilMana:2025nxw
;

;
You:2025uxo
;
Lu:2025yru
.

Tensor networks provide an efficient representation of quantum many-body states, particularly those in gapped phases, including SPT phases; see
Cirac:2020obd
for a review.
Beyond this original motivation, tensor networks have increasingly been recognized as powerful tools for giving microscopic descriptions of non-invertible symmetries
Molnar:2022nmh
;
Garre-Rubio:2022uum
;
Lootens:2021tet
;
Lootens:2022avn
;
Gorantla:2024ocs
.
In particular, in 1+1-dimensional systems, matrix product states (MPS) and matrix product operators (MPO) provide a systematic framework for constructing topological invariants that distinguish different SPT phases with non-invertible symmetries
Garre-Rubio:2022uum
;
Inamura:2024jke
.
Tensor networks have also been employed to investigate the bulk-boundary correspondence and the generalized Thouless pumps for non-invertible SPT phases in 1+1 dimensions
Inamura:2024jke
;
Li:2025wes
.
Given the success of tensor network methods in 1+1d, it is natural to expect that they are also useful in studying non-invertible SPT phases in higher dimensions.

Non-invertible symmetries in higher dimensions are now being actively studied in both mathematics and physics.
In 1+1 dimensions, finite non-invertible symmetries are described by fusion 1-categories
Bhardwaj:2017xup
;
Chang:2018iay
;
Thorngren:2019iar
, which have been extensively studied for a long time; see
EGNO2015
for a review.
Similarly, in 2+1 dimensions, finite non-invertible symmetries are expected to be described by fusion
2
-categories, which were defined in
Douglas:2018qfz
and classified in
Decoppet:2024htz
(see also
Bhardwaj:2024qiv
;
Bullimore:2024khm
).
Recent studies have revealed various realizations of fusion 2-category symmetries in 2+1-dimensional systems, both on the lattice and in the continuum
Kaidi:2021xfk
;
Bhardwaj:2022lsg
;
Bhardwaj:2022maz
;
Bartsch:2022mpm
;
Bartsch:2022ytj
;
Delcamp:2023kew
;
Choi:2024rjm
;
Inamura:2023qzl
;
Hsin:2024aqb
;
Bhardwaj:2024qiv
;
Bullimore:2024khm
;
Cordova:2024jlk
;
Cordova:2024mqg
;
Cao:2025qhg
;
Bhardwaj:2025piv
;
Bhardwaj:2025piv
;
Eck:2025ldx
;
Vancraeynest-DeCuiper:2025wkh
;
Hsin:2025ria
;
Furukawa:2025flp
;

;
KNBalasubramanian:2025vum
.
In particular, in
Choi:2024rjm
, Choi, Sanghavi, Shao, and Zheng constructed two gapped lattice Hamiltonians with a certain fusion 2-category symmetry and showed that their ground states are in distinct SPT phases protected by the fusion 2-category symmetry.
More recently, lattice models for more general SPT phases were constructed in
Cao:2025qhg
;
Inamura:2025cum
using the gauging of finite group symmetries
Delcamp:2023kew
.
On the mathematical side, in
Decoppet:2023bay
, Décoppet and Yu established the classification of fusion
2
-categories admitting a fiber
2
-functor, as well as the classification of fiber
2
-functors of such fusion 2-categories.
While the result of
Decoppet:2023bay
is purely mathematical, from a physical point of view, it can be regarded as a categorical formulation of the classification of SPT phases with fusion 2-category symmetries.

Although there has been progress in various lattice realizations of SPT phases with fusion 2-category symmetry, there has not yet been a systematic understanding of bulk-boundary correspondence and the generalized Thouless pump for such SPT phases.
As in the case of 1+1 dimensions, it is expected that tensor network techniques allow a systematic study of edge modes (or more generally, interface modes) and topological pumping for 2+1d SPT phases with fusion 2-category symmetries.

In this paper, we construct Hamiltonians for 2+1d SPT phases protected by group-theoretical fusion 2-category symmetry
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
using gauging techniques in lattice systems following the construction in
Cao:2025qhg
;
Inamura:2025cum
.
2
2

2

We note that the group-theoretical fusion 2-category
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
includes
2\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{G}}
and
2\text{Rep}(\mathbb{G})
for any split 2-group
\mathbb{G}

Decoppet:2023bay
.

We further provide tensor network representations of the ground states and the symmetry operators.
Since the ground states exhibit a structure similar to that of the cluster state, we refer to them as generalized cluster states.
In addition, we identify the symmetry acting on interfaces---namely, the strip 2-algebra
3
3

3

The strip 2-algebra is a natural categorification of the strip algebra
Cordova:2024iti
. Strip algebras are known by various names in the literature, such as module annular algebras
Bridgeman:2022gdx
, boundary tube algebras
Jia:2024rzr
;
Choi:2024tri
;
Choi:2024wfm
;
Jia:2024zdp
;
Jia:2025yph
, and interface algebras
Inamura:2024jke
;
Lu:2025rwd
. Recent applications of strip algebras are also discussed in, e.g.,
Choi:2023xjw
;
Cordova:2024vsq
;
Copetti:2024onh
;
Copetti:2024dcz
;
Bhardwaj:2024igy
;
Cordova:2024nux
;
Heymann:2024vvf
;
AliAhmad:2025bnd
;

. See also
Kitaev:2011dxc
;

;

;
Bridgeman:2019axg
;
Bridgeman:2019wyu
;
Barter_2022
for earlier works on their relation to gapped excitations on gapped boundaries of 2+1d topological orders.

Gagliano:2025gwr
—and analyze its representations.
This allows us to systematically study interface modes between states belonging to different phases as well as within the same phase.
A more detailed summary of the paper is provided below.

I.2
Outline and summary

We begin with several remarks before presenting the outline and summary.
Since we develop the theory in a general setting, it is inevitable to introduce various notations and technical machinery, thereby rendering the paper long.
To improve readability, we include, as the final subsection of each section, an instantiation of the general theory.
These subsections focus on the case of the
G
-cluster state, which is a simple example of an SPT state with
2\mathrm{Rep}(G)\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{G}
symmetry.
Since most of the notation is defined in the main text rather than in the example subsections, we also provide a list of notation at the beginning of the paper for quick reference.
Accordingly, for readers who are not primarily interested in technical details or the general theory, the paper is organized so that it can be read in an almost self-contained manner by referring to the example subsections at the end of each section.

The structure of the paper is as follows.
In Section
II
, we provide some mathematical concepts, including fusion 2-categories and fiber 2-functors.
We also review a subset of the results proved in
Decoppet:2023bay
.
In Section
III
,
we introduce generalized cluster models in 2+1 dimensions, which realize SPT phases with fusion 2-category symmetries.
This model is constructed by gauging a subgroup symmetry
H\subset G
in a system with finite group
G
symmetry.
After constructing the Hamiltonian, we identify the ground state and provide its tensor network representation.
This section can also be read as a review of gauging finite group symmetries in lattice systems.
In Section
IV
, we identify the symmetry structure of the generalized cluster models.
The symmetry operators are constructed by composing the gauging and ungauging operators with onsite unitary operators.
By construction, these operators have a natural tensor network representation.
We observe that the bond dimensions of these tensor network operators can be reduced by using the fact that the symmetry is non-anomalous.
This may be viewed as the “onsiteability” of non-anomalous non-invertible symmetries in 2+1 dimensions in the same vein as
Meng:2024nxx
.
We also compute the partial action of these operators on a subregion of the system, and read off their action tensors on the ground state.
In Section
V
,
we study the symmetry structure at the interface—the strip 2-algebra—between two generalized cluster states.
We first define the strip 2-algebra as an endomorphism category of an object of a suitable 2-category.
As we will see, in general, the strip 2-algebra is not a fusion category but a multifusion category.
We then analyze the 2-category of modules over the strip 2-algebra, which physically corresponds to the 2-category of gapped interface modes.
We provide a (partly conjectural) description of the 2-category of modules as a functor category, and establish formulas for computing the category of interface modes.
We also give tensor network representations of the symmetry operators at the interface and compute their fusion rule, which agrees with that of the strip 2-algebra.
Using this fusion rule, we show that the interface between the generalized cluster states in different SPT phases must be degenerate, establishing a generalized version of the bulk-boundary correspondence.
This degeneracy cannot be lifted unless the symmetry is explicitly broken, even if the interface is gapless.
This is in contrast to the case of invertible symmetries, where the edge modes can be non-degenerate when they are gapless.
In Section
VI
,
we construct parameterized families of the generalized cluster states and study the generalized Thouless pumps.
To construct the families, we employ the method of symmetry interpolation, which produces an
S^{1}
-parameterized family that preserves the non-invertible symmetry of the generalized cluster state.
We then perform an adiabatic evolution to show that our parameterized families are non-trivial.
More specifically, we show that the adiabatic evolution along the parameter space
S^{1}
pumps a non-trivial self-interface mode, which is in a non-trivial representation of the strip 2-algebra constructed in Section
V
.
In Sections
VII
and
VIII
,
we present two classes of concrete examples.
The first class consists of general SPT phases that preserve the
2\mathrm{Rep}(G)\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{G}
symmetry; the
G
-cluster state mentioned above is an example of this class.
The second class consists of SPT phases that preserve the Tambara-Yamagami fusion 2-category symmetry.
For each class, we explicitly construct the tensor network representation of the ground state for each SPT phase and analyze the interface symmetries and parameterized families.

Various technical details are relegated to the appendices.
In Appendix
A
,
we discuss the dependence of the generalized cluster states on the choice of gauge fixing.
In Appendix
B
,
we discuss condensation surfaces and the condensation of line operators on them.
In Appendix
C
,
we provide detailed derivations of several technical results used in the main text.
In Appendix
D
,
we prove the uniqueness of the action tensors derived in Section
IV
.
In Appendix
E
,
we briefly review the basics of multifusion categories.
In Appendix
F
,
we provide an alternative gauge fixing for the Tambara-Yamagami cluster model discussed in Section
VIII
.
In Appendix
G
, we discuss the relation between the SPT phases of the models in
Choi:2024rjm
and those of the generalized cluster models.

I.3
Notations and conventions

Throughout the paper, we consider lattice models defined on a square lattice with periodic boundary conditions.
The sets of plaquettes, edges, and vertices of the square lattice are denoted by
P
,
E
, and
V
, respectively.
In all tensor network diagrams in the subsequent sections, the physical legs are supposed to be oriented upwards.

The following is a list of notations that we will employ in this paper.

•

G
: a finite group

•

H
: a subgroup of
G

•

K
: a complement of
H
in
G
, i.e., a subgroup of
G
that satisfies
HK=G
and
H\cap K=\{e\}
.

We note that every
g\in G
can be uniquely decomposed as

g=hk

(I.1)

for
h\in H
and
k\in K
.

•

S_{H\backslash G}
: a set of representatives of right
H
-cosets in
G

•

\mathfrak{r}[g]\in S_{H\backslash G}
: the representative of the right
H
-coset that contains
g\in G

4
4

4

For
g=hk
, we have
\mathfrak{r}[g]=\mathfrak{r}[k]
. The map
\mathfrak{r}|_{K}:K\to S_{H\backslash G}
is bijective due to the decomposition (
I.1
).

•

h[k]\in H
: the unique element of
H
that satisfies
\mathfrak{r}[k]=h[k]^{-1}k

•

U_{g;K}(h)\in H
: the unique element of
H
that satisfies
gh^{-1}=U_{g;K}(h)^{-1}k
for some
k\in K

•

\widetilde{U}_{g;S_{H\backslash G}}(h)\in H
: the unique element of
H
that satisfies
gh=\widetilde{U}_{g;S_{H\backslash G}}(h)r
for some
r\in S_{H\backslash G}

•

\overrightarrow{X}_{g}
,
\overleftarrow{X}_{g}
: the left and right multiplications of
g\in G

5
5

5

In
Fechisin:2023odt
,
\overleftarrow{X}_{g}
denotes the right multiplication of
g^{-1}
.

•

U_{g}
: the on-site symmetry operator for
g\in G
(i.e., the tensor product of
\overrightarrow{X}_{g}
’s on all sites)

•

\mathsf{D}_{H}
,
\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{H}
: the gauging and ungauging operators for a subgroup symmetry
H\subset G

•

\mathcal{C}(G;H)
: the 2-category of
(\text{Vec}_{H},\text{Vec}_{H})
-bimodules in
2\text{Vec}_{G}
(i.e., a group-theoretical fusion 2-category obtained by gauging
H
in
G
)

•

V^{g}
: a simple object of
2\text{Vec}_{G}

•

V_{H}^{g}=\text{Vec}_{H}\boxtimes V^{g}\boxtimes\text{Vec}_{H}
: a simple object of
\mathcal{C}(G;H)

•

\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]=\mathsf{D}_{H}U_{g}\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{H}
: the symmetry operator labeled by
V_{H}^{g}\in\mathcal{C}(G;H)

•

\ket{\text{cluster}(G;H;K)}
: a generalized cluster state with symmetry
\mathcal{C}(G;H)

•

\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
: the symmetry category at the interface of
\ket{\text{cluster}(G;H;K_{1})}
and
\ket{\text{cluster}(G;H;K_{2})}
(i.e., the strip 2-algebra)

•

\phi_{K}[V_{H}^{g}]
,
\overline{\phi_{K}}[V_{H}^{g}]
: the action tensors for
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]
acting on
\ket{\text{cluster}(G;H;K)}

•

\{P_{s}\}_{s=1}^{N}
: for a unitary operator
M
satisfying
M^{N}={\mathrm{id}}
,
P_{s}[M]
is the projector onto the eigenspace with eigenvalue
e^{i\frac{2\pi s}{N}}
, i.e.,
P_{s}[M]:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}e^{-i\frac{2\pi s}{N}n}M^{n}
.

•

\log M
: the logarithm of an operator
M
, i.e., an operator satisfying
e^{\log M}=M
. Such an operator is not unique. However, for a unitary operator
M
with
M^{N}=\mathrm{id}
, we always take

\log M=\sum_{s=0}^{N-1}i\frac{2\pi s}{N}P_{s}[M].

(I.2)

II
Preliminaries

In this section, we briefly recall the basics of fusion 2-categories and fiber 2-functors.
All 2-categories and 1-categories discussed in this paper are finite, semisimple, and
\mathbb{C}
-linear.

II.1
Fusion 2-categories

A fusion 2-category consists of various data that capture the algebraic structure of topological defects in 2+1 dimensions.
In what follows, we will briefly describe these data.
See
Douglas:2018qfz
for a precise definition of a fusion 2-category.

The most basic ingredients of a fusion 2-category are objects, 1-morphisms between objects, and 2-morphisms between 1-morphisms.
Physically, objects, 1-morphisms, and 2-morphisms correspond to topological surfaces, topological lines, and topological point operators in (2+1)-dimensional spacetime, cf. Figure
1
.

Figure 1:
An object
X
corresponds to a topological surface. A 1-morphism
x:Y\to X
corresponds to a topological line between
X
and
Y
. A 2-morphism
\chi:y\Rightarrow x
corresponds to a topological point operator between
x
and
y
. The magenta arrows perpendicular to the surfaces represent their coorientation.

An object is said to be simple if there is no non-trivial topological point operator on it.
Similarly, a 1-morphism is said to be simple if there is no non-trivial topological point operator on it.
6
6

6

In other words, a 1-morphism
x
is simple if the endomorphism space
\text{End}(x)
is one-dimensinal, and an object
X
is simple if the identity 1-morphism
{\mathrm{id}}_{X}
is simple.

Any objects and 1-morphisms are assumed to be isomorphic to finite direct sums of simple ones.
Physically, simple objects and simple 1-morphisms correspond to indecomposable topological surfaces and topological lines, respectively.

The fusion of topological surfaces is given by the tensor product of the corresponding objects.
For any pair of simple objects
X
and
Y
, their tensor product
X\square Y
can be decomposed into a finite direct sum of simple objects as

X\square Y\cong\bigoplus_{Z:\text{simples}}N_{XY}^{Z}Z,

(II.1)

where
N_{XY}^{Z}
is a non-negative integer.
The summation on the right-hand side is taken over all (isomorphism classes of) simple objects.
The unit object for this tensor product is denoted by
I
, which is supposed to be simple.
Physically, the unit object
I
corresponds to the identity surface.

Similarly, the fusion of topological lines is given by the composition of the corresponding 1-morphisms.
For any pair of composable 1-morphisms
x:Y\to X
and
y:Z\to Y
, their composition
x\otimes y:Z\to X
can be decomposed into a finite direct sum of simple 1-morphisms.
We note that the fusion of 1-endomorphisms of
X
is again a 1-endomorphism of
X
.
Accordingly, 1-endomorphisms of
X
(and the 2-morphisms between them) form a multifusion category
\text{End}(X)
.
7
7

7

A multifusion category is a finite semisimple
\mathbb{C}
-linear rigid monoidal category
EGNO2015
. A fusion category is a multifusion category whose unit object is simple.

By definition,
\text{End}(X)
is fusion if and only if
X
is simple.
See Figure
2
for the diagrammatic representation of the fusion of surfaces and lines.
The fusion of topological point operators is also defined similarly.

Figure 2:
The fusion of topological surfaces corresponds to the tensor product of objects, while the fusion of topological lines corresponds to the composition of 1-morphisms.

As in the case of fusion 1-categories, the associativity of the tensor product is captured by a natural family of 1-isomorphisms called associators:

\alpha_{X,Y,Z}:(X\square Y)\square Z\to X\square(Y\square Z).

(II.2)

The associators satisfy the pentagon equation up to a natural family of 2-isomorphisms called pentagonators:

(II.3)

The pentagonators must satisfy appropriate coherence conditions, which are represented by the commutativity of the Stasheff polytope
K_{5}

KV1994
;
gordon1995coherence
;
Gurski2007
.
The pentagonators are encoded in the collection of complex numbers called 10-j symbols
Douglas:2018qfz
, which are higher-dimensional generalizations of
F
-symbols in fusion 1-categories.
Throughout most of this paper, we will not explicitly use the data of the associators and pentagonators.

Connected components.

Simple objects
X
and
Y
are said to be connected to each other if and only if there exists a non-zero 1-morphism between them.
We note that
X
and
Y
can be connected even if they are not isomorphic to each other.
As a 2-category, a fusion 2-category
\mathcal{C}
can be decomposed into a finite direct sum of connected components as

\mathcal{C}\cong\bigoplus_{i=1,\cdots,n}\mathcal{C}_{i},\qquad n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}.

(II.4)

All simple objects in the same component are connected to each other, while any simple objects in different components are not connected.
Physically, two simple objects are connected if and only if they are related by the condensation
Kong:2014qka
;
Gaiotto:2019xmp
;
Johnson-Freyd:2020ivj
;
Kong:2024ykr
.
That is, for any pair of simple objects
X
and
Y
in the same connected component, the topological surface corresponding to
X
is obtained by condensing some line operators on the surface corresponding to
Y
, and vice versa.

Examples.

Let us provide some examples of fusion 2-categories that are relevant to later discussions.
Further examples will be discussed in Sections
II.2
and
II.4
.

•

Non-anomalous invertible 0-form symmetry
2\text{Vec}_{G}
.
The simplest non-trivial example of a fusion 2-category is the 2-category
2\text{Vec}_{G}
of finite semisimple
G
-graded 1-categories, where
G
is a finite group.
This 2-category describes a non-anomalous invertible 0-form symmetry
G
in 2+1 dimensions.
8
8

8

More precisely,
2\text{Vec}_{G}
describes the product of the 0-form symmetry
G
and a 2-form symmetry
\mathrm{U}(1)
generated by the identity point operator. The 2-form symmetry
\mathrm{U}(1)
exists in any quantum system in 2+1d.

Objects of
2\text{Vec}_{G}
are finite semisimple
G
-graded categories, 1-morphisms are functors that preserve the
G
-grading, and 2-morphisms are natural transformations between them.
Simple objects of
2\text{Vec}_{G}
are labeled by elements of
G
and are denoted by
V^{g}
for
g\in G
.
Here,
V^{g}
is a
G
-graded semisimple category defined by

V^{g}=\bigoplus_{g^{\prime}\in G}(V^{g})_{g^{\prime}},\qquad(V^{g})_{g^{\prime}}=\delta_{g,g^{\prime}}\text{Vec},

(II.5)

where
(V^{g})_{g^{\prime}}
is the componet of
V^{g}
with grading
g^{\prime}\in G
.
The fusion rules of these simple objects are given by

V^{g}\boxtimes V^{g^{\prime}}\cong V^{gg^{\prime}},

(II.6)

where
\boxtimes
denotes the Deligne tensor product.
The simple objects
V^{g}
and
V^{g^{\prime}}
are connected to each other if and only if
g=g^{\prime}
.
The 1-category of 1-endomorphisms of
V^{g}
is equivalent to
Vec
, whose unique simple object is given by the identity 1-morphism of
V^{g}
.
Namely, we have an equivalence of 1-categories

\text{Hom}_{2\text{Vec}_{G}}(V^{g},V^{g^{\prime}})\cong\delta_{g,g^{\prime}}\text{Vec}.

(II.7)

The 10-j symbols of
2\text{Vec}_{G}
are trivial.

•

Anomalous 0-form symmetry
2\text{Vec}_{G}^{\omega}
.
A slightly more general example of a fusion 2-category is
2\text{Vec}_{G}^{\omega}
, where
\omega\in Z^{4}(G,\mathrm{U}(1))
is a 4-cocycle on
G
.
This fusion 2-category describes an anomalous 0-form symmetry
G
with an anomaly
[\omega]\in H^{4}(G,\mathrm{U}(1))
.
Simple objects
V^{g}\in 2\text{Vec}_{G}^{\omega}
are labeled by elements of
G
and obey the fusion rules (
II.6
).
As in the case of
2\text{Vec}_{G}
, the 1-category of 1-morphisms between
V^{g}
and
V^{g^{\prime}}
is given by (
II.7
).
The 10-j symbols of
2\text{Vec}_{G}^{\omega}
are given by the 4-cocycle
\omega
.
When
\omega
is trivial,
2\text{Vec}_{G}^{\omega}
reduces to
2\text{Vec}_{G}
.

•

Non-anomalous non-invertible 1-form symmetry
2\text{Rep}(G)
.

Another example is the fusion 2-category
2\text{Rep}(G)
that describes the condensation completion of a non-anomalous non-invertible 1-form symmetry
\text{Rep}(G)

Douglas:2018qfz
;
Gaiotto:2019xmp
.
9
9

9

More precisely,
2\text{Rep}(G)
is defined as the 2-category of 2-representations of
G
and is monoidally equivalent to the 2-category of module 1-categories over
\text{Rep}(G)

(
Decoppet2023Morita
, Lemma 1.3.8)
. The latter 2-category is also equivalent to the condensation completion of
\text{Rep}(G)

Douglas:2018qfz
.

Here,
\text{Rep}(G)
denotes the category of finite dimensional representations of
G
.
The 1-form symmetry defects correspond to 1-endomorphisms of the unit object
I\in 2\text{Rep}(G)
and form a symmetric fusion 1-category

\text{End}_{2\text{Rep}(G)}(I)\cong\text{Rep}(G).

(II.8)

All simple objects of
2\text{Rep}(G)
are obtained from the unit object
I
by condensing some line operators of
\text{Rep}(G)
.
In particular, any simple object of
2\text{Rep}(G)
is connected to
I
.

II.2
Group-theoretical fusion 2-categories

Particularly simple examples of fusion 2-categories are those that arise from gauging subgroups of (0-form) finite group symmetries in 2+1 dimensions.
Such fusion 2-categories are called group-theoretical fusion 2-categories.
In this subsection, we provide a brief review of this class of fusion 2-categories following
Decoppet:2023bay
.
We refer the reader to
Bartsch:2022ytj
;
Bhardwaj:2022maz
;
Delcamp:2023kew
for more physical descriptions.
10
10

10

See also
Tachikawa:2017gyf
for the symmetries arising from the gauging of 0-form symmetries in general dimensions.

In later sections, we will study SPT phases with these symmetries.

Let us first introduce the notion of group-theoretical fusion 2-categories from a physics perspective.
To this end, we start from a 2+1d system with a finite group symmetry
G
that possibly has an anomaly
\omega\in Z^{4}(G,\mathrm{U}(1))
.
We then consider gauging a non-anomalous subgroup
H
with a discrete torsion, which is given by a 3-cochain
\nu\in C^{3}(H,\mathrm{U}(1))
such that
d\nu=\omega|_{H}^{-1}
.
Here,
\omega|_{H}
denotes the restriction of
\omega
to
H
.
The symmetry 2-category of the gauged model is described by a group-theoretical fusion 2-category, which is denoted by
\mathcal{C}(G,\omega;H,\nu)
.
When both
\omega
and
\nu
are trivial, this 2-category will be denoted by

\mathcal{C}(G;H):=\mathcal{C}(G,1;H,1).

(II.9)

In the rest of the paper, we will focus on the case where
\omega
and
\nu
are trivial.

Mathematically,
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
is defined as the 2-category of
(\text{Vec}_{H},\text{Vec}_{H})
-bimodules in
2\text{Vec}_{G}
, i.e.,
11
11

11

In general, the 2-category of
(A,B)
-bimodules in a fusion 2-category
\mathcal{C}
is denoted by
{}_{A}\mathcal{C}_{B}
, where
A
and
B
are algebras in
\mathcal{C}
. We note that
\text{Vec}_{H}
is an algebra object in
2\text{Vec}_{G}
.

\mathcal{C}(G;H)={}_{\text{Vec}_{H}}(2\text{Vec}_{G})_{\text{Vec}_{H}}.

(II.10)

Here,
\text{Vec}_{H}
is the 1-category of finite dimensional
H
-graded vector spaces.
Objects, 1-morphisms, and 2-morphisms of
\mathcal{C}(G,H)
are finite semisimple
G
-graded
(\text{Vec}_{H},\text{Vec}_{H})
-bimodule 1-categories,
(\text{Vec}_{H},\text{Vec}_{H})
-bimodule functors preserving the
G
-grading, and
(\text{Vec}_{H},\text{Vec}_{H})
-bimodule natural transformations.
The tensor product in
\mathcal{C}(G,H)
is defined by the relative tensor product over
\text{Vec}_{H}
, which is denoted by
\boxtimes_{\text{Vec}_{H}}
.
The unit object is given by the regular bimodule
\text{Vec}_{H}
.
The connected components of
\mathcal{C}(G,H)
are in one-to-one correspondence with double
H
-cosets in
G
.
Namely,
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
can be decomposed into a direct sum of connected components as

\mathcal{C}(G;H)\cong\bigoplus_{x\in H\backslash G/H}\mathcal{C}(G;H)_{x}.

(II.11)

The connected component
\mathcal{C}(G;H)_{x}
for
x\in H\backslash G/H
contains simple objects of the form

V_{H}^{g}:=\text{Vec}_{H}\boxtimes V^{g}\boxtimes\text{Vec}_{H},\quad\forall g\in x.

(II.12)

We note that
V^{g}_{H}\cong V^{g^{\prime}}_{H}
for all
g,g^{\prime}\in x
because
\text{Vec}_{H}\boxtimes V^{h}\cong V^{h}\boxtimes\text{Vec}_{H}\cong\text{Vec}_{H}
for all
h\in H
.
The fusion rules of the simple objects
V^{g}_{H}
and
V^{g^{\prime}}_{H}
can be computed as

V_{H}^{g}\boxtimes_{\text{Vec}_{H}}V_{H}^{g^{\prime}}\cong\bigoplus_{g^{\prime\prime}\in gHg^{\prime}}V_{H}^{g^{\prime\prime}},

(II.13)

See, e.g.,
ENO2010
;
Greenough:0911.4979
;
Douglas:1406.4204
for the relative tensor product of module categories over a monoidal category.

By definition, any simple object in
\mathcal{C}(G;H)_{x}
is connected to
V^{g}_{H}
for
g\in x
.
Physically, this means that the topological surface operator labeled by any simple object of
\mathcal{C}(G;H)_{x}
can be obtained by condensing some line operators on the surface operator labeled by
V^{g}_{H}
.
The line operators on the surface labeled by
V^{g}_{H}
are labeled by 1-endomorphisms of
V^{g}_{H}
, which form a fusion 1-category
Decoppet:2023bay
12
12

12

More precisely, in
Decoppet:2023bay
, it was shown that
\text{End}_{\mathcal{C}(G;H)}(V^{g}_{H})
is monoidally equivalent to
\text{Vec}_{H\cap gHg^{-1}}
. This is also monoidally equivalent to
\text{Vec}_{H\cap g^{-1}Hg}
because
H\cap gHg^{-1}
is isomorphic to
H\cap g^{-1}Hg
.

\text{End}_{\mathcal{C}(G;H)}(V^{g}_{H})\cong\text{Vec}_{H\cap g^{-1}Hg}.

(II.14)

For example, the line operators on the condensation defect labeled by
V_{H}^{e}
form
\text{Vec}_{H}
.
Condensing an algebra object
\mathbb{C}[H]\in\text{Vec}_{H}
on this condensation defect gives us back the identity surface, which corresponds to the unit object of
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
.

Example:
2\text{Rep}(G)
as a group-theoretical fusion 2-category.

The fusion 2-category
2\text{Rep}(G)
introduced in the previous subsection is an example of a group-theoretical fusion 2-category.
More specifically, there is an equivalence of fusion 2-categories
(
Decoppet:2023bay
, Example 3.1.2)
(see also
Delcamp:2021szr
)

2\text{Rep}(G)\cong\mathcal{C}(G;G).

(II.15)

Physically, this equivalence implies that the
2\text{Rep}(G)
symmetry is obtained by starting from a
G
-symmetric model in 2+1d and gauging the full symmetry
G

Bhardwaj:2022lsg
;
Bartsch:2022mpm
.
13
13

13

The invertible part of
2\text{Rep}(G)
symmetry was studied also in
Barkeshli:2022edm
.

In this context, the non-invertible 1-form symmetry
\text{Rep}(G)\cong\text{End}_{2\text{Rep}(G)}(I)
can be understood as the symmetry generated by the Wilson lines in the gauged theory.
The simple objects of the form
V^{g}_{H}
for
g\in G
are all isomorphic to each other because all
g\in G
are in the same double
G
-coset in
G
.
The corresponding symmetry operator is obtained by condensing the Wilson line for the regular representation
\mathbb{C}[G]^{*}\in\text{Rep}(G)
, which is equipped with the structure of the dual group algebra.

II.3
Fiber 2-functors

A fiber 2-functor of a fusion 2-category
\mathcal{C}
is a tensor 2-fucntor

F:\mathcal{C}\to 2\text{Vec},

(II.16)

where
2\text{Vec}
is the 2-category of finite semisimple 1-categories.
In
Decoppet:2023bay
, it was shown that a fusion 2-category
\mathcal{C}
admits a fiber 2-functor only when
\mathcal{C}
is group-theoretical, i.e.,
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}(G,\omega;H,\nu)
for some
G
,
\omega
,
H
, and
\nu
.
The classification of fiber 2-functors of
\mathcal{C}(G,\omega;H,\nu)
was given in
(
Decoppet:2023bay
, Corollary 4.1.4)
.
In particular, when
\omega
and
\nu
are trivial, a fiber 2-functor of
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
is labeled by a pair
(K,\lambda)
, where
K
is a subgroup of
G
that satisfies

HK=G,\quad H\cap K=\{e\},

(II.17)

and
\lambda\in Z^{3}(K,\mathrm{U}(1))
is a 3-cocycle on
K
.
A subgroup
K
that satisfies (
II.17
) is called a complement of
H
in
G
.
Fiber 2-functors labeled by
(K,\lambda)
and
(K^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})
are equivalent to each other if and only if there exists an element
g\in G
such that
K^{\prime}=gKg^{-1}
and
\lambda(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})/\lambda^{\prime}(gk_{1}g^{-1},gk_{2}g^{-1},gk_{3}g^{-1})
is a 3-coboundary on
K
.
The classification of fiber 2-functors for general
\omega
and
\nu
is similar, see
(
Decoppet:2023bay
, Corollary 4.1.4)
for more details.

Classification of SPT phases.

In 1+1 dimensions, it is known that bosonic SPT phases with fusion 1-category symmetry
\mathcal{D}
are in one-to-one correspondence with (isomorphism classes of) fiber functors of
\mathcal{D}

Thorngren:2019iar
;
Komargodski:2020mxz
.
Similarly, it is natural to expect that 2+1d bosonic SPT phases with fusion 2-category symmetry
\mathcal{C}
are in one-to-one correspondence with (isomorphism classes of) fiber 2-functors of
\mathcal{C}
.
Here, an SPT phase is an invertible gapped phase that can be continuously deformed into the trivial phase if we break the symmetry explicitly.
All SPT phases defined this way are non-chiral, i.e., they admit gapped boundaries, which may or may not preserve the symmetry.

To argue the correspondence between fiber 2-functors and SPT phases, we postulate that the symmetry
\mathcal{C}
of a topological field theory
\mathcal{T}
, which is the low-energy limit of a gapped phase, is implemented by a tensor 2-functor from
\mathcal{C}
to the 2-category
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{T}}
of topological defects in
\mathcal{T}
.
When
\mathcal{T}
is invertible, the 2-category
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{T}}
is equivalent to
2\text{Vec}
.
14
14

14

More generally, when
\mathcal{T}
is the Turaev-Viro-Barrett-Westbury TFT constructed from a fusion 1-category
\mathcal{D}

Turaev:1992hq
;
Barrett:1993ab
,
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{T}}
is equivalent to the 2-category
\mathrm{Bimod}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D})
of
(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D})
-bimodule categories
Fuchs:2012dt
;
Kitaev:2011dxc
;
Meusburger:2022zul
. The invertible TFT corresponds to the case of
\mathcal{D}=\text{Vec}
.

Therefore, the symmetry
\mathcal{C}
in the invertible gapped phase should be implemented by a fiber 2-functor of
\mathcal{C}
.
We expect that non-isomorphic fiber 2-functors correspond to different SPT phases because there is no continuous deformation between such fiber 2-functors.
Assuming in addition that isomorphic fiber 2-functors correspond to the same phase, we conclude that 2+1d SPT phases with symmetry
\mathcal{C}
are in one-to-one correspondence with isomorphism classes of fiber 2-functors of
\mathcal{C}
.

We emphasize that the finite non-invertible symmetry of a 2+1d SPT phase must be group-theoretical because a fusion 2-category admits a fiber 2-functor only when it is group-theoretical.
15
15

15

The converse is not true: not every group-theoretical fusion 2-category admits an SPT phase. The condition for the group-theoretical non-invertible symmetries in general dimension to admit an SPT phase was studied in
Hsin:2025ria
.

Therefore, to study SPT phases in 2+1d, it suffices to consider group-theoretical fusion 2-categories
\mathcal{C}(G,\omega;H,\nu)
.
In this paper, we restrict ourselves to the case where
\omega
and
\nu
are trivial.
According to the classification of fiber 2-functors, 2+1d SPT phases with symmetry
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
should be labeled by pairs
(K,\lambda)
.
Physically, these SPT phases are obtained by starting from a
G
-symmetric gapped phase that spontaneously breaks
G
down to
K
and gauging the subgroup symmetry
H
.
The 3-cocycle
\lambda
specifies the SPT class for the unbroken symmetry
K
before gauging.
In later sections, we will study lattice models for all of these SPT phases with trivial
\lambda
.

II.4
Example: Tambara-Yamagami 2-categories

Let us consider simple examples of group-theoretical fusion 2-categories known as Tambara-Yamagami 2-categories
Decoppet:2023bay
.
These are 2-categorical analogues of Tambara-Yamagami 1-categories
TY1998
.
We recall that a Tambara-Yamagami 1-category is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded fusion 1-category
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{D}_{1}
, where
\mathcal{D}_{0}=\text{Vec}_{A}
for a finite abelian group
A
and
\mathcal{D}_{1}=\text{Vec}
.
Similarly, a Tambara-Yamagami 2-category is defined as a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded fusion 2-category
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{C}_{1}
, where
\mathcal{C}_{0}=2\text{Rep}(A)\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{A}
for a finite abelian group
A
and
\mathcal{C}_{1}=2\text{Vec}
.
It was shown in
Decoppet:2023bay
that any Tambara-Yamagami 2-category is group-theoretical and is of the form

2\text{TY}(A,\zeta):=\mathcal{C}(A\wr\mathbb{Z}_{2},\zeta;A_{L},\nu).

(II.18)

Here,
A\wr\mathbb{Z}_{2}:=(A\times A)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is the wreath product of
A
by
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
,
\zeta
is a 4-cocycle on
A\wr\mathbb{Z}_{2}
that is trivializable on
A\times A\times\{0\}\subset A\wr\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, and
A_{L}
is a subgroup of
A\wr\mathbb{Z}_{2}
defined by

A_{L}:=\{(a,e,0)\mid a\in A\},

(II.19)

where
e\in A
and
0\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
denote the unit elements of
A
and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, respectively.
The above 2-category
2\text{TY}(A,\zeta)
does not depend on the choice of a 3-cochain
\nu\in C^{3}(A_{L},\mathrm{U}(1))
that satisfies
d\nu=\zeta|_{A_{L}}^{-1}
.
The group structure on
A\wr\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is given by

(a_{l},a_{r},n)\cdot(b_{l},b_{r},m)=\begin{cases}(a_{l}b_{l},a_{r}b_{r},n+m\bmod 2)\quad&\text{when $n=0$},\\
(a_{l}b_{r},a_{r}b_{l},n+m\bmod 2)\quad&\text{when $n=1$}.\end{cases}

(II.20)

The Tambara-Yamagami 2-category
2\text{TY}(A,\zeta)
is indeed a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded fusion 2-category

2\text{TY}(A,\zeta)=2\text{TY}(A,\zeta)_{0}\oplus 2\text{TY}(A,\zeta)_{1},

(II.21)

where
2\text{TY}(A,\zeta)_{0}\cong 2\text{Rep}(A)\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{A}
and
2\text{TY}(A,\zeta)_{1}\cong 2\text{Vec}
.
The unique simple object with a non-trivial grading is given by
D:=V_{A_{L}}^{(e,e,1)}
.
Due to (
II.13
),
D
obeys the fusion rule
Decoppet:2023bay

D\boxtimes_{\text{Vec}_{A_{L}}}D\cong\bigoplus_{a\in A}V_{A_{L}}^{(e,a,0)}\cong\bigoplus_{a\in A}V_{A}^{e}\boxtimes V^{a},

(II.22)

where
V_{A}^{e}\boxtimes V^{a}\in 2\text{Rep}(A)\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{A}\cong 2\text{TY}(A,\zeta)_{0}
.
The above fusion rule is reminiscent of the fusion rule of the non-invertible object of a Tambara-Yamagami 1-category
TY1998
.

Physically,
2\text{TY}(A,\zeta)
describes the symmetry of a 2+1d system that is invariant under gauging the 2-group symmetry
A^{[0]}\times A^{[1]}
, where the superscripts specify the form degrees.
In Section
VIII
, we will study 2+1d SPT phases with symmetry
2\text{TY}(A,1)
, i.e., the Tambara-Yamagami symmetry with trivial
\zeta
.
See Section
VIII.1
for the classification of fiber 2-functors of these fusion 2-categories.

Example:
A=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

When
A=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, the wreath product
A\wr\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is isomorphic to
D_{8}
, the dihedral group of order 8.
Thus, the Tambara-Yamagami 2-category for
A=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
with trivial
\zeta
can be written as

2\text{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2},1)=\mathcal{C}(D_{8};\mathbb{Z}_{2}),

(II.23)

where
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
on the right-hand side is a non-normal subgroup of
D_{8}
.
This Tambara-Yamagami 2-category is monoidally equivalent to the 2-category of 2-representations of a 2-group
(\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{[1]}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{[1]})\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{[0]}
, i.e., we have

2\text{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2},1)\cong 2\text{Rep}((\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{[1]}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{[1]})\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{[0]}).

(II.24)

This symmetry has been studied in both continuum field theories
Kaidi:2021xfk
;
Bartsch:2022mpm
;
Bhardwaj:2022maz
;
Bartsch:2022ytj
and lattice models
Choi:2024rjm
;
Cao:2025qhg
;
Furukawa:2025flp
.
We note that not every Tambara-Yamagami 2-category is monoidally equivalent to the 2-category of 2-representations of a finite 2-group.
In particular, when the order of
A
is odd,
2\text{TY}(A,1)
cannot be written as the 2-category of 2-representations of any finite 2-group
Decoppet:2023bay
.

III
Generalized cluster states in 2+1d

In this section, we define concrete lattice models for 2+1d SPT phases with group-theoretical non-invertible symmetry
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
.
As discussed in Section
II.3
, such SPT phases are expected to be classified by equivalence classes of pairs
(K,\lambda)
, where
K
is a complement of
H
in
G
(i.e., a subgroup of
G
that satisfies (
II.17
)) and
\lambda\in Z^{3}(K,\mathrm{U}(1))
is a 3-cocycle on
K
.
Our lattice models realize all of these SPT phases with trivial
\lambda
.
We refer the reader to
Inamura:2025cum
for more general gapped lattice models with more general fusion 2-category symmetries.
16
16

16

The lattice models constructed in
Inamura:2025cum
include those that realize all SPT phases with arbitrary group-theoretical fusion 2-category symmetry
\mathcal{C}(G,\omega;H,\nu)
. However, the properties of these phases were not studied in detail.

The ground states of our models are natural generalizations of the cluster state, and hence we call them generalized cluster states.
We will provide explicit tensor network representations of these states.

III.1
Generalized cluster models

Our lattice model is defined by gauging a subgroup
H
in a
G
-symmetric model that spontaneously breaks the symmetry
G
down to
K
.
By construction, the gauged model realizes a
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
-symmetric SPT phase labeled by
(K,1)
if
K
satisfies (
II.17
).
For concreteness, we will consider the model on a square lattice with periodic boundary conditions and fix the orientation of the edges as shown in Figure
3
.

Figure 3:
The square lattice on which our model is defined. We impose periodic boundary conditions in both directions. The orientation of the edges is fixed as shown above.

In what follows,
P
,
E
, and
V
denote the set of plaquettes, the set of edges, and the set of vertices of the square lattice.
The edge between the neighboring plaquettes
i,j\in P
is denoted by
[ij]\in E
, where
i
is on the left side of
[ij]
(cf. Figure
3
).

III.1.1
G
-symmetric model

Let us first define the
G
-symmetric model whose symmetry is spontaneously broken down to
K
.
The state space of the model is given by

\mathcal{H}=\bigotimes_{i\in P}\mathbb{C}^{|G|}.

(III.1)

Namely, we have a
|G|
-dimensional qudit on each plaquette.
The basis of the local state space on each plaquette
i
is denoted by
\{\ket{g}_{i}\mid g\in G\}
.
We will use the following shorthand notation for the basis of the total state space:

\ket{\{g_{i}\}}:=\bigotimes_{i\in P}\ket{g_{i}}_{i}.

(III.2)

The Hamiltonian of the model is given by the sum

\hat{H}=-\sum_{i\in P}\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}-\sum_{[ij]\in E}\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}.

(III.3)

The palquette term
\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}
and the edge term
\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}
are defined by

\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}\ket{g_{i}}_{i}=\frac{1}{|K|}\sum_{k\in K}\ket{g_{i}k}_{i},\qquad\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}\ket{g_{i}}_{i}\otimes\ket{g_{j}}_{j}=\delta_{g_{i}^{-1}g_{j}\in K}\ket{g_{i}}_{i}\otimes\ket{g_{j}}_{j},

(III.4)

where
\delta_{g_{i}^{-1}g_{j}\in K}
is one if
g_{i}^{-1}g_{j}\in K
and zero otherwise.
We note that
\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}
acts non-trivially only on the palquette
i
, while
\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}
acts non-trivially only on the neighboring plaquettes
i
and
j
.
The above model has a
G
symmetry, whose symmetry operators are defined by

U_{g}\ket{\{g_{i}\}}=\ket{\{gg_{i}\}},\quad\forall g\in G.

(III.5)

It is straightforward to check that
\hat{H}
commutes with
U_{g}
for all
g\in G
.

The Hamiltonian (
III.3
) consists of local commuting projectors, i.e., each local term satisfies

[\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i},\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{j}]=[\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i},\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{jk}]=[\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij},\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{kl}]=0,\qquad\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}^{2}=\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}^{2}=1.

(III.6)

Thus, the model is eactly solvable.
The ground states are obtained by acting with the projector
\prod_{i\in P}\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}\prod_{[ij]\in E}\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}
on generic states in
\mathcal{H}
.
One can immediately see that the (normalized) ground states are explicitly given by

\ket{\text{GS};\mu}:=\bigotimes_{i\in P}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{|K|}}\sum_{g\in x_{\mu}}\ket{g}_{i}\right),\qquad\mu=1,2,\cdots,|G/K|,

(III.7)

where
x_{\mu}
is a left
K
-coset in
G
, i.e.,
x_{\mu}=\{r_{\mu}k\mid k\in K\}
with
r_{\mu}\in G
a representative of
x_{\mu}
.

The above ground states spontaneously break the symmetry
G
down to
K
.
More precisely, the symmetry action on the ground states (
III.7
) is given by

U_{g}\ket{\text{GS};\mu}=\ket{\text{GS};\nu},

(III.8)

where
g=g_{\nu}g_{\mu}^{-1}
for any
g_{\mu}\in x_{\mu}
and
g_{\nu}\in x_{\nu}
.
In particular, each vacuum
\ket{\text{GS};\mu}
preserves the subgroup symmetry
g_{\mu}Kg_{\mu}^{-1}
, which is isomorphic to
K
.

III.1.2
Gauged model

Now, we gauge a subgroup
H
in the above
G
-symmetric model.

State space.

The state space of the gauged model is given by

\mathcal{H}_{\text{gauged}}=\hat{\pi}_{\text{Gauss}}\left[\left(\bigotimes_{i\in P}\mathbb{C}^{|G|}\right)\otimes\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}\left(\bigotimes_{[ij]\in E}\mathbb{C}^{|H|}\right)\right],

(III.9)

where
\hat{\pi}_{\text{Gauss}}
and
\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}
are the projectors that implement the Gauss law constraint and the flatness condition, respectively.
The details of these projectors will be explained shortly.
The dynamical degrees of freedom are
|G|
-dimensional qudits on the plaquettes and
|H|
-dimensional qudits on the edges.
The qudits on the plaquettes are called matter fields, while the qudits on the edges are called gauge fields.
The projector
\hat{\pi}_{\text{Gauss}}
is given by the product of local commuting projectors

\hat{\pi}_{\text{Gauss}}=\bigotimes_{i\in P}\hat{G}_{i}.

(III.10)

Each projector
\hat{G}_{i}
is the Gauss law operator defined by

\hat{G}_{i}=\frac{1}{|H|}\sum_{h\in H}\overrightarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}L_{h}^{(\partial i)},

(III.11)

where
\overrightarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}
and
L_{h}^{(\partial i)}
are defined by

\overrightarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}\ket{g}_{i}=\ket{hg}_{i},\quad L_{h}^{(\partial i)}\Ket{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}}=\Ket{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}}=\Ket{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}}.

(III.12)

These operators act non-trivially only on the plaquette
i
and the edges on the boundary of
i
, respectively.
We note that the product
\overrightarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}L_{h}^{(\partial i)}
acts as a gauge transformation around the plaquette
i\in P
.
Gauge equivalent configurations of the dynamical variables are identified in the image of
\hat{\pi}_{\text{Gauss}}
because

\hat{\pi}_{\text{Gauss}}=\hat{\pi}_{\text{Gauss}}\overrightarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}L_{h}^{(\partial i)},\quad\forall h\in H,\penalty\ \forall i\in P.

(III.13)

Thus, in the projected subspace, the matter fields and the gauge fields satisfy the Gauss law constraint.
Similarly, the projector
\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}
is given by the product of local commuting projectors

\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}=\bigotimes_{v\in V}\hat{F}_{v},

(III.14)

where each projector
F_{v}
imposes the flatness condition on the gauge fields around the vertex
v\in V
:

\hat{F}_{v}\Ket{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}}=\delta_{h_{1}h_{2},h_{3}h_{4}}\Ket{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}}.

(III.15)

Thus, in the image of
\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}
, the gauge fields satisfy the flatness condition everywhere on the square lattice.

Hamiltonian.

The Hamiltonian of the gauged model is obtained by introducing the minimal coupling between the matter fields and the gauge fields.
More specifically, the gauged Hamiltonian is given by

\hat{H}_{\text{gauged}}=-\sum_{i\in P}\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}^{\text{gauged}}-\sum_{[ij]\in E}\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}^{\text{gauged}},

(III.16)

where
\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}^{\text{gauged}}
and
\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}^{\text{gauged}}
are defined by

\displaystyle\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}^{\text{gauged}}\ket{g_{i}}_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|K|}\sum_{k\in K}\ket{g_{i}k}_{i},

(III.17)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}^{\text{gauged}}\ket{g_{i}}_{i}\otimes\ket{h_{ij}}_{ij}\otimes\ket{g_{j}}_{j}

\displaystyle=\delta_{g_{i}^{-1}h_{ij}g_{j}\in K}\ket{g_{i}}_{i}\otimes\ket{h_{ij}}_{ij}\otimes\ket{g_{j}}_{j}.

(III.18)

We note that the above operators commute with the projectors
\hat{\pi}_{\text{Gauss}}
and
\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}
, i.e.,

[\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}^{\text{gauged}},\hat{\pi}_{\text{Gauss}}]=[\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}^{\text{gauged}},\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}]=[\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}^{\text{gauged}},\hat{\pi}_{\text{Gauss}}]=[\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}^{\text{gauged}},\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}]=0,

(III.19)

This implies that the Hamiltonian (
III.16
) maps any state in
\mathcal{H}_{\text{gauged}}
to another state in
\mathcal{H}_{\text{gauged}}
because

\displaystyle\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}_{\text{gauged}}\penalty

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \ket{\psi}=\hat{\pi}_{\text{Gauss}}\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}\ket{\psi}

(III.20)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\penalty\ H_{\text{gauged}}\ket{\psi}=\hat{\pi}_{\text{Gauss}}\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}H\ket{\psi}\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ H\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}_{\text{gauged}}.

Therefore, the gauged Hamiltonian is well-defined as a linear map from
\mathcal{H}_{\text{gauged}}
to itself.

III.1.3
Gauge fixing

Let us fix the gauge in order to solve the model explicitly.
To this end, we first recall that the matter fields on the plaquettes can be shifted by any element of
H
by the gauge transformation:

\Ket{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}}\sim\Ket{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}}.

(III.21)

Here,
\ket{\psi}\sim\ket{\psi^{\prime}}
means that
\ket{\psi}
and
\ket{\psi^{\prime}}
are gauge equivalent.
In other words, they are identified with each other upon applying the Gauss law projector:

\ket{\psi}\sim\ket{\psi^{\prime}}\penalty\ \overset{\text{def}}{\Longleftrightarrow}\penalty\ \hat{\pi}_{\text{Gauss}}\ket{\psi}=\hat{\pi}_{\text{Gauss}}\ket{\psi^{\prime}}.

(III.22)

Using this gauge transformation, we can always shift the matter field
g_{i}\in G
to a representative of the right
H
-coset
Hg_{i}=\{hg_{i}\mid h\in H\}
.
This operation is referred to as gauge fixing.
The state space after the gauge fixing can be written as

\mathcal{H}_{\text{g.f.}}=\left(\bigotimes_{i\in P}\mathbb{C}^{|S_{H\backslash G}|}\right)\otimes\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}\left(\bigotimes_{[ij]\in E}\mathbb{C}^{|H|}\right),

(III.23)

where
S_{H\backslash G}
is the set of representatives of right
H
-cosets in
G
.
The matter fields after the gauge fixing are labeled by elements of
S_{H\backslash G}
.
We note that
|S_{H\backslash G}|=|G|/|H|
.

We emphasize that no information is lost by the gauge fixing because the state spaces before and after the gauge fixing are isomorphic to each other:

\mathcal{H}_{\text{g.f.}}\cong\mathcal{H}_{\text{gauged}}.

(III.24)

An isomorphism
\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}:\mathcal{H}_{\text{gauged}}\to\mathcal{H}_{\text{g.f.}}
is given by

\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}(\hat{\pi}_{\text{Gauss}}\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}\ket{\{g_{i};h_{ij}\}})=\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}\ket{\{r_{i};h_{i}^{-1}h_{ij}h_{j}\}},

(III.25)

where
r_{i}\in S_{H\backslash G}
is a representative of the right
H
-coset
Hg_{i}
, and
h_{i}\in H
is the unique element that satisfies
g_{i}=h_{i}r_{i}
.
Diagrammatically, the above isomorphism can be expressed as

\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}\Ket{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}}=\Ket{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}}=\Ket{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}},

(III.26)

where
g_{i}=h_{i}r_{i}
and
g_{j}=h_{j}r_{j}
with
h_{i},h_{j}\in H
and
r_{i},r_{j}\in S_{H\backslash G}
.
The inverse
\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}^{-1}:\mathcal{H}_{\text{g.f.}}\to\mathcal{H}_{\text{gauged}}
is given by

\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}^{-1}(\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}\ket{\{r_{i};h_{ij}\}})=\hat{\pi}_{\text{Gauss}}\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}\ket{\{r_{i};h_{ij}\}}.

(III.27)

By a direct computation, one can vertify that
\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}^{-1}\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}={\mathrm{id}}_{\mathcal{H}_{\text{gauged}}}
and
\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}^{-1}={\mathrm{id}}_{\mathcal{H}_{\text{g.f.}}}
.

The Hamiltonian after the gauge fixing is then given by

\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}=\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}\hat{H}_{\text{gauged}}\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}^{-1}=-\sum_{i\in P}\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}^{\text{g.f.}}-\sum_{[ij]\in E}\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}^{\text{g.f.}}.

(III.28)

The local terms
\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}^{\text{g.f.}}:=\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}^{\text{gauged}}\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}^{-1}
and
\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}^{\text{g.f.}}:=\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}^{\text{gauged}}\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}^{-1}
can be computed as

\displaystyle\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}^{\text{g.f.}}\Ket{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|K|}\sum_{k\in K}\Ket{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}},

(III.29)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}^{\text{g.f.}}\ket{r_{i}}_{i}\otimes\ket{h_{ij}}_{ij}\otimes\ket{r_{j}}_{j}

\displaystyle=\delta_{r_{i}^{-1}h_{ij}r_{j}\in K}\ket{r_{i}}_{i}\otimes\ket{h_{ij}}_{ij}\otimes\ket{r_{j}}_{j},

(III.30)

where
r_{i}^{\prime}\in S_{H\backslash G}
and
h\in H
in (
III.29
) are uniquely determined by

r_{i}k=h^{-1}r_{i}^{\prime}

(III.31)

for given
r_{i}\in S_{H\backslash G}
and
k\in K
.

By construction, the gauged model after the gauge fixing does not depend on the choice of representatives up to isomorphism.
Nevertheless, the Hamiltonian (
III.28
) after the gauge fixing does depend on
S_{H\backslash G}
because the isomorphism
\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}
does.
Accordingly, the ground states of (
III.28
) also depend on
S_{H\backslash G}
.
The relation between the models with different choices of representatives will be discussed in more detail in Appendix
A
.

III.1.4
Generalized cluster model

The gauged model defined above realizes an SPT phase when
K
is a complement of
H
in
G
, i.e., when
K
is a subgroup of
G
that satisfies (
II.17
).
The model for such a choice of
K
will be called a generalized cluster model, for the reason that will become clear in the next subsection.
Below, we will write down the Hamiltonian of this model.

When
K
is a complement of
H
, any element
g\in G
can be uniquely decomposed into the product

g=hk,\qquad h\in H,\quad k\in K.

(III.32)

This decomposition implies that any right
H
-coset in
G
can be written as

x[k]:=Hk.

(III.33)

Furthermore, the uniqueness of the decomposition (
III.32
) implies that
x[k]=x[k^{\prime}]
if and only if
k=k^{\prime}
.
The representative of
x[k]
is denoted by
\mathfrak{r}[k]\in S_{H\backslash G}
, which can also be uniquely decomposed as

\mathfrak{r}[k]=h[k]^{-1}k,\qquad h[k]\in H.

(III.34)

Using the above decomposition, one can solve (
III.31
) explicitly as follows:

\mathfrak{r}[k_{i}]k=h^{-1}\mathfrak{r}[k_{i}^{\prime}]\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \begin{cases}k_{i}^{\prime}=k_{i}k,\\
h=h[k_{i}^{\prime}]^{-1}h[k_{i}].\end{cases}

(III.35)

This enables us to slightly simplify the expressions (
III.29
) and (
III.30
).

We can now write down the Hamiltonian of the generalized cluster model as

\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}=-\sum_{i\in P}\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}^{\text{g.f.}}-\sum_{[ij]\in E}\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}^{\text{g.f.}},

(III.36)

where the local terms
\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}^{\text{g.f.}}
and
\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}^{\text{g.f.}}
are given by

\displaystyle\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}^{\text{g.f.}}\Ket{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|K|}\sum_{k_{i}^{\prime}\in K}\Ket{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}},

(III.37)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}^{\text{g.f.}}\ket{r_{i}}_{i}\otimes\ket{h_{ij}}_{ij}\otimes\ket{r_{j}}_{j}

\displaystyle=\delta_{h_{ij},h_{i}^{-1}h_{j}}\ket{r_{i}}_{i}\otimes\ket{h_{ij}}_{ij}\otimes\ket{r_{j}}_{j}.

(III.38)

Here, we employed the following shorthand notations:

r_{i}=\mathfrak{r}[k_{i}],\quad r_{i}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{r}[k_{i}^{\prime}],\quad h_{i}=h[k_{i}],\quad h_{i}^{\prime}=h[k_{i}^{\prime}],\qquad\forall k_{i},k_{i}^{\prime}\in K.

(III.39)

The Kronecker delta in (
III.38
) comes from the equality

\delta_{r_{i}^{-1}h_{ij}r_{j}\in K}=\delta_{h_{i}h_{ij}h_{j}^{-1}\in K}=\delta_{h_{ij},h_{i}^{-1}h_{j}},

(III.40)

where the last equality follows from
H\cap K=\{e\}
.

III.2
Generalized cluster states

In this subsection, we write down the ground state of the generalized cluster model using tensor networks.
The ground state turns out to be a natural generalization of the ordinary cluster state.
Thus, we will call this state a generalized cluster state.

Generalized cluster states.

To find a ground state of the generalized cluster model, we note that the Hamiltonian (
III.36
) is a sum of local commuting projectors:

[\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}^{\text{g.f.}},\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{j}^{\text{g.f.}}]=[\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}^{\text{g.f.}},\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{jk}^{\text{g.f.}}]=[\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}^{\text{g.f.}},\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{kl}^{\text{g.f.}}]=0,\qquad(\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}^{\text{g.f.}})^{2}=(\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}^{\text{g.f.}})^{2}=1.

(III.41)

Therefore, a ground state is obtained by applying the product of all local projectors to a generic state in
\mathcal{H}_{\text{g.f.}}
. i.e., we have

\ket{\text{GS}}=\prod_{i\in P}\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}^{\text{g.f.}}\prod_{[ij]\in E}\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}^{\text{g.f.}}\ket{\psi},\qquad\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}_{\text{g.f.}}.

(III.42)

We can show that the above state does not depend on
\ket{\psi}
up to normalization, which means that the ground state is unique.
To see this, we first notice that in the state
\prod_{[ij]}\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}^{\text{g.f.}}\ket{\psi}
, the configuration of the gauge fields is uniquely determined by the configuration of the matter fields.
Specifically, when the matter fields on the neighboring plaquettes
i,j\in P
are given by
r_{i},r_{j}\in S_{H\backslash G}
, the gauge field on the edge
[ij]\in E
must be given by
h_{i}^{-1}h_{j}\in H
, where
r_{i}
and
h_{i}
are related as in (
III.39
).
Thus, we find

\prod_{[ij]\in E}\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}^{\text{g.f.}}\ket{\psi}=\sum_{\{r_{i}\in S_{H\backslash G}\}}\psi(\{r_{i}\})\ket{\{r_{i};h_{i}^{-1}h_{j}\}},

(III.43)

where
\psi(\{r_{i}\})
is a complex number that depends only on the configuration of the matter fields.
By applying the projector
\prod_{i}\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}^{\text{g.f.}}
to the above state, we obtain

\displaystyle\ket{\text{GS}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|K|^{|P|}}\sum_{\{r_{i}\in S_{H\backslash G}\}}\psi(\{r_{i}\})\sum_{\{r_{i}^{\prime}\in S_{H\backslash G}\}}\ket{\{r_{i}^{\prime};(h_{i}^{\prime})^{-1}h_{j}^{\prime}\}}

(III.44)

\displaystyle\propto\sum_{\{r_{i}\in S_{H\backslash G}\}}\ket{\{r_{i};h_{i}^{-1}h_{j}\}},

where
|P|
is the number of plaquettes.
The last expression of the above equation does not depend on
\ket{\psi}
, and hence it is the unique ground state of (
III.36
).
This ground state will be referred to as the generalized cluster state and denoted by

\displaystyle\ket{\text{cluster}(G;H;K)}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{|K|^{|P|/2}}\sum_{\{r_{i}\in S_{H\backslash G}\}}\ket{\{r_{i};h_{i}^{-1}h_{j}\}}

(III.45)

\displaystyle\penalty\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|K|^{|P|/2}}\sum_{\{k_{i}\in K\}}\ket{\{\mathfrak{r}[k_{i}];h[k_{i}]^{-1}h[k_{j}]\}}.

Here,
1/|K|^{|P|/2}
is a normalization factor.
We note that the state
\ket{\text{cluster}(G;H;K)}
depends on the choice of representatives
S_{H\backslash G}
because
h[k_{i}]
does.
17
17

17

In particular,
\ket{\text{cluster}(G;H;K)}
becomes the trivial product state when
S_{H\backslash G}=K
.

We will discuss this dependency in more detail in Appendix
A
.

PEPS representation.

The generalized cluster state (
III.45
) can be represented by the following tensor network:

\ket{\text{cluster}(G;H;K)}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}

(III.46)

A tensor network state of the above form is known as a projected-entangled pair state (PEPS)
Cirac:2020obd
.
The black and white dots in (
III.46
) are located on the plaquettes and edges of the underlying square lattice.
Accordingly, the physical legs of the black and white tensors are labeled by elements on
S_{H\backslash G}
and
H
, respectively.
On the other hand, the virtual bonds are all labeled by elements of
H
.
In particular, the bond dimension is
|H|
.
The components of the local tensors in (
III.46
) are defined by

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|K|}}\prod_{i=1,2,3,4}\delta_{h_{i},h[k]},\quad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{h,h_{1}^{-1}h_{2}}.

(III.47)

One can easily check that the tensor network in (
III.46
) correctly reproduces the generalized cluster state (
III.45
).
Indeed, the first equality of (
III.47
) implies that when the physical leg on the plaquette
i\in P
is labeled by
r_{i}=\mathfrak{r}[k_{i}]
for
k_{i}\in K
, the virtual bonds around it are all labeled by
h_{i}=h[k_{i}]
.
In particular, the virtual bonds on the left side and the right side of the edge
[ij]\in E
are labeled by
h_{i}
and
h_{j}
, respectively.
Then, the second equality of (
III.47
) implies that the physical leg on the edge
[ij]
is labeled by
h_{i}^{-1}h_{j}
.
This shows that the tensor network state (
III.46
) is an equal-weight superposition of the states
\ket{\{r_{i};h_{i}^{-1}h_{j}\}}
for all configurations of the matter fields
\{r_{i}\in S_{H\backslash G}\mid i\in P\}
.
Therefore, the tensor network state (
III.46
) agrees with the generalized cluster state (
III.45
).

We note that the black tensor on each plaquette can be thought of as a generalization of the copy tensor, which copies a part of the matter field to the nearby virtual bonds.
Specifically, when the matter field on the plaquette
i\in P
is given by
r_{i}=h_{i}^{-1}k_{i}
, the black tensor copies
h_{i}
to the virtual bonds around
i
.
On the other hand, the white tensor on each edge is the multiplication tensor.

III.3
Example:
G
-cluster state

As a simple example, we consider the generalized cluster state whose input data are given by

G=G_{0}\times G_{0},\quad H=G_{0}^{\text{left}}:=\{(g,e)\mid g\in G_{0}\},\quad K=G_{0}^{\text{diag}}:=\{(g,g)\mid g\in G_{0}\},

(III.48)

where
G_{0}
is an arbitrary finite group.
We choose the set of representatives of right
H
-cosets in
G
to be

S_{H\backslash G}=G_{0}^{\text{right}}:=\{(e,g)\mid g\in G_{0}\}.

(III.49)

The generalized cluster state obtained from the above input data is denoted by

\ket{\text{$G_{0}$-cluster}}:=\ket{\text{cluster}(G_{0}\times G_{0};G_{0}^{\text{left}};G_{0}^{\text{diag}})}.

(III.50)

This state will be called the
G_{0}
-cluster state, which is a 2+1d analogue of the 1+1d
G_{0}
-cluster state studied in
Brell_2015
;
Fechisin:2023odt
.
The corresponding model will be referred to as the
G_{0}
-cluster model.
The above input data implies that, as in 1+1d
Fechisin:2023odt
, the
G_{0}
-cluster model is obtained by gauging the
G_{0}^{\text{left}}
symmetry in the
G_{0}\times G_{0}
-symmetric model that spontaneously breaks the symmetry down to
G_{0}^{\text{diag}}
.
Accordingly, the symmetry of this model is described by
2\text{Rep}(G_{0})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}
; see Section
IV.3
for more details on this symmetry.
As we will see below, the
G_{0}
-cluster model reduces to the ordinary cluster model when
G_{0}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

In what follows, we will write down the
G_{0}
-cluster model and its ground state explicitly based on the general construction in the previous subsection.
More details of this model are investigated in our companion paper
Ohyama:parameterized
.

The
G_{0}
-cluster model.

The state space of the
G_{0}
-cluster model on a square lattice is given by

\mathcal{H}_{\text{g.f.}}=\left(\bigotimes_{i\in P}\mathbb{C}^{|G_{0}|}\right)\otimes\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}\left(\bigotimes_{[ij]\in E}\mathbb{C}^{|G_{0}|}\right).

(III.51)

The matter field on plauquette
i\in P
and the gauge field on edge
[ij]\in E
are labeled by
g_{i}\in G_{0}
and
g_{ij}\in G_{0}
.
18
18

18

According to the general construction in Section
III.1
, the matter field and the gauge filed are labeled by elements of
G_{0}^{\text{right}}
and
G_{0}^{\text{left}}
, respectively. These elements are identified with elements of
G_{0}
in this subsection.

The corresponding states are denoted by
\ket{g_{i}}_{i}
and
\ket{g_{ij}}_{ij}
, respectively.
The projector
\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}
in (
III.51
) imposes the flatness condition on the gauge fields.
The Hamiltonian of the model is

\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}=-\sum_{i\in P}\hat{\mathsf{h}}^{\text{g.f.}}_{i}-\sum_{[ij]\in E}\hat{\mathsf{h}}^{\text{g.f.}}_{ij},

(III.52)

where
\hat{\mathsf{h}}^{\text{g.f.}}_{i}
and
\hat{\mathsf{h}}^{\text{g.f.}}_{ij}
are the local commuting projectors defined by

\displaystyle\hat{\mathsf{h}}^{\text{g.f.}}_{i}\Ket{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|G_{0}|}\sum_{g\in G_{0}}\Ket{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}},

(III.53)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathsf{h}}^{\text{g.f.}}_{ij}\ket{g_{i}}_{i}\otimes\ket{g_{ij}}_{ij}\otimes\ket{g_{j}}_{j}

\displaystyle=\delta_{g_{ij},g_{i}^{-1}g_{j}}\ket{g_{i}}_{i}\otimes\ket{g_{ij}}_{ij}\otimes\ket{g_{j}}_{j}.

(III.54)

Here, the loop on the right-hand side of (
III.53
) represents the loop operator acting on the gauge fields, cf. (
III.12
).
When
G_{0}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, the above local terms become
\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}^{\text{g.f.}}=\frac{1}{2}(1+X^{(i)}\prod_{[ij]\in\partial i}X^{(ij)})
and
\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}^{\text{g.f.}}=\frac{1}{2}(1+Z^{(i)}Z^{(ij)}Z^{(j)})
, where
X
and
Z
are the Pauli-X and Pauli-Z operators.
Thus, in this case, the
G_{0}
-cluster model reduces to the ordinary cluster model
Briegel:0004051
;
PhysRevLett.86.5188
;
Raussendorf:0301052
, which realizes an SPT phase with
2\text{Rep}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
symmetry
Yoshida:2015cia
.
In general, the
G_{0}
-cluster model realizes an SPT phase with
2\text{Rep}(G_{0})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}
symmetry.
The symmetry structure of this model will be discussed in more detail in Section
IV.3
.

The
G_{0}
-cluster state.

The Hamiltonian (
III.52
) is solvable because it is a sum of local commuting projectors.
The ground state of this model can be written explicitly as (cf. (
III.45
))

\ket{\text{$G_{0}$-cluster}}=\frac{1}{|G_{0}|^{|P|/2}}\sum_{\{g_{i}\in G_{0}\}}\ket{\{g_{i};g_{i}^{-1}g_{j}\}}.

(III.55)

This state can be represented by a PEPS of the form

\ket{\text{$G_{0}$-cluster}}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},

(III.56)

where both the physical legs and the virtual legs take values in
G_{0}
.
The black dot and the white dot in (
III.56
) are the copy tensor and the multiplication tensor, respectively, i.e.,

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|G_{0}|}}\prod_{i=1,2,3,4}\delta_{g,g_{i}},\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{g,g_{1}^{-1}g_{2}}.

(III.57)

When
G_{0}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, equation (
III.56
) agrees with the PEPS representation of the ordinary cluster state.

IV
Non-invertible symmetries of 2+1d generalized cluster states

By construction, the generalized cluster model defined in Section
III
has a group-theoretical fusion 2-category symmetry
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
.
In this section, we will write down the symmetry operators explicitly using tensor networks.
In addition, we study the symmetry action on the generalized cluster states (
III.45
).
We will see that the non-invertible symmetry operators act fractionally on the virtual legs of the tensor network representation (
III.46
).

IV.1
Tensor network representations of symmetry operators

1-form symmetry.

The 1-form symmetry operators are labeled by 1-endomorphisms of the unit object
I\in\mathcal{C}(G;H)
.
These 1-morphisms form a symmetric fusion category

\text{End}_{\mathcal{C}(G;H)}(I)\cong\text{Rep}(H).

(IV.1)

The corresponding symmetry operators are the Wilson lines for the
H
gauge fields.
The action of the Wilson line
W_{\rho}
labeled by a representation
\rho\in\text{Rep}(H)
is defined by

W_{\rho}(\gamma)\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}\ket{\{r_{i};h_{ij}\}}=\mathrm{tr}_{V_{\rho}}(\rho(\text{hol}_{\gamma}(\{h_{ij}\})))\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}\ket{\{r_{i};h_{ij}\}},

(IV.2)

where
V_{\rho}
is the representation space of
\rho
,
\gamma
is an oriented closed loop on the dual lattice as shown in Figure
4
, and
\text{hol}_{\gamma}(\{h_{ij}\})
is the holonomy of the
H
gauge field along
\gamma
.

Figure 4:
A loop
\gamma
on the dual lattice.

More specifically, the holonomy
\text{hol}_{\gamma}(\{h_{ij}\})
is defined by

\text{hol}_{\gamma}(\{h_{ij}\})=\prod_{[ij]\in E_{\gamma}}h_{ij}^{s_{ij}},

(IV.3)

where
\prod_{[ij]\in E_{\gamma}}
is the path-ordered product over all edges that intersect
\gamma
,
19
19

19

Precisely, we need to specify the base point of
\gamma
so that the path-ordered product is well-defined. Nevertheless, the Wilson line
W_{\rho}
is independent of the base point because we take the trace over
V_{\rho}
.
and
s_{ij}
is the sign defined by

s_{ij}=+1\quad\text{for }\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},\qquad s_{ij}=-1\quad\text{for }\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(IV.4)

We note that the Wilson line
W_{\rho}
is topological in spatial directions due to the flatness condition on the gauge fields.
The generalized cluster state (
III.45
) preserves this 1-form symmetry because

W_{\rho}(\gamma)\ket{\text{cluster}(G;H;K)}=\dim(\rho)\ket{\text{cluster}(G;H;K)}

(IV.5)

for any loop
\gamma
and any representation
\rho\in\text{Rep}(H)
.

The Wilson line
W_{\rho}(\gamma)
can be written as a matrix product operator (MPO) along
\gamma
, which is expressed diagrammatically as

\scalebox{0.9}[0.9]{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}}.

(IV.6)

Here, the gray dotted lines represent the underlying square lattice, and the vertical black segments represent the physical legs.
The bond Hilbert space of the MPO is the representation space
V_{\rho}
, and the local MPO tensors represented by the blue dots are defined by

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(IV.7)

We note that the MPO tensors depend on the relative orientation of
\gamma
and the edge of the square lattice.
The intersections without the blue dots in (
IV.6
) and (
IV.7
) represent the trivial braiding.

0-form symmetry.

The 0-form symmetry operators are labeled by objects of
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
.
As we reviewed in Section
II.2
, all simple objects of
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
are connected to the simple objects of the following form
Decoppet:2023bay
:

V_{H}^{g}=\text{Vec}_{H}\boxtimes V^{g}\boxtimes\text{Vec}_{H},\quad g\in G.

(IV.8)

Here, we recall that
V^{g}
is a simple object of
2\text{Vec}_{G}
, and
\text{Vec}_{H}=\bigoplus_{h\in H}V^{h}
is an algebra object of
2\text{Vec}_{G}
.
The symmetry operator corresponding to (
IV.8
) is given by
Inamura:2025cum

\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]:=\mathsf{D}_{H}U_{g}\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{H},

(IV.9)

where
\mathsf{D}_{H}:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}_{\text{g.f.}}
is the gauging operator,
\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{H}:\mathcal{H}_{\text{g.f.}}\to\mathcal{H}
is the ungauging operator, and
U_{g}:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}
is a symmetry operator of the original
G
-symmetric model.
See below for more details of the gauging and ungauging operators.
We note that
V_{H}^{g}
is isomorphic to
V_{H}^{g^{\prime}}
if and only if
g
and
g^{\prime}
are in the same double
H
-coset in
G
.
Correspondingly, the symmetry operators (
IV.9
) satisfy

\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]=\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g^{\prime}}]\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ HgH=Hg^{\prime}H,

(IV.10)

which imediately follows from
\mathsf{D}_{H}U_{h}=\mathsf{D}_{H}
and
U_{h}\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{H}=\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{H}
for all
h\in H
.

In what follows, we will write down the 0-form symmetry operators (
IV.9
) using tensor networks.
Furthermore, we will also obtain tensor network representations of all the other symmetry operators by condensing line operators on the symmetry operators (
IV.9
).

IV.1.1
Gauging operator

Let us first write down the gauging operator
\mathsf{D}_{H}
, which serves as a building block of the symmetry operators (
IV.9
).
The gauging operator
\mathsf{D}_{H}:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}_{\text{g.f.}}
is an operator that implements the gauging of the subgroup symmetry
H\subset G
in the sense that

\mathsf{D}_{H}\hat{H}=\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}\mathsf{D}_{H},

(IV.11)

where
\hat{H}
is the Hamiltonian of the original
G
-symmetric model and
\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}
is the Hamiltonian of the gauged model after the gauge fixing.
20
20

20

The gauging operator
\mathsf{D}_{H}
is defined so that (
IV.11
) holds for any
G
-symmetric Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
and the corresponding gauged Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}
.

Such a gauging operator is given by the composition

\mathsf{D}_{H}=\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}\mathsf{D}_{H}^{\prime},

(IV.12)

where
\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}:\mathcal{H}_{\text{gauged}}\to\mathcal{H}_{\text{g.f.}}
is the gauge fixing operator (
III.25
) and
\mathsf{D}_{H}^{\prime}:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}_{\text{gauged}}
is the gauging operator before the gauge fixing.
The explicit form of
\mathsf{D}_{H}^{\prime}
is given by
Haegeman:2014maa

\mathsf{D}_{H}^{\prime}\ket{\{g_{i}\}}=\hat{\pi}_{\text{Gauss}}\ket{\{g_{i};e\}},

(IV.13)

where
\ket{\{g_{i};e\}}
is a state in which the gauge fields are trivial on all edges.
By construction, the above gauging operator satisfies

\mathsf{D}_{H}^{\prime}\hat{H}=\hat{H}_{\text{gauged}}\mathsf{D}_{H}^{\prime},

(IV.14)

which in turn imples (
IV.11
).
Combining (
III.25
), (
IV.12
), and (
IV.13
), we find
Tantivasadakarn:2022hgp
;
Inamura:2025cum
;
Delcamp:2023kew

\mathsf{D}_{H}\ket{\{g_{i}\}}=\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}\hat{\pi}_{\text{Gauss}}\ket{\{g_{i};e\}}=\ket{\{r_{i};h_{i}^{-1}h_{j}\}},

(IV.15)

where
r_{i}\in S_{H\backslash G}
and
h_{i}\in H
are uniquely determined by
g_{i}=h_{i}r_{i}
.

We note that the gauging operator
\mathsf{D}_{H}
is neither injective nor surjective.
It is not injective because it satisfies

\mathsf{D}_{H}U_{h}=\mathsf{D}_{H},\quad\forall h\in H,

(IV.16)

which implies that any state charged non-trivially under the
H
symmetry is in the kernel of
\mathsf{D}_{H}
.
Similarly, it is not surjective because it satisfies

W_{\rho}(\gamma)\mathsf{D}_{H}=\dim(\rho)\mathsf{D}_{H},\quad\forall\rho\in\text{Rep}(H),\penalty\ \forall\gamma:\text{loop},

(IV.17)

which implies that any state charged non-trivially under the
\text{Rep}(H)
symmetry are not in the image of
\mathsf{D}_{H}
.
As we will see later,
\mathsf{D}_{H}
is invertible on the symmetric subspace of
\mathcal{H}
, even though it is non-invertible on the entire
\mathcal{H}
.

By construction, the gauging operator
\mathsf{D}_{H}
maps the ground states of the
G
-symmetric model to the ground states of the gauged model.
In particular, the ground states of the
G/K
-SSB Hamiltonian (
III.3
) are all mapped to the generalized cluster state (
III.45
), i.e.,

\mathsf{D}_{H}\ket{\text{GS};\mu}=\ket{\text{cluster}(G;H;K)},\quad\mu=1,2,\cdots,|G/K|,

(IV.18)

where
\ket{\text{GS};\mu}
is defined by (
III.7
).

PEPO representation.

The gauging operator
\mathsf{D}_{H}
defined by (
IV.15
) can be represented by the following tensor network:

\mathsf{D}_{H}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(IV.19)

A tensor network operator of this form is called a projected-entangled pair operator (PEPO)
Cirac:2020obd
.
As in the PEPS representation (
III.46
) of the generalized cluster state, the black and white dots in (
IV.19
) are located on the plaquettes and edges of the underlying square lattice.
The bottom and top legs of each black dot are the physical legs associated with the matter fields of the original model and the gauged model.
Accordingly, they are labeled by elements of
G
and
S_{H\backslash G}
, respectively.
Similarly, the top leg of each white dot is the physical leg associated with the gauge fields of the gauged model, and hence it is labeled by an element of
H
.
Each virtual bond, written in magenta, is also labeled by an element of
H
, meaning that the bond dimension is
|H|
.
The local tensors in (
IV.19
) are defined by

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{r,r^{\prime}}\prod_{i=1,2,3,4}\delta_{h,h_{i}},\quad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{h,h_{1}^{-1}h_{2}}.

(IV.20)
