It is straightforward to check that the action of the PEPO (
IV.19
) on a state
\ket{\{g_{i}\}}
agrees with (
IV.15
).

IV.1.2
Ungauging operator

The ungauging operator
\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{H}:\mathcal{H}_{\text{g.f.}}\to\mathcal{H}
is an operator that implements the ungauging of the symmetry
H
in the sense that

\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{H}\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}=\hat{H}\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{H}.

(IV.21)

The explicit form of
\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{H}
is given by

\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{H}\ket{\{r_{i};h_{ij}\}}=\sum_{\{h_{i}\in H\}}\left(\prod_{[ij]\in E}\delta_{h_{ij},h_{i}^{-1}h_{j}}\right)\ket{\{h_{i}r_{i}\}}.

(IV.22)

We note that the gauging and ungauging operators are related by

\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{H}=\mathsf{D}_{H}^{\dagger},

(IV.23)

where the Hermitian conjugate is defined with respect to the following Hermitian inner product:

\braket{\{g_{i}\}|\{g_{i}^{\prime}\}}=\prod_{i\in P}\delta_{g_{i},g_{i}^{\prime}},\quad\braket{\{r_{i};h_{ij}\}|\{r_{i}^{\prime};h_{ij}^{\prime}\}}=\prod_{i\in P}\delta_{r_{i},r_{i}^{\prime}}\prod_{[ij]\in E}\delta_{h_{ij},h_{ij}^{\prime}}.

(IV.24)

Given that the Hamiltonians are Hermitian, equation (
IV.21
) automatically follows from (
IV.11
).
21
21

21

In
Inamura:2025cum
, the ungauging operator (
IV.22
) was derived without assuming the Hermiticity of the Hamiltonians.

A direct computation shows that the gauging and ungauging operators satisfy

\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{H}\mathsf{D}_{H}=|H|\hat{\pi}_{\text{sym}},\quad\mathsf{D}_{H}\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{H}=|H|\hat{\pi}_{\text{hol}},

(IV.25)

where
\hat{\pi}_{\text{sym}}:=\frac{1}{|H|}\sum_{h\in H}U_{h}
is the projection to the symmetric subspace of the original state space
\mathcal{H}
, while
\hat{\pi}_{\text{hol}}
is the projection to the subspace of
\mathcal{H}_{\text{g.f.}}
in which the gauge fields have the trivial holonomy along any loop (i.e., the gauge fields have the trivial charge under the
\text{Rep}(H)
symmetry).
The first equality of (
IV.25
) holds because

\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{H}\mathsf{D}_{H}\ket{\{g_{i}\}}\overset{\eqref{eq: D gf}}{=}\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{H}\ket{\{r_{i};h_{i}^{-1}h_{j}\}}\overset{\eqref{eq: D gf bar}}{=}\sum_{\{h_{i}^{\prime}\in H\}}\left(\prod_{[ij]\in E}\delta_{h_{i}^{-1}h_{j},(h_{i}^{\prime})^{-1}h_{j}^{\prime}}\right)\ket{\{h_{i}^{\prime}r_{i}\}}=\sum_{h\in H}\ket{\{hh_{i}r_{i}\}}.

In the last equality, we used the fact that the summand on the left-hand side is non-zero only when there exists
h\in H
such that
h_{i}^{\prime}=hh_{i}
for all
i\in P
.
Similarly, the second equality of (
IV.25
) follows from

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{H}\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{H}\ket{\{r_{i};h_{ij}\}}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq: D gf bar}}{=}\mathsf{D}_{H}\sum_{\{h_{i}\in H\}}\left(\prod_{[ij]\in E}\delta_{h_{ij},h_{i}^{-1}h_{j}}\right)\ket{\{h_{i}r_{i}\}}\overset{\eqref{eq: D gf}}{=}\sum_{\{h_{i}\in H\}}\left(\prod_{[ij]\in E}\delta_{h_{ij},h_{i}^{-1}h_{j}}\right)\ket{\{r_{i};h_{i}^{-1}h_{j}\}}

(IV.26)

\displaystyle\penalty\penalty\displaystyle=\sum_{\{h_{i}\in H\}}\left(\prod_{[ij]\in E}\delta_{h_{ij},h_{i}^{-1}h_{j}}\right)\ket{\{r_{i};h_{ij}\}}=|H|\delta_{\text{hol}}(\{h_{ij}\})\ket{\{r_{i};h_{ij}\}},

where
\delta_{\text{hol}}(\{h_{ij}\})=1
if the holonomy of the gauge fields
\{h_{ij}\mid[ij]\in E\}
is trivial along any loop and
\delta_{\text{hol}}(\{h_{ij}\})=0
otherwise.
Equation (
IV.25
) implies that
\mathsf{D}_{H}
and
\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{H}
are mutually inverse bijections (up to normalization) on the symmetric subspaces of
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\text{g.f.}}
.

As with the gauging operator, the ungauging operator
\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{H}
maps the ground states of the gauged model to the ground states of the original
G
-symmetric model.
In particular, the generalized cluster state (
III.45
) is mapped to the symmetric linear combination of the symmetry-breaking ground states (
III.7
), i.e.,

\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{H}\ket{\text{cluster}(G;H;K)}=\sum_{\mu=1,2,\cdots,|G/K|}\ket{\text{GS};\mu}.

(IV.27)

This result immediately follows from (
IV.18
) and (
IV.25
).

PEPO representation.

The tensor network representation of
\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{H}
is obtained by turning the tensor network for
\mathsf{D}_{H}
upside down as follows:

\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{H}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(IV.28)

We note that the orientations of the virtual bonds are opposite to those of (
IV.19
).
22
22

22

As always, the physical legs are still oriented upwards.

Furthermore, as opposed to (
IV.19
), the bottom legs of the black tensors are now labeled by elements of
S_{H\backslash G}
, while the top legs are labeled by elements of
G
.
The physical legs of the white tensors, as well as the virtual bonds, are still labeled by elements of
H
.
The local tensors in (
IV.28
) are defined by

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{r,r^{\prime}}\prod_{i=1,2,3,4}\delta_{h,h_{i}},\quad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{h,h_{1}^{-1}h_{2}}.

(IV.29)

It is straightforward to check that the PEPO (
IV.28
) acting on
\ket{\{r_{i};h_{ij}\}}
agrees with (
IV.22
).

IV.1.3
Symmetry operators

Using the PEPO representations (
IV.19
) and (
IV.28
) of the gauging and ungauging operators, we can write down the PEPO representation of the symmetry operator (
IV.9
) as follows:

\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(IV.30)

The local tensors on the top and bottom layers are defined by (
IV.20
) and (
IV.29
), respectively.
The green dots on the middle layer represent the on-site symmetry operator
U_{g}
, which is given by the left multiplication of
g
.
The composite tensor on each plaquette of the square lattice can be computed as

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{h^{\prime},ghr\mathfrak{r}[ghr]^{-1}}\delta_{r^{\prime},\mathfrak{r}[ghr]},

(IV.31)

where
\mathfrak{r}[ghr]\in S_{H\backslash G}
is the representative of the right
H
-coset
Hghr
, i.e.,

ghr\in H\mathfrak{r}[ghr].

(IV.32)

We note that
ghr\mathfrak{r}[ghr]^{-1}
is an element of
H
by definition.
The total bond dimension of the PEPO (
IV.30
) is
|H|^{2}
because both the top and bottom layers have bond dimension
|H|
.
As we will see below, this bond dimension can be reduced to
|H|
, which turns out to be crucial for later computations.

Before proceeding, we mention that the symmetry operator
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]
correctly obeys the same fusion rule as
V_{H}^{g}
.
One can verify this by computing the fusion rule of
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]
explicitly as

\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g^{\prime}}]=\mathsf{D}_{H}U_{g}\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{H}\mathsf{D}_{H}U_{g^{\prime}}\mathsf{D}_{H}=\sum_{h\in H}\mathsf{D}_{H}U_{ghg^{\prime}}\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{H}=\sum_{g^{\prime\prime}\in gHg^{\prime}}\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g^{\prime\prime}}],

(IV.33)

where the second equality follows from (
IV.25
).
The above fusion rule agrees with (
II.13
), which justifies the claim that
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]
is the symmetry operator corresponding to
V_{H}^{g}\in\mathcal{C}(G;H)
.

Reduction of bond dimension.

One can reduce the bond dimension of the PEPO (
IV.30
) to
|H|
when the symmetry
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
is non-anomalous.
23
23

23

This may be viewed as an analogue of the “onsiteability” of non-anomalous fusion category symmetries in 1+1d
Meng:2024nxx
;
Evans:2025msy
. In
Meng:2024nxx
, symmetry operators are said to be on-site if they are represented by MPOs whose bond dimensions are equal to the quantum dimensions of the corresponding objects in the fusion category. See also
Seifnashri:2025vhf
;
Feng:2025yge
for the relation between onsiteability and anomalies for invertible symmetries.

To reduce the bond dimension, we choose the representatives of right
H
-cosets in
G
so that they form a subgroup of
G
.
In this case, the set
S_{H\backslash G}
becomes a complement of
H
in
G
because it satisfies

HS_{H\backslash G}=G,\quad H\cap S_{H\backslash G}=\{e\}.

(IV.34)

Such a choice of representatives is possible because
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
is supposed to be non-anomalous.
For this choice of
S_{H\backslash G}
, the representative
\mathfrak{r}[ghk]
satisfies

\mathfrak{r}[ghr]=\mathfrak{r}[gh]r.

(IV.35)

Therefore, the right-hand side of (
IV.31
) reduces to

\delta_{h^{\prime},ghr\mathfrak{r}[ghr]^{-1}}\delta_{r^{\prime},\mathfrak{r}[ghr]}=\delta_{h^{\prime},gh\mathfrak{r}[gh]^{-1}}\delta_{r^{\prime},\mathfrak{r}[gh]r}.

(IV.36)

Namely, the composite tensor (
IV.31
) is non-zero only when
h^{\prime}=gh\mathfrak{r}[gh]^{-1}
and
r^{\prime}=\mathfrak{r}[gh]r
.
We note that
h^{\prime}=gh\mathfrak{r}[gh]^{-1}
does not depend on
r
.
This property allows us to decompose the composite tensor (
IV.31
) as follows:

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(IV.37)

The local tensors on the right-hand side are given by

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{r^{\prime},\mathfrak{r}[gh]r},\quad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{h^{\prime},gh\mathfrak{r}[gh]^{-1}}.

(IV.38)

Using the decomposition (
IV.37
), we can deform the PEPO representation (
IV.30
) as follows:

\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(IV.39)

The tensors represented by the filled green dots are defined by the first equality of (
IV.38
), while the tensors represented by the unfilled dots are defined by

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}:=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{h,h_{1}^{-1}h_{2}}\delta_{h^{\prime},\mathfrak{r}[gh_{1}]h\mathfrak{r}[gh_{2}]^{-1}}.

(IV.40)

We emphasize that the bond dimension of the PEPO representation (
IV.39
) is
|H|
rather than
|H|^{2}
.

Line operators on
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]
.

Line operators on the surface operator
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]
are labeled by 1-endomorphisms of
V_{H}^{g}
, which form a fusion 1-category
Decoppet:2023bay

\text{End}_{\mathcal{C}(G;H)}(V_{H}^{g})\cong\text{Vec}_{H\cap g^{-1}Hg}.

(IV.41)

These line operators will be denoted by
\{\mathsf{L}_{h}\mid h\in H\cap g^{-1}Hg\}
.
The surface operator in the presence of a line operator
\mathsf{L}_{h}
can be represented by the following tensor network:

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(IV.42)

The bond dimension of the line operator
\mathsf{L}_{h}
is one, which means that
\mathsf{L}_{h}
is the tensor product of on-site operators along the line.
Nevertheless, as a visual aid, we draw a dashed line as in (
IV.42
) to represent the line operator
\mathsf{L}_{h}
.
Each on-site operator along the line is given by the left multiplication of
h
or
h^{-1}
, depending on the orientation:

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}:=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\lower-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}:=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\lower-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(IV.43)

The line operator
\mathsf{L}_{h}
defined above is topological in spatial directions because it satisfies the following pulling-through equations:

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(IV.44)

To show the pulling-through equations, we first notice that these equations are equivalent to

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(IV.45)

Due to (
IV.38
) and (
IV.40
), these equations can also be written more explicitly as

\delta_{r^{\prime},\mathfrak{r}[ghh_{0}]r}=\delta_{r^{\prime},\mathfrak{r}[gh_{0}]r},\qquad\delta_{h_{3},h_{1}^{-1}h_{2}}\delta_{h_{3}^{\prime},\mathfrak{r}[ghh_{1}]h_{3}\mathfrak{r}[ghh_{2}]^{-1}}=\delta_{h_{3},h_{1}^{-1}h_{2}}\delta_{h_{3}^{\prime},\mathfrak{r}[gh_{1}]h_{3}\mathfrak{r}[gh_{2}]^{-1}}.

(IV.46)

The first equality holds if and only if
\mathfrak{r}[ghh_{0}]=\mathfrak{r}[gh_{0}]
for all
h_{0}\in H
, that is, if and only if
ghh_{0}
and
gh_{0}
are in the same right
H
-coset in
G
.
This condition is equivalent to

Hgh=Hg\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ h\in g^{-1}Hg.

(IV.47)

The second equality of (
IV.46
) also holds if and only if
h\in g^{-1}Hg
.
Therefore, we find that the line operator
\mathsf{L}_{h}
labeled by
h\in H\cap g^{-1}Hg
satisfies the pulling-through equations (
IV.44
).

Other symmetry operators.

Since all simple objects of
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
are connected to
V_{H}^{g}
for some
g\in G
, all 0-form symmetry operators can be obtained by condensing line operators on the corresponding surface operator
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]
.
The line operators condensed on
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]
form a (
\Delta
-separable symmetric) Frobenius algebra in
\text{End}_{\mathcal{C}(G;H)}(V_{H}^{g})\cong\text{Vec}_{H\cap g^{-1}Hg}
.
A Frobesnius algebra object of
\text{Vec}_{H\cap g^{-1}Hg}
is labeled by a pair
(H^{\prime},\kappa)
, where
H^{\prime}
is a subgroup of
H\cap g^{-1}Hg
and
\kappa\in Z^{2}(H^{\prime},\mathrm{U}(1))
is a 2-cocycle on
H^{\prime}

EGNO2015
.
The algebra labeled by
(H^{\prime},\kappa)
can be chosen to be the twisted group algebra
\mathbb{C}[H^{\prime}]^{\kappa}
.
24
24

24

More generally, an algebra object labeled by
(H^{\prime},\kappa)
can be any algebra that is Morita equivalent to
\mathbb{C}[H^{\prime}]^{\kappa}
in
\text{Vec}_{H\cap g^{-1}Hg}
. The surface operator obtained by the condensation depends only on the Morita class of the algebra.

The corresponding line operator is
\bigoplus_{h\in H^{\prime}}\mathsf{L}_{h}
.
The multiplication
m
and the comultiplication
\Delta
for the Frobenius algebra structure on
\mathbb{C}[H^{\prime}]^{\kappa}
are given by

m(v_{h_{1}}\otimes v_{h_{2}})=\kappa(h_{1},h_{2})v_{h_{1}h_{2}},\quad\Delta(v_{h_{1}})=\frac{1}{|H^{\prime}|}\sum_{h_{2}\in H^{\prime}}\kappa(h_{1}h_{2}^{-1},h_{2})^{-1}v_{h_{1}h_{2}^{-1}}\otimes v_{h_{2}},

(IV.48)

where
\{v_{h}\mid h\in H^{\prime}\}
is a basis of
\mathbb{C}[H^{\prime}]^{\kappa}
.
One can check that
\mathbb{C}[H^{\prime}]^{\kappa}
equipped with the above multiplication and comultiplication is indeed a
\Delta
-separable symmetric Frobenius algebra, whose unit
u:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}[H^{\prime}]^{\kappa}
and counit
\epsilon:\mathbb{C}[H^{\prime}]^{\kappa}\to\mathbb{C}
are given by

u(1)=v_{e},\qquad\epsilon(v_{h})=|H^{\prime}|\delta_{h,e},

(IV.49)

where
e
denotes the unit element of
H
.
Here, without loss of generality, we supposed that
\kappa
is normalized so that
\kappa(e,h)=\kappa(h,e)=1
for any
h\in H^{\prime}
.

In the context of tensor networks, the condensation of an algebra object
\mathbb{C}[H^{\prime}]^{\kappa}
on
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]
is defined by
25
25

25

One may define the condensation by a finer mesh of an algebra object. However, one can always reduce the mesh using the Frobenius relation and the
\Delta
-separability of
\mathbb{C}[H^{\prime}]^{\kappa}
.

\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g};\mathbb{C}[H^{\prime}]^{\kappa}]=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(IV.50)

The virtual Hilbert space associated with the blue bond is
\mathbb{C}[H^{\prime}]^{\kappa}
, and the local tensors represented by the filled and unfilled blue dots are given by

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(IV.51)

We note that the PEPO representation (
IV.50
) has the bond dimension
|H||H^{\prime}|
.
As we will show in Appendix
B.2
, one can recover the identity surface by condensing the algebra object
\mathbb{C}[H]
on a condensation surface
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{e}]
, i.e., we have
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{e};\mathbb{C}[H]]={\mathrm{id}}
.

One can coarsen the blue mesh in (
IV.50
) by using the pulling-through equations (
IV.44
) and the
\Delta
-separable Frobenius algebra structure on
\mathbb{C}[H^{\prime}]^{\kappa}
.
In particular, for periodic boundary conditions, we can reduce the number of vertical lines and horizontal lines to one:

\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g};\mathbb{C}[H^{\prime}]^{\kappa}]=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(IV.52)

This expression makes it manifest that
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g};\mathbb{C}[H^{\prime}]^{\kappa}]
commutes with the Hamiltonian (
III.36
), i.e.,

[\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g};\mathbb{C}[H^{\prime}]^{\kappa}],\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}]=0.

(IV.53)

This equation automatically follows from the commutativity of the local tensors of
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]
and the local terms of
\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}
, because one can freely move the line operators in (
IV.52
) so that they are far away from the local operators involved in the commutation relation.
Therefore, the surface operator (
IV.52
) is indeed a symmetry operator of the generalized cluster model.

IV.2
Fractionalized symmetries

In this subsection, we study the symmetry action on the generalized cluster state (
III.45
).
In particular, we will see that the symmetry operator
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]
acting on the physical degrees of freedom is “fractionalized” into operators acting on the virtual degrees of freedom of the PEPS (
III.46
).
In what follows, we suppose that
S_{H\backslash G}
is a complement of
H
in
G
so that the symmetry operator
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]
is represented by the PEPO (
IV.39
) with bond dimension
|H|
.

The generalized cluster state (
III.45
) must be invariant (up to scalar) under the symmetry action because it is the unique ground state of the symmetric Hamiltonian (
III.36
).
Indeed, due to (
IV.18
) and (
IV.27
), one can compute the global action of
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]
on the generalized cluster state as

\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]\ket{\text{cluster}(G;H;K)}=|H|\ket{\text{cluster}(G;H;K)}.

(IV.54)

On the other hand, at the level of the local tensors,
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]
acts on the generalized cluster PEPS (
III.46
) as

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\,,\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\,.

(IV.55)

See Appendix
C.1
for a derivation.
All the virtual bonds in the above equation take values in
H
, and the tensors
\phi_{K}[V_{H}^{g}]
and
\overline{\phi_{K}}[V_{H}^{g}]
are defined by

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}:=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\,,\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}:=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\,,

(IV.56)

where the local tensors on the right-hand sides are given by

\displaystyle=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{h,h_{1}^{-1}h_{2}},\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\prod_{i,j=1,2,3,4}\delta_{h_{i},h_{j}},

(IV.57)

\displaystyle=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{h_{1},h}\delta_{h_{2},gh\mathfrak{r}[gh]^{-1}},\quad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{h^{\prime},U_{g;K}(h)}.

(IV.58)

Here,
U_{g;K}(h)
is the unique element of
H
that satisfies

U_{g;K}(h)gh^{-1}\in K.

(IV.59)

We note that the white and magenta dots in (
IV.57
) are the multiplication and copy tensors, while the three-leg tensors in (
IV.58
) are the same as those in (
IV.38
).

Action tensors.

For later use, we decompose
\phi_{K}[V_{H}^{g}]
and
\overline{\phi_{K}}[V_{H}^{g}]
into the components labeled by
h\in H
as follows:

\phi_{K}[V_{H}^{g}]_{h}:=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},\qquad\overline{\phi_{K}}[V_{H}^{g}]_{h}:=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(IV.60)

Here,
\pi_{h}
is the projection to the virtual state labeled by
h\in H
, and
U_{g;K}:H\to H
is the map defined by (
IV.59
).
26
26

26

We note that
U_{g;K}:H\to H
is bijective and hence invertible. A proof goes as follows. By defintion, for any
h_{1},h_{2}\in H
, there exist
k_{1},k_{2}\in K
such that
U_{g;K}(h_{1})gh_{1}^{-1}=k_{1}
and
U_{g;K}(h_{2})gh_{2}^{-1}=k_{2}
. If
U_{g;K}(h_{1})=U_{g;K}(h_{2})
, we have
k_{1}h_{1}=k_{2}h_{2}
, which implies
h_{1}=h_{2}
because
K
is a complement of
H
. Therefore,
U_{g;K}
is injective. Furthermore, since the source and target of
U_{g;K}
are the same, the injectivity implies the surjectivity. Thus,
U_{g;K}
is bijective. More explicitly, the inverse of
U_{g;K}
is given by
U_{g;K}^{-1}(h)=hg^{-1}U_{g;K}(h)^{-1}hg
.

The tensors
\phi_{K}[V_{H}^{g}]_{h}
and
\overline{\phi_{K}}[V_{H}^{g}]_{h}
are called action tensors.
By using the action tensors, the local action (
IV.55
) of the symmetry operator
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]
on the generalized cluster state can be written as

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\penalty\ =\sum_{h\in H}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\penalty\ =\sum_{h\in H}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(IV.61)

The action tensors defined by (
IV.60
) satisfy the following orthogonality and completeness relations:

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{h_{1},h_{2}}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},\qquad\sum_{h\in H}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(IV.62)

A derivation of the above equations will be given in Appendix
C.2
.
27
27

27

As shown in Appendix
C.2
, the completeness relation automatically follows from the orthogonality relation.

In Appendix
D
, we will show that the action tensors satisfying the orthogonality and completeness relations are essentially unique.
Due to the orthogonality relation, one can freely reconnect the fractionalized symmetry operators as

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(IV.63)

Using the local symmetry action (
IV.55
) and the reconnection (
IV.63
), one can recover the global symmetry action (
IV.54
).

IV.3
Example: symmetry of the
G
-cluster state

As a simple example, let us consider the symmetry of the
G_{0}
-cluster model and its action on the
G_{0}
-cluster state (
III.55
), where
G_{0}
is any finite group.
To this end, we first recall that the
G_{0}
-cluster model is obtained by gauging the
G_{0}^{\text{left}}
symmetry in a model with
G_{0}\times G_{0}
symmetry as mentioned in Section
III.3
.
Thus, by construction, this model has a group-theoretical fusion 2-category symmetry

\mathcal{C}(G_{0}\times G_{0};G_{0}^{\text{left}})\cong 2\text{Rep}(G_{0})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}.

(IV.64)

In what follows, we will write down the tensor network representations of the (non-invertible) 0-form symmetry operators and compute their local actions on the
G_{0}
-cluster state.

Symmetry operators.

As discussed in Section
IV.1
, all the 0-form symmetry operators up to condensation are of the form

\mathsf{D}[V_{G_{0}^{\text{left}}}^{(g_{\ell},g_{r})}]\coloneq\mathsf{D}_{G_{0}^{\text{left}}}U_{(g_{\ell},g_{r})}\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{G_{0}^{\text{left}}},\qquad\forall g_{\ell},g_{r}\in G_{0},

(IV.65)

where
\mathsf{D}_{G_{0}^{\text{left}}}
and
\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{G_{0}^{\text{left}}}
are the gauging and ungauging operators for the
G_{0}^{\text{left}}
symmetry, and
U_{(g_{\ell},g_{r})}
is the invertible symmetry operator of the ungauged model.
More explicitly, the operators on the right-hand side are given by
28
28

28

The general definitions of the gauging and ungauging operators are given in (
IV.15
) and (
IV.22
), respectively.

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{G_{0}^{\text{left}}}\ket{\{g_{i},g_{i}^{\prime}\}}=\ket{\{g_{i}^{\prime};g_{i}^{-1}g_{j}\}},\quad\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{G_{0}^{\text{left}}}=\mathsf{D}_{G_{0}^{\text{left}}}^{\dagger},\quad U_{(g_{\ell},g_{r})}\ket{\{g_{i},g_{i}^{\prime}\}}=\ket{\{g_{\ell}g_{i},g_{r}g_{i}^{\prime}\}},

(IV.66)

where
\{\ket{\{g_{i},g_{i}^{\prime}\}}\mid g_{i},g_{i}^{\prime}\in G_{0}\}
is a basis of the state space of the ungauged model.
Any other 0-form symmetry operators can be obtained from (
IV.65
) by condensing line operators.
Due to the general result in Section
IV.1.3
, the symmetry operator (
IV.65
) can be represented by a tensor network of the form

\mathsf{D}[V_{G_{0}^{\text{left}}}^{(g_{\ell},g_{r})}]=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},

(IV.67)

where both the physical legs and the virtual legs take values in
G_{0}
.
The filled and unfilled green dots on the right-hand side are defined as in (
IV.38
) and (
IV.40
), i.e.,

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{g^{\prime},g_{r}g}\prod_{i,j=1,2,3,4}\delta_{g_{i},g_{j}},\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{g,g_{1}^{-1}g_{2}}\delta_{g^{\prime},g}.

(IV.68)

From the
(g_{\ell},g_{r})
-dependence of the above tensors, we find

\mathsf{D}[V_{G_{0}^{\text{left}}}^{(g_{\ell},g_{r})}]=U_{g_{r}}\mathsf{D}[V_{G_{0}^{\text{left}}}^{(e,e)}],

(IV.69)

where
U_{g_{r}}
is the left multiplication of
g_{r}
acting on the matter fields:

U_{g_{r}}\coloneq\bigotimes_{i\in P}\overrightarrow{X}_{g_{r}}^{(i)}.

(IV.70)

We note that
\mathsf{D}[V_{G_{0}^{\text{left}}}^{(g_{\ell},g_{r})}]
in (
IV.69
) does not depend on
g_{\ell}\in G_{0}
.
This is an immediate consequence of the fact that the symmetry operator for
G_{0}^{\text{left}}
is absorbed by the gauging operator
\mathsf{D}_{G_{0}^{\text{left}}}
in (
IV.65
).

Action tensors.

Due to the general discussions in Section
IV.2
, the symmetry operators of the form (
IV.67
) act on the local tensors of the
G_{0}
-cluster state (
III.56
) as

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\penalty\ =\sum_{g\in G_{0}}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},\quad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\penalty\ =\sum_{g\in G_{0}}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},

(IV.71)

where the action tensors
\phi[V_{G_{0}^{\text{left}}}^{(g_{\ell},g_{r})}]_{g}
and
\overline{\phi}[V_{G_{0}^{\text{left}}}^{(g_{\ell},g_{r})}]_{g}
are defined as in (
IV.60
).
Based on the general expression (
IV.60
), we can compute the components of these action tensors explicitly as
29
29

29

Although these action tensors depend on
g_{\ell}
, the set of the action tensors
\{\phi[V_{G_{0}^{\text{left}}}^{(g_{\ell},g_{r})}]_{g}\mid g\in G_{0}\}
does not because
g_{\ell}
only shifts the subscript
g
.
This is consistent with the fact that the symmetry operator is independent of
g_{\ell}
.

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{g_{1}g_{3}^{-1},gg_{\ell}}\delta_{g_{2}g_{3}^{-1},g_{r}^{-1}gg_{\ell}}.

(IV.72)

These action tensors are the building blocks of the symmetry operators on the boundary of the
G_{0}
-cluster state, which will be discussed in Section
V.2.3
.
See also Section
V.1.3
for a category-theoretical description of the symmetry on the boundary of the
G_{0}
-cluster state.

V
Interfaces of 2+1d generalized cluster states

In this section, we discuss the interface of 2+1d generalized cluster states with group-theoretical fusion 2-category symmetry
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
.
Specifically, we determine the symmetry structure at the interface and investigate gapped interfaces allowed by the symmetry.
We provide both a category-theoretical description and a tensor network description of the interfaces in subsections
V.1
and
V.2
, respectively.
The readers interested only in the tensor network description may skip subsection
V.1
.

V.1
Category theoretical description

We first provide a purely categorical description of the interface between the SPT phases realized by the generalized cluster states.
Our discussion does not rely on the concrete lattice model.
As such, this subsection can be read independently of Sections
III
and
IV
.

V.1.1
Interface symmetries: strip 2-algebras

As we reviewed in Section
II.3
, fiber 2-functors of fusion 2-category
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
are labeled by pairs
(K,\lambda)
, where
K
is a complement of
H
in
G
(i.e., a subgroup of
G
satisfying (
II.17
)) and
\lambda\in Z^{3}(K,\mathrm{U}(1))
is a 3-cocycle on
K
.
In what follows, we will restrict our attention to fiber 2-functors with trivial
\lambda
, in which case a fiber 2-functor is labeled only by
K
.
The fiber 2-functor labeled by
K
can be described as a left
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
-module 2-category
Decoppet:2023bay
30
30

30

We recall that a fiber 2-functor is equivalently defined as a module 2-category that is equivalent to
2\text{Vec}
as a semisimple 2-category.

\mathcal{M}(K):={}_{\text{Vec}_{H}}(2\text{Vec}_{G})_{\text{Vec}_{K}},

(V.1)

which is equivalent to
2\text{Vec}
as a semisimple 2-category because
K
is a complement of
H
in
G

Decoppet:2023bay
.
The left action of
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
on
\mathcal{M}(K)
is given by the relative tensor product over
\text{Vec}_{H}
.
Physically, this fiber 2-functor corresponds to the SPT phase realized by the generalized cluster state (
III.45
).
This SPT phase will be denoted by
\mathsf{SPT}_{K}
.

Now, let us consider an interface between
\mathsf{SPT}_{K_{1}}
and
\mathsf{SPT}_{K_{2}}
.
A symmetry operator acting on the interface can be depicted as

\mathsf{L}(X;m_{1},m_{2}):=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},

(V.2)

where
X\in\mathcal{C}(G;H)
is an object of
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
,
M_{1}\in\mathcal{M}(K_{1})
and
M_{2}\in\mathcal{M}(K_{2})
are the unique simple objects defined by

M_{1}=\text{Vec}_{H}\boxtimes\text{Vec}_{K_{1}},\qquad M_{2}=\text{Vec}_{H}\boxtimes\text{Vec}_{K_{2}},

(V.3)

and
m_{1}\in\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}(K_{1})}(X\boxtimes_{\text{Vec}_{H}}M_{1},M_{1})
and
m_{2}\in\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}(K_{2})}(M_{2},X\boxtimes_{\text{Vec}_{H}}M_{2})
are 1-morphisms of
\mathcal{M}(K_{1})
and
\mathcal{M}(K_{2})
.
Physically,
X
corresponds to a symmetry operator in the bulk,
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
correspond to the ground states of
\mathsf{SPT}_{K_{1}}
and
\mathsf{SPT}_{K_{2}}
, and
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
correspond to topological lines on which the bulk symmetry operator ends.
31
31

31

From the tensor network point of view, the 1-morphisms
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
correspond to the fractionalized symmetry operators defined in Section
IV.2
. See Section
V.2
for more details on this point.

The small red arrows in (
V.2
) specify the coorientations of the surfaces, which will be omitted when no confusion can arise.
We note that
\mathsf{L}(X;m_{1},m_{2})
acts as a line operator on the interface.
Similarly, a topological point operator between
\mathsf{L}(X;m_{1},m_{2})
and
\mathsf{L}(X^{\prime};m_{1}^{\prime},m_{2}^{\prime})
can be depicted as

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},

(V.4)

where
x\in\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}(G;H)}(X,X^{\prime})
is a 1-morphism in
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
, and
\chi_{1}\in\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}(K_{1})}(m_{1},m_{1}^{\prime}\circ(x\boxtimes_{\text{Vec}_{H}}{\mathrm{id}}_{M_{1}}))
and
\chi_{2}\in\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}(K_{2})}((x\boxtimes_{\text{Vec}_{H}}{\mathrm{id}}_{M_{2}})\circ m_{2},m_{2}^{\prime})
are 2-morphisms in
\mathcal{M}(K_{1})
and
\mathcal{M}(K_{2})
.
The line operator
\mathsf{L}(X;m_{1},m_{2})
is not necessarily simple even if
X
,
m_{1}
, and
m_{2}
are simple because there may exist a non-trivial topological point operator on it.
To obtain a simple summand, one may condense an idempotent of the algebra of topological point operators on
\mathsf{L}(X;m_{1},m_{2})
. Fortunately, as we will see later, the line operator
\mathsf{L}(X;m_{1},m_{2})
becomes simple if we choose
X
,
m_{1}
, and
m_{2}
appropriately, and any simple line can be obtained in that way.
Thus, we do not discuss the condensation of an idempotent on the line operator.

If we fold the picture in (
V.2
) along the interface, we obtain

\mathsf{L}(X;m_{1},m_{2})=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},

(V.5)

where
\overline{\mathsf{SPT}_{K_{2}}}
denotes the orientation-reversal of
\mathsf{SPT}_{K_{2}}
.
This SPT phase is labeled by a right
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
-module 2-category
32
32

32

The orientation-reversal of
\mathsf{SPT}_{K_{2}}
has a symmetry
\mathcal{C}(G;H)^{\text{mop}}
, which is the fusion 2-category obtained by reversing the order of the tensor product in
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
. Therefore,
\overline{\mathsf{SPT}_{K_{2}}}
is labeled by a left
\mathcal{C}(G;H)^{\text{mop}}
-module 2-category, or equivalently, a right
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
-module 2-category.

\overline{\mathcal{M}(K_{2})}={}_{\text{Vec}_{K_{2}}}(2\text{Vec}_{G})_{\text{Vec}_{H}}.

(V.6)

The unique simple object of this 2-category is given by
\overline{M_{2}}=\text{Vec}_{K_{2}}\boxtimes\text{Vec}_{H}
.
The figure in (
V.5
) suggests that the symmetry operators acting on the interface can be identified with 1-endomorphisms of
\overline{M}_{2}\boxtimes_{\text{Vec}_{H}}M_{1}
in the 2-category
{}_{\text{Vec}_{K_{2}}}(2\text{Vec}_{G})_{\text{Vec}_{K_{1}}}
.
Similarly, topological point operators between these symmetry operators can be identified with 2-morphisms in the same 2-category.
Therefore, the symmetry category at the interface of
\mathsf{SPT}_{K_{1}}
and
\mathsf{SPT}_{K_{2}}
should be

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}

\displaystyle:=\text{End}_{{}_{\text{Vec}_{K_{2}}}(2\text{Vec}_{G})_{\text{Vec}_{K_{1}}}}(\overline{M_{2}}\boxtimes_{\text{Vec}_{H}}M_{1})

(V.7)

\displaystyle\penalty\displaystyle\cong\text{End}_{{}_{\text{Vec}_{K_{2}}}(2\text{Vec}_{G})_{\text{Vec}_{K_{1}}}}(\text{Vec}_{K_{2}}\boxtimes\text{Vec}_{H}\boxtimes\text{Vec}_{K_{1}}),

which is a multifusion 1-category, whose monoidal structure is given by the composition of 1-morphisms in
{}_{\text{Vec}_{K_{2}}}(2\text{Vec}_{G})_{\text{Vec}_{K_{1}}}
.
The above category will be called a strip 2-algebra following
Cordova:2024iti
;
Gagliano:2025gwr
.

Strip 2-algebra
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
.

Let us describe the multifusion category structure on
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
in more detail.
To this end, we first enumerate all simple objects of
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
.
Pictorially, simple objects of the category on the second line of (
V.7
) can be represented by the following diagram:

\mathsf{L}(h_{1},h_{2},k_{1},k_{2}):=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(V.8)

The relation to the objects of the form (
V.5
) will be explained later.
The green surface labeled by
h_{1},h_{2}\in H
,
k_{1}\in K_{1}
, and
k_{2}\in K_{2}
represent the simple objects
V^{h_{1}},V^{h_{2}},V^{k_{1}},V^{k_{2}}\in 2\text{Vec}_{G}
.
These objects must satisfy

h_{1}k_{1}=k_{2}h_{2}

(V.9)

so that the configuration of the surfaces is compatible with the fusion rules.
We note that
h_{2}
and
k_{2}
are uniquely determined by
h_{1}
and
k_{1}
because
K_{2}
is a complement of
H
in
G
.
The 1-morphisms
\iota_{h_{1}}:\text{Vec}_{H}\to V^{h_{1}}
and
\pi_{h_{2}}:V^{h_{2}}\to\text{Vec}_{H}
in (
V.8
) are the inclusion and projection 1-morphisms.
The simple 1-morphism at the junction of the vertical surface and the middle surface is unique because of the following equivalence of semisimple 1-categories:

\text{Hom}_{2\text{Vec}_{G}}(V^{h_{1}}\boxtimes V^{k_{1}},V^{k_{2}}\boxtimes V^{h_{2}})\cong\delta_{h_{1}k_{1},k_{2}h_{2}}\text{Vec}.

(V.10)

Similarly, the simple 1-morphisms on the top and bottom surfaces where the vertical surface ends are also unique because

\text{Hom}_{(2\text{Vec}_{G})_{\text{Vec}_{K_{1}}}}(V^{k_{1}}\boxtimes\text{Vec}_{K_{1}},\text{Vec}_{K_{1}})\cong\text{Hom}_{{}_{\text{Vec}_{K_{2}}}(2\text{Vec}_{G})}(\text{Vec}_{K_{2}},\text{Vec}_{K_{2}}\boxtimes V^{k_{2}})\cong\text{Vec}.

(V.11)

We note that
\mathsf{L}(h_{1},h_{2},k_{1},k_{2})
is a simple object because we cannot put any non-trivial topological line on the vertical surface in (
V.8
), meaning that
\mathsf{L}(h_{1},h_{2},k_{1},k_{2})
cannot have a non-trivial topological point operator on it.
Furthermore, the same argument shows that there exists a topological point operator between
\mathsf{L}(h_{1},h_{2},k_{1},k_{2})
and
\mathsf{L}(h_{1}^{\prime},h_{2}^{\prime},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}^{\prime})
if and only if
(h_{1},h_{2},k_{1},k_{2})=(h_{1}^{\prime},h_{2}^{\prime},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}^{\prime})
, which implies that

\mathsf{L}(h_{1},h_{2},k_{1},k_{2})\cong\mathsf{L}(h_{1}^{\prime},h_{2}^{\prime},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}^{\prime})\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ (h_{1},h_{2},k_{1},k_{2})=(h_{1}^{\prime},h_{2}^{\prime},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}^{\prime}).

(V.12)

Since
\mathsf{L}(h_{1},h_{2},k_{1},k_{2})
is uniquely determined by the pair
(h_{1},k_{1})
, the number of the isomorphism classes of simple objects is
|H||K_{1}|=|G|
.
33
33

33

This is consistent with the equivalence of semisimple 1-categories
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}\cong\text{Hom}_{2\text{Vec}_{G}}(\text{Vec}_{H},\text{Vec}_{K_{2}}\boxtimes\text{Vec}_{H}\boxtimes\text{Vec}_{K_{1}})\cong\text{Vec}^{\oplus|G|}
, where the first equivalence follows from
(
Decoppet:2023bay
, Lemma 3.2.2)
, and
\text{Vec}^{\oplus|G|}
denotes the direct sum of
|G|
copies of
Vec
. We emphasize that the equivalence
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}\cong\text{Vec}^{\oplus|G|}
is not a monoidal equivalence.

The tensor product of the objects (
V.8
) is given by the horizontal concatenation

\mathsf{L}(h_{1},h_{2},k_{1},k_{2})\otimes\mathsf{L}(h_{1}^{\prime},h_{2}^{\prime},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}^{\prime})=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{0.95}[0.95]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$}.

(V.13)

Since
\pi
and
\iota
on the right-hand side satisfy
\pi_{h_{2}}\circ\iota_{h_{1}^{\prime}}=\delta_{h_{2},h_{1}^{\prime}}{\mathrm{id}}_{V^{h_{1}^{\prime}}}
, it follows that the simple objects obey the following fusion rules:

\mathsf{L}(h_{1},h_{2},k_{1},k_{2})\otimes\mathsf{L}(h_{1}^{\prime},h_{2}^{\prime},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}^{\prime})\cong\delta_{h_{2},h_{1}^{\prime}}\mathsf{L}(h_{1},h_{2}^{\prime},k_{1}k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}k_{2}^{\prime}).

(V.14)

The
F
-symbols of
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
are trivial because we have chosen all the cocycle data to be trivial.
We note that the fusion outcome of two non-zero simple objects can be empty, indicating that
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
is generally a multifusion category rather than a fusion category.
34
34

34

A multifusion category is a finite semisimple
\mathbb{C}
-linear rigid monoidal category
EGNO2015
. A fusion category is a multifusion category whose unit object is simple. See Appendix
E
for more details of multifusion categories.

Direct sum decomposition of
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
.

We can decompose
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
into a direct sum of indecomposable multifusion categories by using the following monoidal equivalence:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{h_{1}\in H,h_{2}\in H}\text{Hom}_{{}_{\text{Vec}_{K_{2}}}(2\text{Vec}_{G})_{\text{Vec}_{K_{1}}}}(\text{Vec}_{K_{2}}\boxtimes V^{h_{2}}\boxtimes\text{Vec}_{K_{1}},\text{Vec}_{K_{2}}\boxtimes V^{h_{1}}\boxtimes\text{Vec}_{K_{1}})

(V.15)

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{x\in K_{2}\backslash G/K_{1}}\bigoplus_{h_{1},h_{2}\in H\cap x}\text{Hom}_{{}_{\text{Vec}_{K_{2}}}(2\text{Vec}_{G})_{\text{Vec}_{K_{1}}}}(\text{Vec}_{K_{2}}\boxtimes V^{h_{2}}\boxtimes\text{Vec}_{K_{1}},\text{Vec}_{K_{2}}\boxtimes V^{h_{1}}\boxtimes\text{Vec}_{K_{1}})

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{x\in K_{2}\backslash G/K_{1}}\bigoplus_{h_{1},h_{2}\in H\cap x}(\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x})_{h_{1},h_{2}}.

Here, we defined

(\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x})_{h_{1},h_{2}}:=\text{Hom}_{{}_{\text{Vec}_{K_{2}}}(2\text{Vec}_{G})_{\text{Vec}_{K_{1}}}}(\text{Vec}_{K_{2}}\boxtimes V^{h_{2}}\boxtimes\text{Vec}_{K_{1}},\text{Vec}_{K_{2}}\boxtimes V^{h_{1}}\boxtimes\text{Vec}_{K_{1}})

(V.16)

for
x\in K_{2}\backslash G/K_{1}
and
h_{1},h_{2}\in H\cap x
.
The first equivalence of (
V.15
) immediately follows from (
V.7
), and the second equivalence holds because the direct sum component of the right-hand side on the first line is non-empty if and only if
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
are in the same
(K_{2},K_{1})
-double coset in
G
.
The monoidal structure on the right-hand side of (
V.15
) is given by the composition of 1-morphisms in
{}_{\text{Vec}_{K_{2}}}(2\text{Vec}_{G})_{\text{Vec}_{K_{1}}}
.
We note that for any pair of objects
\mathsf{L}\in(\mathcal{C}^{x}_{K_{1},K_{1}})_{h_{1},h_{2}}
and
\mathsf{L}^{\prime}\in(\mathcal{C}^{x^{\prime}}_{K_{1},K_{2}})_{h_{1}^{\prime},h_{2}^{\prime}}
, the tensor product
\mathsf{L}\otimes\mathsf{L}^{\prime}
is zero if
x\neq x^{\prime}
.
Thus, we find that the category

\mathcal{C}^{x}_{K_{1},K_{2}}:=\bigoplus_{h_{1},h_{2}\in H\cap x}(\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x})_{h_{1},h_{2}}

(V.17)

is itself a multifusion category for each
x\in K_{2}\backslash G/K_{1}
.
Furthermore,
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x}
is indecomposable because for any object
\mathsf{L}\in\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x}
, there exists
\mathsf{L}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x}
such that
\mathsf{L}\otimes\mathsf{L}^{\prime}
is non-zero.
Therefore, the strip 2-algebra
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
can be decomposed into a direct sum of indecomposable multifusion categories as

\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}\cong\bigoplus_{x\in K_{2}\backslash G/K_{1}}\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x},

(V.18)

where
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x}
is the full subcategory that contains simple objects
\mathsf{L}(h_{1},h_{2},k_{1},k_{2})
for all
h_{1},h_{2}\in H\cap x
.

Indecomposable multifusion category
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x}
.

Let us unpack the definition of
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x}
.
To this end, we first recall that
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x}
can be decomposed into a direct sum (
V.17
) as a semisimple category.
Simple objects of each component
(\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x})_{h_{1},h_{2}}
are
\mathsf{L}(h_{1},h_{2},k_{1},k_{2})
, where

h_{1}k_{1}=k_{2}h_{2}\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ k_{1}=h_{1}^{-1}k_{2}h_{2}\in K_{1}\cap h_{1}^{-1}K_{2}h_{2}.

(V.19)

The set
K_{1}\cap h_{1}^{-1}K_{2}h_{2}
is not empty because
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
are in the same double
(K_{2},K_{1})
-coset in
G
.
Furthermore, for each
k_{1}\in K_{1}\cap h_{1}^{-1}K_{2}h_{2}
, there exists a unique
k_{2}\in K_{2}
that satisfies the above condition.
Thus, the simple objects of
(\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x})_{h_{1},h_{2}}
are in one-to-one correspondence with elements of
K_{1}\cap h_{1}^{-1}K_{2}h_{2}
.
This implies the equivalence of semisimple categories

(\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x})_{h_{1},h_{2}}\cong\text{Vec}_{K_{1}\cap h_{1}^{-1}K_{2}h_{2}},

(V.20)

where the right-hand side is the category of finite dimensional vector spaces graded by the set
K_{1}\cap h_{1}^{-1}K_{2}h_{2}
.
Therefore, we find

\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x}\cong\bigoplus_{h_{1},h_{2}\in H\cap x}\text{Vec}_{K_{1}\cap h_{1}^{-1}K_{2}h_{2}}.

(V.21)

We note that each component
\text{Vec}_{K_{1}\cap h_{1}^{-1}K_{2}h_{2}}
is not necessarily a monoidal category because
K_{1}\cap h_{1}^{-1}K_{2}h_{2}
is not necessarily a group.
Nevertheless, the direct sum (
V.21
) has a monoidal structure, which we describe shortly.

The decomposition (
V.21
) implies that an object of
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x}
can be identified with a
|H\cap x|\times|H\cap x|
matrix whose
(h_{1},h_{2})
-component is an object of
\text{Vec}_{K_{1}\cap h_{1}^{-1}K_{2}h_{2}}
.
The tensor product of objects is then given by matrix multiplication, where the sum and the product of components are defined by the direct sum and the tensor product of graded vector spaces.
In particular, a simple object
\mathsf{L}(h_{1},h_{2},k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x}
corresponds to a matrix
(\mathbb{C}_{k_{1}})_{h_{1},h_{2}}
whose
(h_{1},h_{2})
-component is a one-dimensional vector space
\mathbb{C}_{k_{1}}
graded by
k_{1}\in K_{1}\cap h_{1}^{-1}K_{2}h_{2}
and other components are empty.
The tensor product of the simple objects corresponds to the matrix multiplication

(\mathbb{C}_{k_{1}})_{h_{1},h_{2}}(\mathbb{C}_{k_{1}^{\prime}})_{h_{1}^{\prime},h_{2}^{\prime}}=\delta_{h_{2},h_{1}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{C}_{k_{1}k_{1}^{\prime}})_{h_{1},h_{2}^{\prime}},

(V.22)

which agrees with the fusion rule (
V.14
).

More abstractly, the monoidal structure on
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x}
is specified by a
((\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x})_{h_{1},h_{1}},(\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x})_{h_{2},h_{2}})
-bimodule category structure on
(\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x})_{h_{1},h_{2}}
for every pair
h_{1},h_{2}\in H\cap x
.
This bimodule structure is given by the obvious one induced by the group multiplication, i.e.,

\mathbb{C}_{k_{1}}\vartriangleright\mathbb{C}_{k_{1}^{\prime}}\vartriangleleft\mathbb{C}_{k_{1}^{\prime\prime}}=\mathbb{C}_{k_{1}k_{1}^{\prime}k_{1}^{\prime\prime}}

(V.23)

for all
k_{1}\in K_{1}\cap h_{1}^{-1}K_{2}h_{1}
,
k_{1}^{\prime}\in K_{1}\cap h_{1}^{-1}K_{2}h_{2}
, and
k_{1}^{\prime\prime}\in K_{1}\cap h_{2}^{-1}K_{2}h_{2}
.
Here,
\vartriangleright
and
\vartriangleleft
denote the left action of
(\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x})_{h_{1},h_{1}}\cong\text{Vec}_{K_{1}\cap h_{1}^{-1}K_{2}h_{1}}
and the right action of
(\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x})_{h_{2},h_{2}}\cong\text{Vec}_{K_{1}\cap h_{2}^{-1}K_{2}h_{2}}
on
(\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x})_{h_{1},h_{2}}\cong\text{Vec}_{K_{1}\cap h_{1}^{-1}K_{2}h_{2}}
.

Relation between (
V.5
) and (
V.8
).

The objects of the strip 2-algebra
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
were originally expressed as in (
V.5
).
On the other hand, in the above discussion, we used another expression (
V.8
).
We now explain the relation between them.
According to (
V.5
), the strip 2-algebra
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
has an object of the form

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},

(V.24)

where
f_{1}\in\text{Hom}_{{}_{\text{Vec}_{H}}(2\text{Vec}_{G})_{\text{Vec}_{K_{1}}}}(V_{H}^{g}\boxtimes_{\text{Vec}_{H}}M_{1},M_{1})
and
f_{2}\in\text{Hom}_{{}_{\text{Vec}_{K_{2}}}(2\text{Vec}_{G})_{\text{Vec}_{H}}}(\overline{M_{2}},\overline{M_{2}}\boxtimes_{\text{Vec}_{H}}V_{H}^{g})
are simple 1-morphisms.
Recalling that
V_{H}^{g}=\text{Vec}_{H}\boxtimes V^{g}\boxtimes\text{Vec}_{H}
,
M_{1}=\text{Vec}_{H}\boxtimes\text{Vec}_{K_{1}}
, and
\overline{M_{2}}=\text{Vec}_{K_{2}}\boxtimes\text{Vec}_{H}
, we can write any simple 1-morphisms
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
as follows:

f_{1}=f_{1}(g;h_{1}):=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},

(V.25)

f_{2}=f_{2}(g;h_{2})=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(V.26)

Here, the elements
h_{1},\widetilde{h}_{1},h_{2},\widetilde{h}_{2}\in H
,
k_{1}\in K_{1}
, and
k_{2}\in K_{2}
must satisfy

h_{1}k_{1}=g\widetilde{h}_{1},\qquad h_{2}g=k_{2}\widetilde{h}_{2}.

(V.27)

We note that
\widetilde{h}_{1}
and
k_{1}
are uniquely determined by
g
and
h_{1}
because
K_{1}
is a complement of
H
in
G
.
Similarly,
\widetilde{h}_{2}
and
k_{2}
are uniquely determined by
g
and
h_{2}
.
More specifically,
\tilde{h}_{1}
and
\tilde{h}_{2}
can be written as

\widetilde{h}_{1}=(U_{g;K_{1}}^{-1}(h_{1}^{-1}))^{-1},\qquad\widetilde{h}_{2}=(U_{g;K_{2}}^{-1}(h_{2}^{-1}))^{-1},

(V.28)

where
U_{g;K}:H\to H
is defined by (
IV.59
).
The 1-morphism
m:\text{Vec}_{H}\boxtimes\text{Vec}_{H}\to\text{Vec}_{H}
in (
V.25
) is the multiplication 1-morphism of
\text{Vec}_{H}\in 2\text{Vec}_{G}
(i.e., the tensor product of
\text{Vec}_{H}
), and
m^{*}:\text{Vec}_{H}\to\text{Vec}_{H}\boxtimes\text{Vec}_{H}
in (
V.26
) is the dual of
m
.
Using (
V.25
), (
V.26
), and the equivalence
\text{Vec}_{H}\boxtimes_{\text{Vec}_{H}}\text{Vec}_{H}\cong\text{Vec}_{H}
, we can express the diagram in (
V.24
) as

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(V.29)

By comparing this with (
V.8
), we find that the object (
V.24
) with
f_{1}=f_{1}(g;h_{1})
and
f_{2}=f_{2}(g;h_{2})
can be identified with
\mathsf{L}(h_{2}h_{1},\widetilde{h}_{2}\widetilde{h}_{1},k_{1},k_{2})
.
In particular, this shows that the object (
V.24
) is simple, and any simple object is of this form.
The isomorphism class of the above object depends only on the pair
(h_{2}h_{1},k_{1})
due to (
V.12
).
Since
k_{1}
is the unique element of
K_{1}\cap h^{-1}gH
, we find

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\cong\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ (h_{2}h_{1},h_{1}^{-1}gH)=(h_{2}^{\prime}h_{1}^{\prime},(h_{1}^{\prime})^{-1}g^{\prime}H).

(V.30)

Fusion rules revisited.

For later convenience, we compute the fusion rules of the objects of the form (
V.29
).
35
35

35

We recall that the fusion rules of the objects (
V.8
) are already computed as in (
V.14
).

The tensor product of the objects is given by the concatenation

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},

(V.31)

where
\widetilde{h}_{1}
and
\widetilde{h}_{2}
are defined by (
V.28
), and
\widetilde{h}_{1}^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{h}_{2}^{\prime}
are also defined similarly.
By fusing the middle gray surfaces on the right-hand side, we end up with

\text{RHS}\cong\bigoplus_{h\in H}\delta_{h,\widetilde{h}_{1}(h_{1}^{\prime})^{-1}}\delta_{h,\widetilde{h}_{2}(h_{2}^{\prime})^{-1}}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(V.32)

By combining the above two equations, we find

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\cong\delta_{\widetilde{h}_{1}(h_{1}^{\prime})^{-1},\widetilde{h}_{2}(h_{2}^{\prime})^{-1}}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},

(V.33)

where
g^{\prime\prime}=g\widetilde{h}_{1}(h_{1}^{\prime})^{-1}g^{\prime}
.

V.1.2
Gapped interface modes

Following the classification of 1+1d gapped phases with non-invertible symmetries
Thorngren:2019iar
;
Komargodski:2020mxz
, we expect that gapped interfaces between
\mathsf{SPT}_{K_{1}}
and
\mathsf{SPT}_{K_{2}}
are classified by indecomposable
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
-module categories.
36
36

36

In
Thorngren:2019iar
;
Komargodski:2020mxz
, it was shown that 1+1d gapped phases described by topological field theories with fusion category symmetry
\mathcal{C}
are classified by indecomposable
\mathcal{C}
-module categories. We expect that the same result holds when
\mathcal{C}
is a multifusion category.

The degeneracy of the interface modes should be equal to the number of simple objects of the corresponding module category.
In particular, a non-degenerate interface should correspond to a
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
-module category
Vec
, i.e., a fiber functor of
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
.
In what follows, we discuss the necessary and sufficient condition for
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
to admit a fiber functor.
We will also study the 2-category of gapped interfaces and its relation to the Morita dual of
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
.

Existence of non-degenerate gapped interfaces.

We show that
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
admits a fiber functor if and only if
\mathsf{SPT}_{K_{1}}
and
\mathsf{SPT}_{K_{2}}
correspond to isomorphic fiber 2-functors.
This result implies that the symmetry at the interface of different SPT phases is anomalous in the sense that it does not admit a non-degenerate gapped ground state.

To show the above statement, we first notice that
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
admits a fiber functor if and only if at least one of the direct sum components in (
V.18
) does.
Since each component
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x}
is an indecomposable multifusion category, it admits a fiber functor only if it is fusion (see Appendix
E.2
for a proof).
By definition,
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x}
is fusion if and only if
|H\cap x|=1
.
When
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x}
is fusion, it admits a fiber functor because it is monoidally equivalent to
\text{Vec}_{K_{1}\cap h^{-1}K_{2}h}
, where
h\in H\cap x
.
Therefore,
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
admits a fiber functor if and only if there exists a double coset
x\in K_{2}\backslash G/K_{1}
such that
|H\cap x|=1
.

Now, we show that the existence of a fiber functor of
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
implies an isomorphism between the fiber 2-functors corresponding to
\mathsf{SPT}_{K_{1}}
and
\mathsf{SPT}_{K_{2}}
.
To this end, we suppose that
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
admits a fiber functor.
In this case, there exists
x\in K_{2}\backslash G/K_{1}
such that
|H\cap x|=1
.
Such a double coset
x
can be written as

x=K_{2}hK_{1}=hK_{1},

(V.34)

where
h
is the unique element of
H\cap x
.
The second equality holds because if
K_{2}hK_{1}
were strictly bigger than
hK_{1}
,
x
would contain other elements of
H
.
The above equation implies

K_{2}hK_{1}=hK_{1}\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ K_{2}h=hK_{1}\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ K_{2}=hK_{1}h^{-1}.

(V.35)

This shows that if
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
admits a fiber functor, there exists
h\in H
that satisfies
K_{2}=hK_{1}h^{-1}
.
Thus, due to the results reviewed in Section
II.3
, the fiber 2-functors corresponding to
\mathsf{SPT}_{K_{1}}
and
\mathsf{SPT}_{K_{2}}
are isomorphic to each other.

Conversely, we can also show that an isomorphism between the fiber 2-functors corresponding to
\mathsf{SPT}_{K_{1}}
and
\mathsf{SPT}_{K_{2}}
implies the existence of a fiber functor of
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
.
To see this, we suppose that
\mathsf{SPT}_{K_{1}}
and
\mathsf{SPT}_{K_{2}}
correspond to isomorphic fiber 2-functors.
Namely, we suppose that there exists
h\in H
such that
K_{2}=hK_{1}h^{-1}
.
37
37

37

We recall that fiber 2-functors corresponding to
\mathsf{SPT}_{K_{1}}
and
\mathsf{SPT}_{K_{2}}
are isomorphic to each other if and only if there exists
g\in G
such that
K_{2}=gK_{1}g^{-1}
. Since
K_{1}
is a complement of
H
in
G
, there exist
h\in H
and
k_{1}\in K_{1}
such that
g=hk_{1}
, which implies that
gK_{1}g^{-1}=hK_{1}h^{-1}
.

In this case, we have

K_{2}hK_{1}=hK_{1}h^{-1}hK_{1}=hK_{1},

(V.36)

which implies that
x:=K_{2}hK_{1}
satisfies
|H\cap x|=1
.
This shows that if
\mathsf{SPT}_{K_{1}}
and
\mathsf{SPT}_{K_{2}}
correspond to isomorphic fiber 2-functors, there exists
x\in K_{2}\backslash G/K_{1}
such that
|H\cap x|=1
, and hence
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
admits a fiber functor.

Set of non-degenerate gapped interfaces.

As mentioned above, non-degenerate gapped interfaces between
\mathsf{SPT}_{K_{1}}
and
\mathsf{SPT}_{K_{2}}
should correspond to fiber functors of
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
.
The fiber functors of
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
are in one-to-one correspondence with those of the direct sum components of
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
.
Each direct sum component
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x}
admits a fiber functor if and only if
x
satisfies
|H\cap x|=1
.
Such an
x
can always be written as
x=K_{2}hK_{1}
, where
h\in H
satisfies
K_{2}=hK_{1}h^{-1}
.
When
x=K_{2}hK_{1}
, there is a monoidal equivalence
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x}\cong\text{Vec}_{K_{1}\cap h^{-1}K_{2}h}=\text{Vec}_{K_{1}}
.
Thus, fiber functors of
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x}
are classified by the second group cohomology
H^{2}(K_{1},\mathrm{U}(1))
.
Taking all direct sum components into account, we find that fiber functors of
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
are in one-to-one correspondence with pairs
(h,[\xi])
, where
h\in H
satisfies
K_{2}=hK_{1}h^{-1}
and
[\xi]\in H^{2}(K_{1},\mathrm{U}(1))
is an element of the second group cohomology of
K_{1}
.
In other words, we have the following equality of sets:

\{\text{Fiber functors of $\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}$}\}=\{(h,[\xi])\mid h\in H\text{ s.t. }K_{2}=hK_{1}h^{-1},\penalty\ [\xi]\in H^{2}(K_{1},\mathrm{U}(1))\}.

(V.37)

Here, the left-hand side is the set of (isomorphism classes of) fiber functors of
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
.

2-category of gapped interfaces.

General gapped interfaces between
\mathsf{SPT}_{K_{1}}
and
\mathsf{SPT}_{K_{2}}
should be classified by module categories over
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
.
Similarly, gapped junctions between gapped interfaces would correspond to
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
-module functors, and junction-changing operators, which are point-like operators in spacetime, would correspond to module natural transformations.
Thus, we expect that gapped interfaces, gapped junctions, and junction-changing operators form a 2-category
\text{Mod}(\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}})
, whose objects, 1-morphisms, and 2-morphisms are
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
-module categories, module functors, and module natural transformations.
In what follows, we will show the equivalence of semisimple 2-categories

\text{Mod}(\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}})\cong\text{Fun}_{\mathcal{C}(G;H)}(\mathcal{M}(K_{2}),\mathcal{M}(K_{1})),

(V.38)

where the objects, 1-morphisms, and 2-morphisms of the right-hand side are
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
-module 2-functors from
\mathcal{M}(K_{1})
to
\mathcal{M}(K_{2})
, module natural transformations between them, and module modifications between the module natural transformations.

To show the equivalence (
V.38
), let us first compute the left-hand side of (
V.38
).
The direct sum decomposition (
V.18
) implies

\text{Mod}(\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}})\cong\bigoplus_{x\in K_{2}\backslash G/K_{1}}\text{Mod}(\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x}),

(V.39)

where
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x}
is an indecomposable multifusion category (
V.21
).
Since
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x}
is indecomposable,
\text{Mod}(\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x})
is equivalent to
\text{Mod}((\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x})_{h,h})
for any diagonal component
(\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}^{x})_{h,h}

(
Douglas:2018qfz
, Example 1.4.16)
.
Therefore, we find

\text{Mod}(\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}})\cong\bigoplus_{x\in K_{2}\backslash G/K_{1}}\text{Mod}(\text{Vec}_{K_{1}\cap h_{x}^{-1}K_{2}h_{x}}),

(V.40)

where
h_{x}\in H\cap x
is arbitrary.
On the other hand, the right-hand side of (
V.38
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\text{Fun}_{\mathcal{C}(G;H)}(\mathcal{M}(K_{2}),\mathcal{M}(K_{1}))

\displaystyle\cong\text{Fun}_{2\text{Vec}_{G}}((2\text{Vec}_{G})_{\text{Vec}_{K_{2}}},(2\text{Vec}_{G})_{\text{Vec}_{K_{1}}})

(V.41)

\displaystyle\cong{}_{\text{Vec}_{K_{2}}}(2\text{Vec}_{G})_{\text{Vec}_{K_{1}}},

where the first equivalence follows from the Morita equivalence between
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
and
2\text{Vec}_{G}

(
Decoppet2023Morita
, Theorem 5.4.3)
and the second equivalence follows from
(
Decoppet2023Morita
, Theorem 5.1.2)
.
The right-hand side of the above equation is also equivalent to
\bigoplus_{x\in K_{2}\backslash G/K_{1}}\text{Mod}(\text{Vec}_{K_{1}\cap h_{x}^{-1}K_{2}h_{x}})
due to
(
Decoppet:2023bay
, Proposition 3.2.1)
.
Therefore, we find

\text{Fun}_{\mathcal{C}(G;H)}(\mathcal{M}(K_{2}),\mathcal{M}(K_{1}))\cong\bigoplus_{x\in K_{2}\backslash G/K_{1}}\text{Mod}(\text{Vec}_{K_{1}\cap h_{x}^{-1}K_{2}h_{x}}).

(V.42)

Equations (
V.40
) and (
V.42
) imply the equivalence (
V.38
).

Relation to the dual 2-category.

When
K_{1}=K_{2}=:K
, equation (
V.38
) reduces to the equivalence of semisimple 2-categories

\text{Mod}(\mathcal{C}_{K,K})\cong\text{Fun}_{\mathcal{C}(G;H)}(\mathcal{M}(K),\mathcal{M}(K)).

(V.43)

The right-hand side is known as the Morita dual of
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
with respect to
\mathcal{M}(K)
.
This 2-category has a monoidal structure defined by the composition of module functors, and there is a monoidal equivalence
(
Decoppet:2023bay
, Corollary 4.1.5)

\text{Fun}_{\mathcal{C}(G;H)}(\mathcal{M}(K),\mathcal{M}(K))\cong\mathcal{C}(G;K).

(V.44)

On the other hand, the left-hand side of (
V.43
) should also have a monoidal structure, which is physically given by the fusion of gapped self-interfaces of
\mathsf{SPT}_{K}
(i.e., gapped excitations within
\mathsf{SPT}_{K}
).
It is then natural to expect that (
V.43
) is a monoidal equivalence, not just an equivalence of 2-categories.
38
38

38

The 1-categorical analogue of this monoidal equivalence is known to hold
Kitaev:2011dxc
;
Bai:2025zze
.

Namely, we expect a monoidal equivalence

\text{Mod}(\mathcal{C}_{K,K})\cong\mathcal{C}(G;K).

(V.45)

To show this monoidal equivalence, we need to rigorously define a monoidal structure on
\text{Mod}(\mathcal{C}_{K,K})
by using an additional structure on
\mathcal{C}_{K,K}
.
We will not do this in this paper.
39
39

39

Nevertheless, we mention that invertible objects of
\mathcal{C}(G;K)
should correspond to fiber functors of
\mathcal{C}_{K,K}
under the conjectural monoidal equivalence (
V.45
).
In particular, due to (
V.37
), invertible objects of
\mathcal{C}(G;K)
should be labeled by pairs
(h,[\xi])
, where
h\in H
satisfies
K=hKh^{-1}
and
[\xi]\in H^{2}(K,\mathrm{U}(1))
.
The object labeled by
(h,[\xi])
can be obtained by starting from a non-invertible object
V_{K}^{h}=\text{Vec}_{K}\boxtimes V^{h}\boxtimes\text{Vec}_{K}
and condensing the line operators forming the twisted group algebra
\mathbb{C}[H]^{\xi}\in\text{Vec}_{H}\cong\text{End}_{\mathcal{C}(G;K)}(V_{K}^{h})
.

V.1.3
Example: gapped boundary of the
G
-cluster state

As a simple example, let us consider the interface between the SPT phases realized by the
G_{0}
-cluster state (
III.50
) and the trivial product state.
As discussed in Section
III.3
, the SPT phase of the
G_{0}
-cluster state is obtained by the following choice of the input data:

G=G_{0}\times G_{0},\quad H=G_{0}^{\text{left}},\quad K=K_{\text{cluster}}:=G_{0}^{\text{diag}}.

(V.46)

The symmetry of the
G_{0}
-cluster state is
2\text{Rep}(G_{0})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}
.
On the other hand, the trivial product state with the same symmetry is obtained by choosing
G
and
H
as above and
K
to be

K=K_{\text{triv}}:=G_{0}^{\text{right}}.

(V.47)

The symmetry at the interface of the above SPT phases is described by the strip 2-algebra (
V.18
) with
K_{1}=K_{\text{cluster}}
and
K_{2}=K_{\text{triv}}
.
Since a
(K_{\text{triv}},K_{\text{cluster}})
-double coset
x
in
G_{0}\times G_{0}
is unique, the right-hand side of (
V.18
) consists only of a single component, which implies that the strip 2-algebra
\mathcal{C}_{K_{\text{cluster}},K_{\text{triv}}}
is an indecomposable multifusion category.
The unique double coset
x
is given by
G_{0}\times G_{0}
itself, and hence it follows that
H\cap x=H=G_{0}^{\text{left}}
.
Therefore, as a semisimple category, we have

\mathcal{C}_{K_{\text{cluster}},K_{\text{triv}}}\cong\bigoplus_{g_{1},g_{2}\in G_{0}^{\text{left}}}\text{Vec}_{G^{\text{diag}}_{0}\cap g_{1}^{-1}G_{0}^{\text{right}}g_{2}}.

(V.48)

Each component on the right-hand side has a unique simple object up to isomorphism because the set
G_{0}^{\text{diag}}\cap g_{1}^{-1}G_{0}^{\text{right}}g_{2}
is a singleton for any pair
g_{1},g_{2}\in G_{0}^{\text{left}}
.
If we denote the simple object of each component by
\mathsf{L}(g_{1},g_{2})
, the fusion rules can be written as

\mathsf{L}(g_{1},g_{2})\otimes\mathsf{L}(g_{1}^{\prime},g_{2}^{\prime})\cong\delta_{g_{2},g_{1}^{\prime}}\mathsf{L}(g_{1},g_{2}^{\prime}).

(V.49)

The above fusion rules imply that there is a monoidal equivalence

\mathcal{C}_{K_{\text{cluster}},K_{\text{triv}}}\cong\text{Mat}_{|G_{0}|}(\text{Vec}):=\text{End}(\text{Vec}^{\oplus|G_{0}|}),

(V.50)

where
\text{Vec}^{\oplus|G|}
denotes the direct sum of
|G_{0}|
copies of
Vec
.

An indecomposable module category over
\mathcal{C}_{K_{\text{cluster}},K_{\text{triv}}}\cong\text{Mat}_{|G_{0}|}(\text{Vec})
is unique up to equivalence and is given by
\text{Vec}^{\oplus|G_{0}|}
.
In particular,
\mathcal{C}_{K_{\text{cluster}},K_{\text{triv}}}
does not have a fiber functor.
Therefore, the SPT phase realized by the
G_{0}
-cluster state does not admit a non-degenerate gapped boundary unless the symmetry is explicitly broken.
This result can be regarded as the bulk-boundary correspondence.

V.2
Tensor network description

We now describe the interface of the generalized cluster states using the tensor networks.
We will see that the tensor network description is consistent with the category-theoretical description.
Unless otherwise stated, the set
S_{H\backslash G}
of right
H
-cosets in
G
is taken to be a complement of
H
in
G
as in Section
IV.2
.
This choice of
S_{H\backslash G}
guarantees that the symmetry operator
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]
is represented by the PEPO (
IV.39
), and the action tensors for it are given by (
IV.60
).

V.2.1
Interface symmetries

We first write down the tensor network representations of the symmetry operators acting on the interface.
For simplicity, we will consider the interface represented by the following MPO:
40
40

40

More generally, one can consider the interface that supports physical degrees of freedom as long as the entire system preserves the symmetry
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
. We will not consider such an interface in this paper.

\ket{\mathcal{I}}:=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(V.51)

Here, the left side of the interface is occupied by
\ket{\text{cluster}(G;H;K_{1})}
, while the right side is occupied by
\ket{\text{cluster}(G;H;K_{2})}
.
The orange dot represents an MPO tensor
\mathcal{I}
on the interface.
Using (
IV.61
) and (
IV.62
), one can compute the action of the symmetry operator
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]
on
\ket{\mathcal{I}}
as

\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]\ket{\mathcal{I}}=\sum_{h_{1},h_{2}\in H}\ket{\phi_{K_{1}}[V_{H}^{g}]_{h_{1}}\cdot\mathcal{I}\cdot\overline{\phi_{K_{2}}}[V_{H}^{g}]_{h_{2}}},

(V.52)

where the new MPO tensor on the right-hand side is defined by

\phi_{K_{1}}[V_{H}^{g}]_{h_{1}}\cdot\mathcal{I}\cdot\overline{\phi_{K_{2}}}[V_{H}^{g}]_{h_{2}}:=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(V.53)

We recall that
\phi_{K}[V_{H}^{g}]_{h}
and
\overline{\phi_{K}}[V_{H}^{g}]_{h}
are the action tensors defined by (
IV.60
).
The above equation implies that the symmetry operators on the interface are of the form

\hat{\mathsf{L}}(g;h_{1},h_{2}):=\bigotimes_{i:\text{sites}}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}(g;h_{1},h_{2}).

(V.54)

The tensor product is taken over all sites on the interface, and each on-site operator
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}(g;h_{1},h_{2})
is defined by

\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}(g;h_{1},h_{2}):=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(V.55)

In the second equality, we folded the diagram along the interface.
The last diagram represents an operator acting on the two legs on the right.
Since each leg has the bond Hilbert space
\mathbb{C}[H]
, the on-site operator
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}(g;h_{1},h_{2})
acts on
\mathbb{C}[H]^{\otimes 2}
.
Using (
IV.60
) and (
V.55
), one can compute the symmetry action on each site explicitly as

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}(g;h_{1},h_{2})\ket{h_{\ell},h_{r}}_{i}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}

(V.56)

\displaystyle=\delta_{h_{r}h_{\ell}^{-1},U_{g;K_{2}}^{-1}(h_{2})(U_{g;K_{1}}^{-1}(h_{1}))^{-1}}\ket{h_{1}\widetilde{U}_{g;S_{H\backslash G}}((U_{g;K_{1}}^{-1}(h_{1}))^{-1}h_{\ell}),h_{2}\widetilde{U}_{g;S_{H\backslash G}}((U_{g;K_{2}}^{-1}(h_{2}))^{-1}h_{r})}_{i},

where
U_{g;K}:H\to H
and
\widetilde{U}_{g;S_{H\backslash G}}:H\to H
are the invertible maps uniquely determined by

U_{g;K}(h)gh^{-1}\in K,\qquad(\widetilde{U}_{g;S_{H\backslash G}}(h))^{-1}gh\in S_{H\backslash G},\qquad\forall h\in H.

(V.57)

A direct computation based on (
V.56
) shows that the symmetry operators (
V.54
) obey the following fusion rules:

\hat{\mathsf{L}}(g;h_{1},h_{2})\hat{\mathsf{L}}(g^{\prime};h_{1}^{\prime},h_{2}^{\prime})=\delta_{(h_{1}^{\prime})^{-1}U^{-1}_{g;K_{1}}(h_{1}),(h_{2}^{\prime})^{-1}U^{-1}_{g;K_{2}}(h_{2})}\hat{\mathsf{L}}(g(U_{g;K_{1}}^{-1}(h_{1}))^{-1}h_{1}^{\prime}g^{\prime};h_{1},h_{2}).

(V.58)

A detailed derivation will be provided in Appendix
C.3
.
The Kronecker delta on the right-hand side indicates that the symmetry at the interface is described by a multifusion category, which is not necessarily fusion.
In particular, as opposed to the case of a fusion category, the identity operator can be decomposed into a sum of symmetry operators as

{\mathrm{id}}=\sum_{h\in H}\hat{\mathsf{L}}(e;e,h),

(V.59)

which readily follows from
{\mathrm{id}}_{\mathbb{C}[H]^{\otimes 2}}=\sum_{h\in H}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}(e;e,h)
.

Relation to the category theoretical description.

The fusion rules (
V.58
) suggest that the symmetry operator
\hat{\mathsf{L}}(g;h_{1},h_{2})
corresponds to the following simple object of the strip 2-algebra
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
:

\hat{\mathsf{L}}(g;h_{1},h_{2})\penalty\ \longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(V.60)

Here,
f_{1}(g;h_{1}^{-1})
and
f_{2}(g;h_{2})
are the simple 1-morphisms defined by (
V.25
) and (
V.26
).
We note that the fusion rules (
V.33
) of the objects on the right-hand side agree with the fusion rules (
V.58
) of the operators on the left-hand side.
Equation (
V.60
) implies that the action tensors
\phi_{K_{1}}[V_{H}^{g}]_{h_{1}}
and
\overline{\phi_{K_{2}}}[V_{H}^{g}]_{h_{2}}
correspond to the following simple 1-morphisms:

\phi_{K_{1}}[V_{H}^{g}]_{h_{1}}\leftrightarrow f_{1}(g;h_{1}^{-1}),\qquad\overline{\phi_{K_{2}}}[V_{H}^{g}]_{h_{2}}\leftrightarrow f_{2}(g;h_{2}).

(V.61)

The symmetry operators
\hat{\mathsf{L}}(g;h_{1},h_{2})
and
\hat{\mathsf{L}}(g^{\prime};h_{1}^{\prime},h_{2}^{\prime})
are equal to each other if the corresponding objects of
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
are isomorphic to each other.
More specifically, we have (cf. (
V.30
))

(h_{2}h_{1}^{-1},h_{1}gH)=(h_{2}^{\prime}(h_{1}^{\prime})^{-1},h_{1}^{\prime}g^{\prime}H)\penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \hat{\mathsf{L}}(g;h_{1},h_{2})=\hat{\mathsf{L}}(g^{\prime};h_{1}^{\prime},h_{2}^{\prime}).

(V.62)

See Appendix
C.4
for a proof.
On the other hand, the converse is not necessarily true.
Namely, in general, the symmetry operators corresponding to non-isomorphic objects of
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
can coincide with each other.
In other words, the action of the symmetry
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
on the interface is generally unfaithful.
41
41

41

We may say that the strip 2-algebra
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
acts via a tensor functor from
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
to another multifusion category
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
that acts faithfully on the interface (
V.51
). The unfaithfulness of the symmetry action does not affect the bulk-boundary correspondence because the symmetry
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
is (non-)anomalous if and only if its faithful part
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
is (non-)anomalous.

Some examples of unfaithful action of
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
will be discussed in Section
VIII.4
.

V.2.2
Gapped interface modes

In this subsection, we study the necessary and sufficient conditions for the symmetry at the interface of the generalized cluster states to admit a non-degenerate gapped state.
Here, a state
\ket{\psi}
is said to be non-degenerate if it forms a singlet under the symmetry action, that is, it satisfies

\hat{\mathsf{L}}(g;h_{1},h_{2})\ket{\psi}\propto\ket{\psi},\qquad\forall g\in G,\penalty\ \forall h_{1},h_{2}\in H.

(V.63)

On the other hand, a state
\ket{\psi}
is said to be gapped if it can be written as an MPO with finite bond dimension.
42
42

42

This definition is based on the fact that gapped ground states in 1+1d can be written as MPS with finite bond dimension
Cirac:2020obd
;
FNW92
;
PhysRevB.73.094423
;
Hastings:2007iok
.

In what follows, we will show that the symmetry at the interface of
\ket{\text{cluster}(G;H;K_{1})}
and
\ket{\text{cluster}(G;H;K_{2})}
admits a non-degenerate gapped state if and only if there exists
h\in H
such that
K_{2}=hK_{1}h^{-1}
.
More precisely, we will show the following:

•

If there exists
h\in H
such that
K_{2}=hK_{1}h^{-1}
, the symmetry at the interface admits a non-degenerate product state, which is gapped.

•

If the symmetry at the interface admits a non-degenerate state, which is not necessarily gapped, there exists
h\in H
such that
K_{2}=hK_{1}h^{-1}
.

This result is consistent with the category theoretical description of the interface modes discussed in Section
V.1.2
.
43
43

43

From the category theoretical point of view, non-degenerate gapped interfaces should correspond to fiber functors of the strip 2-algebra
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
. In particular, when
K_{1}=K_{2}=K
, they would also correspond to invertible objects of the dual 2-category
\mathcal{C}(G;K)
under the conjectural monoidal equivalence (
V.45
). This correspondence suggests that the non-degenerate interfaces would be invertible in an appropriate sense. Indeed, the non-degenerate interface that we construct in (
V.64
) is invertible as an operator acting on the virtual bonds.

Let us first show the first bullet point.
To this end, we suppose that there exists
h\in H
such that
K_{2}=hK_{1}h^{-1}
.
Under this assumption, we show that the following product state is non-degenerate:

\ket{\psi(h)}=\bigotimes_{i:\text{sites}}\ket{\psi(h)}_{i},\qquad\ket{\psi(h)}_{i}:=\sum_{h_{\ell}\in H}\ket{h_{\ell},hh_{\ell}}_{i}.

(V.64)

We note that the product state
\ket{\psi(h)}
is gapped because it can be written as an MPO with bond dimension one.
A direct computation shows that the symmetry operators act on
\ket{\psi(h)}
as

\hat{\mathsf{L}}(g;h_{1},h_{2})\ket{\psi(h)}=\delta_{h,U^{-1}_{g;K_{2}}(h_{2})(U^{-1}_{g;K_{1}}(h_{1}))^{-1}}\ket{\psi(h_{2}h_{1}^{-1})}.

(V.65)

Due to the definition of
U_{g;K_{2}}
, there exists
k_{2}\in K_{2}
such that
h_{2}g=k_{2}U^{-1}_{g;K_{2}}(h_{2})
.
Furthermore, due to the assumption that
K_{2}=hK_{1}h^{-1}
, there exists
k_{1}\in K_{1}
such that
k_{2}=hk_{1}h^{-1}
.
Therefore, we find
h^{-1}h_{2}g=k_{1}h^{-1}U^{-1}_{g;K_{2}}(h_{2})
for some
k_{1}\in K_{1}
.
This implies

h^{-1}U^{-1}_{g;K_{2}}(h_{2})=U^{-1}_{g;K_{1}}(h^{-1}h_{2}).

(V.66)

Thus, the Kronecker delta in (
V.65
) can be written as

\delta_{h,U^{-1}_{g;K_{2}}(h_{2})(U^{-1}_{g;K_{1}}(h_{1}))^{-1}}=\delta_{U^{-1}_{g;K_{1}}(h_{1}),U^{-1}_{g;K_{1}}(h^{-1}h_{2})}=\delta_{h_{1},h^{-1}h_{2}}.

(V.67)

Plugging this into (
V.65
), we find

\hat{\mathsf{L}}(g;h_{1},h_{2})\ket{\psi(h)}=\delta_{h,h_{2}h_{1}^{-1}}\ket{\psi(h)}.

(V.68)

This shows that
\ket{\psi(h)}
is a non-degenerate gapped state at the interface.

Next, we show the second bullet point.
To this end, we first relabel the symmetry operators as

\hat{\mathcal{L}}(h_{1},h_{2},k_{1},k_{2}):=\hat{\mathsf{L}}(k_{1};e,h_{1}),\qquad\forall h_{1}\in H,\penalty\ \forall k_{1}\in K_{1},

(V.69)

where
h_{2}\in H
and
k_{2}\in K_{2}
are uniquely determined by
h_{1}\in H
and
k_{1}\in K_{1}
via

h_{1}k_{1}=k_{2}h_{2}.

(V.70)

We note that any symmetry operator is equal to the operator of the form (
V.69
) due to (
V.62
).
The fusion rules (
V.58
) then reduce to

\hat{\mathcal{L}}(h_{1},h_{2},k_{1},k_{2})\hat{\mathcal{L}}(h_{1}^{\prime},h_{2}^{\prime},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}^{\prime})=\delta_{h_{2},h_{1}^{\prime}}\hat{\mathcal{L}}(h_{1},h_{2}^{\prime},k_{1}k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}k_{2}^{\prime}),

(V.71)

which, due to (
V.60
), immediately follows from the fusion rules (
V.14
) of the strip 2-algebra
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
.
44
44

44

One can also verify the fusion rules (
V.71
) by a direct computation based on (
V.58
) and (
V.69
).

Now, we suppose that the symmetry at the interface admits a (non-zero) non-degenerate state
\ket{\psi}
, which is not necessarily gapped.
Since
\ket{\psi}
is non-degenerate, it satisfies

\hat{\mathcal{L}}(h_{1},h_{2},k_{1},k_{2})\ket{\psi}=\alpha(h_{1},k_{1})\ket{\psi},\qquad\forall h_{1}\in H,\penalty\ \forall k_{1}\in K_{1},

(V.72)

where
\alpha(h_{1},k_{1})\in\mathbb{C}
is a complex number.
We note that
\alpha
depends only on
h_{1}
and
k_{1}
because
h_{2}
and
k_{2}
are uniquely determined by
h_{1}
and
k_{1}
.
Due to the fusion rules (
V.71
),
\alpha
satisfies

\alpha(h_{1},k_{1})\alpha(h_{2},k_{1}^{-1})=\alpha(h_{1},e),\qquad\forall k_{1}\in K_{1},

(V.73)

where
h_{2}\in H
is determined by (
V.70
).
The above equation implies that
\alpha(h_{1},k_{1})
is non-zero for all
k_{1}\in K_{1}
if
\alpha(h_{1},e)
is non-zero.
On the other hand, the decomposition (
V.59
) of the identity operator implies that
\alpha
satisfies

\sum_{h_{1}\in H}\alpha(h_{1},e)=1,

(V.74)

which in turn implies that there exists
h_{1}\in H
such that
\alpha(h_{1},e)\neq 0
.
Thus, we find that there exists
h_{1}\in H
such that
\alpha(h_{1},k_{1})\neq 0
for any
k_{1}\in K_{1}
.
When
\alpha(h_{1},k_{1})
is non-zero, we have

\alpha(h_{1},k_{1})^{2}=\delta_{h_{1},h_{2}}\alpha(h_{1},k_{1}^{2})\neq 0,

(V.75)

where the first equality follows from the fusion rules (
V.71
).
The above equation shows that
h_{1}=h_{2}
