for all
k_{1}\in K_{1}
.
Recalling that
h_{2}\in H
is defined by (
V.70
), we find

h_{1}k_{1}h_{1}^{-1}=h_{1}k_{1}h_{2}^{-1}\in K_{2},\qquad\forall k_{1}\in K_{1},

(V.76)

which shows that
h_{1}K_{1}h_{1}^{-1}\subset K_{2}
.
Since
|K_{1}|=|K_{2}|
, it then follows that

h_{1}K_{1}h_{1}^{-1}=K_{2}.

(V.77)

This concludes the proof.

Notably, our proof of the second part relies only on the fusion rules (
V.71
) of the symmetry operators (
V.69
).
Therefore, as long as the symmetry operators at the interface obey the same fusion rules as (
V.71
), the proof can be applied to more general interfaces than (
V.51
).

Symmetry-enforced degeneracy.

As shown above, if there exists no element
h\in H
such that
K_{2}=hK_{1}h^{-1}
, any state at the interface of
\ket{\text{cluster}(G;H;K_{1})}
and
\ket{\text{cluster}(G;H;K_{2})}
cannot be non-degenerate under the symmetry.
This implies that the ground states in the presence of an interface must be degenerate unless the symmetry is explicitly broken.
In other words, the degeneracy is enforced by the symmetry.
Notably, this statement is irrespective of whether the interface is gapped or gapless.
For example, it follows that the boundary of the ordinary
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-cluster model must be degenerate unless the symmetry is explicitly broken.
This is consistent with the observation made in
Yoshida:2015cia
that the boundary of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-cluster model has a ferromagnetic order.
We emphasize that this type of symmetry-enforced degeneracy is stronger than the usual LSM-type constraint that allows a non-degenerate gapless state.
This stronger constraint originates from the fact that the strip 2-algebra
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
is not a fusion category but a multifusion category.
45
45

45

We refer the reader to
Chang:2018iay
for the first examples of the LSM-type constraint for fusion category symmetries. To the best of our knowledge, it is still open whether every fusion category can be realized as the symmetry of a 1+1d gapless system with a non-degenerate ground state. Nevertheless, many fusion categories are known to be realizable in non-degenerate gapless systems, including the Haagerup fusion categories
Huang:2021nvb
;
Vanhove:2021zop
(see also
Liu:2022qwn
;
Hung:2025gcp
;
Albert:2025umy
;
Bottini:2025hri
).

V.2.3
Example: gapped boundary of the
G
-cluster state

As an example, we consider the gapped interface between the
G_{0}
-cluster state (
III.55
) and the trivial product state with
2\text{Rep}(G_{0})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}
symmetry, where
G_{0}
is an arbitrary finite group.
To this end, we first recall that the input data of the
G_{0}
-cluster state and the trivial product state are given by

G=G_{0}\times G_{0},\qquad H=G_{0}^{\text{left}},\qquad K_{\text{cluster}}=G_{0}^{\text{diag}},\qquad K_{\text{triv}}=G_{0}^{\text{right}},\qquad S_{H\backslash G}=G_{0}^{\text{right}}.

(V.78)

For simplicity, we will focus on the interfaces represented by the tensor network of the form (
V.51
).
In what follows, an element
(g,e)\in H=G_{0}^{\text{left}}
will be written simply as
g\in G_{0}
.

Symmetry operators on the boundary.

According to the general discussions in Section
V.2.1
, the symmetry operators at the interface of the
G_{0}
-cluster state and the trivial product state can be written as

\hat{\mathsf{L}}(g_{\ell},g_{r};g_{1},g_{2})=\bigotimes_{i:\text{sites}}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}(g_{\ell},g_{r};g_{1},g_{2}),\qquad\forall g_{\ell},g_{r},g_{1},g_{2}\in G_{0},

(V.79)

where the tensor product is taken over all sites on the interface, and
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}(g_{\ell},g_{r};g_{1},g_{2})
is defined by

\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}(g_{\ell},g_{r};g_{1},g_{2})\coloneq\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(V.80)

Here,
\phi[V_{G_{0}^{\text{left}}}^{(g_{\ell},g_{r})}]_{g}
is the action tensor (
IV.72
) for the
G_{0}
-cluster state, and
\phi_{\text{triv}}[V_{G_{0}^{\text{left}}}^{(g_{\ell},g_{r})}]_{g}
is the action tensor for the trivial product state, which is given by
46
46

46

One can derive (
V.81
) by using the general formula (
IV.60
) for the action tensors.

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{g_{1}g_{3}^{-1},gg_{\ell}}\delta_{g_{1},g_{2}}.

(V.81)

By plugging (
IV.72
) and (
V.81
) into (
V.80
), we can compute the action of the symmetry operator at the interface as

\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}(g_{\ell},g_{r};g_{1},g_{2})\ket{h_{\ell},h_{r}}_{i}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{h_{r}h_{\ell}^{-1},g_{2}g_{1}^{-1}g_{r}}\ket{g_{r}h_{\ell},h_{r}}_{i},

(V.82)

where
h_{\ell},h_{r}\in G_{0}
.
Since the above operator depends only on
g_{r}
and
g_{2}g_{1}^{-1}
, we can choose
g_{\ell}=g_{1}=e
without loss of generality.
The independent symmetry operators can then be written as

\hat{\mathsf{L}}(g,g^{\prime})\coloneq\hat{\mathsf{L}}(e,g^{-1}g^{\prime};e,g),\qquad\forall g,g^{\prime}\in G_{0}.

(V.83)

A direct computation based on (
V.82
) shows that the redefined symmetry operators (
V.83
) obey the fusion rule

\hat{\mathsf{L}}(g_{1},g_{2})\hat{\mathsf{L}}(g_{1}^{\prime},g_{2}^{\prime})=\delta_{g_{2},g_{1}^{\prime}}\hat{\mathsf{L}}(g_{1},g_{2}^{\prime}).

(V.84)

This fusion rule agrees with the fusion rule (
V.49
) derived within the context of category theory.
In particular, the above fusion rule suggests that the symmetry at the interface is described by a multifusion category (
V.50
), that is,

\mathcal{C}_{K_{\text{cluster}},K_{\text{triv}}}\cong\text{Mat}_{|G_{0}|}(\text{Vec}).

(V.85)

Gapped boundary mode.

Since the
G_{0}
-cluster state and the trivial product state are in different SPT phases,
47
47

47

This is because there is no element
h\in H
such that
K_{\text{triv}}=hK_{\text{cluster}}h^{-1}
.
we cannot have a non-degenerate gapped state at the interface without explicitly breaking the symmetry.
Thus, any gapped states at the interface must be degenerate.
48
48

48

More stringently, as shown in Section
V.2.2
, any states at the interface must be degenerate, whether or not they are gapped.

Indeed, the product states (
V.64
) at the interface form a multiplet under the symmetry action, and hence they are degenerate.
More specifically, for each
h\in G_{0}
, we have the following product state

\ket{\psi(h)}=\bigotimes_{i:\text{sites}}\ket{\psi(h)}_{i},\qquad\ket{\psi(h)}_{i}:=\sum_{h_{\ell}\in G_{0}}\ket{h_{\ell},hh_{\ell}}_{i},

(V.86)

where the tensor product is taken over all sites on the interface, and the states
\{\ket{\psi(h)}\mid h\in G_{0}\}
are permuted by the action of the symmetry operators (
V.83
) as follows:

\hat{\mathsf{L}}(g,g^{\prime})\ket{\psi(h)}=\delta_{g^{\prime},h}\ket{\psi(g)}.

(V.87)

From the category-theoretical point of view, equation (
V.87
) suggests that the product state
\ket{\psi(h)}
corresponds to the unique simple object of each component of an indecomposable module category
\text{Vec}^{\oplus|G_{0}|}
over the symmetry category (
V.85
).

VI
Parameterized families of 2+1d generalized cluster states

In this section, we construct
S^{1}
-parameterized families of generalized cluster states and study the pumping phenomena of gapped interface modes.
Specifically, for each
h\in H
satisfying
hKh^{-1}=K
, we construct an
S^{1}
-parameterized family of
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
-symmetric states that are unitarily equivalent to the generalized cluster state
\ket{\text{cluster}(G;H;K)}
.
We show that the generalized Thouless pump associated with this family is given by the non-degenerate self-interface mode (
V.64
).
We also briefly discuss the relation to the conjectural classification of
S^{1}
-parameterized families based on category theory.

VI.1
S^{1}
-parameterized families

VI.1.1
Families of
G/K
-SSB models

We first construct
S^{1}
-parameterized families of
G/K
-SSB models.
The
S^{1}
-parameterized families of generalized cluster models are obtained by gauging the subgroup symmetry
H
in the
G/K
-SSB models.

Symmetry interpolation method.

In general, there are several ways to construct parameterized families of quantum systems on the lattice
PhysRevResearch.2.042024
;
Tantivasadakarn:2021wdv
;
Shiozaki:2021weu
;
Wen:2021gwc
.
In our construction, we employ the symmetry interpolation method, which we now explain briefly.
First, suppose that a Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
possesses a symmetry
U=\prod_{i}u_{i}
, that is

[\hat{H},U]=0.

(VI.1)

Here,
u_{i}
is a local unitary operator acting on a finite number of degrees of freedom around a single site
i
.
Since each
u_{i}
is unitary, there exists a continuous path
\{u_{i}(\theta)\mid 0\leq\theta\leq 2\pi\}
connecting it to the identity, that is
u_{i}(0)={\mathrm{id}}
and
u_{i}(2\pi)=u_{i}
.
Using this, we define
U(\theta)=\prod_{i}u_{i}(\theta)
and deform the Hamiltonian by conjugation with
U(\theta)
:
49
49

49

The Hamiltonian
\hat{H}(\theta)
after the conjugation is a sum of local terms as long as
H
is because
U(\theta)
is a finite-depth local unitary.

\hat{H}(\theta)=U(\theta)\hat{H}U(\theta)^{\dagger}.

(VI.2)

Then, due to the condition
[\hat{H},U]=0
, the Hamiltonian
\hat{H}(\theta)
becomes
2\pi
-periodic.
In this way, when the Hamiltonian has a (unitary) symmetry
U
, one can construct an
S^{1}
-parameterized family by interpolating between
U
and the identity.
Note that the Hamiltonian
\hat{H}(\theta)
generally does not possess the symmetry
U
except for
\theta=0,2\pi
.

For our purposes, we take
\hat{H}
to be the parent Hamiltonian (
III.3
) of the
G/K
-SSB states (
III.7
).
Moreover, to construct a
G
-symmetric parameterized family, we require that the interpolated operator
U(\theta)
commutes with the
G
symmetry for all
\theta\in[0,2\pi]
.
We note that
U
is not necessarily a symmetry operator for the
G
symmetry under consideration.

An
S^{1}
-parameterized family of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-SSB models.

As a warm-up, we first construct an
S^{1}
-parameterized family of the models where the symmetry
G=\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\{e,\eta\}
is spontaneously broken down to
K=\{e\}
.
To this end, we consider the following
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-SSB Hamiltonian of qubits on a square lattice:

\hat{H}_{\text{$\mathbb{Z}_{2}$-SSB}}=-\sum_{[ij]\in E}Z^{(i)}Z^{(j)}.

(VI.3)

The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry of this model is generated by
X=\bigotimes_{i\in P}X^{(i)}
.
The above Hamiltonian has twofold degenerate ground states
\bigotimes_{i\in P}\ket{e}_{i}
and
\bigotimes_{i\in P}\ket{\eta}_{i}
, where
\ket{e}_{i}
and
\ket{\eta}_{i}
are the eigenstates of
Z^{(i)}
with eigenvalues
+1
and
-1
, respectively.
These two ground states are exchanged by the symmetry operator
X
, and hence the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry is spontaneously broken.

The symmetry operator
X
satisfies all the properties required for
U
, i.e., it is a product of local unitaries and commutes with both the Hamiltonian and the symmetry operator.
Thus, one can construct a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-symmetric
S^{1}
-parameterized family by interpolating between
X
and the identity operator.
An example of an interpolation preserving the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry is

U(\theta)=\bigotimes_{i\in P}e^{i\frac{\theta}{4}(X^{(i)}-1)}.

(VI.4)

By conjugating the Hamiltonian (
VI.3
) with this
U(\theta)
, we can obtain an
S^{1}
-parameterized family of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-SSB models.

The
S^{1}
-parameterized family constructed in this way is non-trivial.
To see this, we consider the Hamiltonian (
VI.3
) conjugated by
U(\theta)
only on a subregion
P^{\prime}\subset P
of the square lattice, i.e.,

\hat{H}_{\text{$\mathbb{Z}_{2}$-SSB}}^{\text{text.}}(\theta)=U^{\prime}(\theta)\hat{H}_{\text{$\mathbb{Z}_{2}$-SSB}}U^{\prime}(\theta)^{\dagger},\qquad U^{\prime}(\theta)=\bigotimes_{i\in P^{\prime}}e^{i\frac{\theta}{4}(X^{(i)}-1)}.

(VI.5)

We will refer to this Hamiltonian as a textured Hamiltonian.
We note that the textured Hamiltonian is
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetric for all
\theta\in[0,2\pi]
, although it is not
2\pi
-periodic.
The ground states of the textured Hamiltonian are obtained by applying
U_{P^{\prime}}(\theta)
to the ground states of
\hat{H}_{\text{$\mathbb{Z}_{2}$-SSB}}
.
In particular, at
\theta=2\pi
, the ground states become the following domain wall configurations:

\displaystyle\left(\bigotimes_{i\in P\setminus P^{\prime}}\ket{e}_{i}\right)\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{i\in P^{\prime}}\ket{\eta}_{i}\right),\qquad\left(\bigotimes_{i\in P\setminus P^{\prime}}\ket{\eta}_{i}\right)\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{i\in P^{\prime}}\ket{e}_{i}\right).

(VI.6)

Therefore, adiabatically changing the parameter
\theta
from
0
to
2\pi
pumps a domain wall on the boundary of
P^{\prime}
.
This indicates that the
S^{1}
-parameterized family obtained above is non-trivial as a family of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-SSB models.

Domain wall creation operators in
G/K
-SSB model.

The above example suggests that one can construct a non-trivial
S^{1}
-parameterized family of
G/K
-SSB models for general
G
and
K
by taking
U
to be an operator that permutes the symmetry-breaking ground states.
Such an operator creates a domain wall when it acts on a ground state.
Thus, we refer to it as a domain wall creation operator.
As in the case of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-SSB models, we require that the domain wall creation operator commutes with the
G
symmetry so that the resulting family becomes
G
-symmetric.

To find the domain wall creation operators in a
G/K
-SSB model, we consider the Hamiltonian (
III.3
) of
G
-qudits on a square lattice, i.e.,

\hat{H}=-\sum_{i\in P}\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}-\sum_{[ij]\in E}\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij},

(VI.7)

where
\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}
and
\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}
are defined by

\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}\ket{g}_{i}=\frac{1}{|K|}\sum_{k\in K}\ket{g_{i}k}_{i},\qquad\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}\ket{g_{i}}_{i}\otimes\ket{g_{j}}_{j}=\delta_{g_{i}^{-1}g_{j}\in K}\ket{g_{i}}_{i}\otimes\ket{g_{j}}_{j}.

(VI.8)

This model possesses a
G
symmetry whose action is given by the left multiplication of
G
.
Namely, the Hamiltonian (
VI.7
) commutes with the following symmetry operators:

\overrightarrow{X}_{g}\coloneq\bigotimes_{g\in G}\overrightarrow{X}_{g}^{(i)},\qquad\forall g\in G.

(VI.9)

The ground states of the Hamiltonian (
VI.7
) are given by (
III.7
), which spontaneously break the symmetry
G
down to
K
.

One can show that the Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
commutes with the right multiplication of
h\in H
satisfying
hKh^{-1}=K
, that is,

[\hat{H},\overleftarrow{X}_{h}]=0,\qquad\forall h\in H\text{ s.t. }hKh^{-1}=K,

(VI.10)

where
\overleftarrow{X}_{h}\coloneq\bigotimes_{i\in P}\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}
.
The operator
\overleftarrow{X}_{h}
obviously commutes with the symmetry operators (
VI.9
) because it acts from the right, while the symmetry operators act from the left.
Furthermore, it permutes the
G/K
-SSB ground states (
III.7
).
Therefore, the right multiplication
\overleftarrow{X}_{h}
for
h\in H
satisfying
hKh^{-1}=K
is a domain wall creation operator preserving the
G
symmetry.

S^{1}
-parameterized families of
G/K
-SSB models.

Now, we construct an
S^{1}
-parameterized family of
G/K
-SSB models by interpolating the domain wall creation operator
\overleftarrow{X}_{h}
and the identity operator.
An example of an interpolation preserving the
G
symmetry is

U_{h}(\theta)=\bigotimes_{i\in P}u_{h}^{(i)}(\theta),\qquad u_{h}^{(i)}(\theta)\coloneq\exp{\left(\frac{\theta}{2\pi}\log\overleftarrow{X}^{(i)}_{h}\right)}.

(VI.11)

Here, we choose the principal branch of the logarithm as

\log\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}=\sum_{s=0}^{\left|H\right|-1}i\frac{2\pi s}{\left|H\right|}P_{s}[\overleftarrow{X}^{(i)}_{h}],

(VI.12)

where
P_{s}[\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}]
is the projection onto the eigenspace of
\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}
with eigenvalue
e^{i\frac{2\pi}{|H|}s}
.
50
50

50

We note that the eigenvalues of
\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}
are quantized to the
|H|
-th roots of unity because
(\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)})^{|H|}={\mathrm{id}}
.

This projection can be written explicitly as a polynomial

P_{s}[\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}]:=\frac{1}{\left|H\right|}\sum_{n=0}^{\left|H\right|-1}e^{-i\frac{2\pi s}{\left|H\right|}n}(\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)})^{n},

(VI.13)

which obeys the following relations:

P_{s}[\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}]P_{s^{\prime}}[\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}]=\delta_{s,s^{\prime}}P_{s}[\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}],\qquad P_{s}[\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}]^{\dagger}=P_{s}[\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}].

(VI.14)

We note that
u_{h}^{(i)}(\theta)
in (
VI.11
) acts as
e^{i\frac{\theta}{|H|}s}
on the eigenspace of
\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}
with eigenvalue
e^{i\frac{2\pi}{|H|}s}
.
In particular, it satisfies
u_{h}^{(i)}(0)={\mathrm{id}}
and
u_{h}^{(i)}(2\pi)=\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}
.
Furthermore, since
u_{h}^{(i)}(\theta)
is a polynomial of the right multiplication
\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}
, it automatically commutes with the symmetry operators (
VI.9
) that are given by the left multiplication of
G
.
Therefore, we can obtain an
S^{1}
-parameterized family of
G/K
-SSB models by conjugating the Hamiltonian (
VI.7
) with the above
U_{h}(\theta)
, i.e.,

\hat{H}_{h}(\theta)\coloneq U_{h}(\theta)\hat{H}U_{h}(\theta).

(VI.15)

The ground states of
\hat{H}_{h}(\theta)
are obtained by applying
U_{h}(\theta)
to the ground states (
III.7
) of
\hat{H}
.

We can see the non-triviality of the
S^{1}
-parameterized family (
VI.15
) by using the following textured Hamiltoian:

\hat{H}_{h}^{\text{text.}}(\theta)\coloneq U_{h}^{\prime}(\theta)\hat{H}U_{h}^{\prime}(\theta)^{\dagger},\qquad U_{h}^{\prime}(\theta)\coloneq\bigotimes_{i\in P^{\prime}}u_{h}^{(i)}(\theta).

(VI.16)

Here,
P^{\prime}\subset P
is a subregion of the square lattice.
By construction, if we change
\theta
adiabatically from
0
to
2\pi
in the textured Hamiltonian (
VI.16
), the ground states aquire a non-trivial domain wall along the boundary of
P^{\prime}
.
This indicates that the
S^{1}
-parameterized family constructed above is non-trivial.

VI.1.2
Families of generalized cluster models

We now construct
S^{1}
-parameterized families of generalized cluster models with
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
symmetry.
This is achieved by gauging the subgroup symmetry
H
in the models defined in the previous subsection.
Unless otherwise stated, in this subsection, the gauged model refers to the model after the gauge fixing.

Dual of the domain wall creation operator.

We first define the dual of the domain wall creation operator, which we will use later to write down the Hamiltonian of the gauged model.
The dual of the domain wall creation operator
\overleftarrow{X}_{h}
is defined by

\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{h}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}=\bigotimes_{i\in P}\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.f.}},

(VI.17)

where
\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}
is the local operator obtained by transforming
\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}
with the gauging operator (
IV.15
):
51
51

51

In general, for a local operator
\mathcal{O}
, an operator
\mathcal{O}|_{\text{g.f.}}
satisfying
\mathcal{O}|_{\text{g.f.}}\mathsf{D}_{H}=\mathsf{D}_{H}\mathcal{O}
is not unique: it is unique only up to operators that act as the identity on the image of
\mathsf{D}_{H}
. However, the
local
operator
\mathcal{O}|_{\text{g.f.}}
satisfying
\mathcal{O}|_{\text{g.f.}}\mathsf{D}_{H}=\mathsf{D}_{H}\mathcal{O}
is unique if it exists, because there is no non-trivial local operator that acts as the identity on the image of
\mathsf{D}_{H}
.

\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}\mathsf{D}_{H}=\mathsf{D}_{H}\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}.

(VI.18)

By definition, we have
\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{h}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}\mathsf{D}_{H}=\mathsf{D}_{H}\overleftarrow{X}_{h}
.
As we will see shortly, the explicit form of
\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}
is given by

\left.\overleftarrow{X}^{(i)}_{h}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}\Ket{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}}=\Ket{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}},

(VI.19)

where
k^{\prime}:=h^{-1}kh
and
h^{\prime}:=h[k]^{-1}hh[k^{\prime}]
.
We note that
k^{\prime}
is an element of
K
because
h\in H
satisfies
K=hKh^{-1}
.
The above operator can be written more concisely as

\left.\overleftarrow{X}^{(i)}_{h}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}=\sum_{k\in K}L^{(\partial i)}_{h[h^{-1}kh]^{-1}h^{-1}h[k]}\otimes\ket{\mathfrak{r}[h^{-1}kh]}\bra{\mathfrak{r}[k]},

(VI.20)

where
L^{(\partial i)}_{h[h^{-1}kh]^{-1}h^{-1}h[k]}
is the loop operator defined in (
III.12
).

Let us now verify that
\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}
in (
VI.20
) satisfies the defining equation (
VI.18
).
To this end, we use the tensor network representation (
IV.19
) of the gauging operator
\mathsf{D}_{H}
.
To show (
VI.18
), it suffices to focus on the local tensors of
\mathsf{D}_{H}
around the plaquette
i\in P
.
For the local tensor acting on the matter field, we have

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$}\;\;=\;\;\sum_{k\in K}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$},

(VI.21)

where
w_{h}[k]:=\ket{\mathfrak{r}[h^{-1}kh]}\bra{\mathfrak{r}[k]}
and
h^{\prime}=h[k]^{-1}hh[h^{-1}kh]
.
One can show the above equation by comparing the tensors on both sides while fixing the label of the bottom (physical) leg to
h_{0}\mathfrak{r}[k_{0}]
for any
h_{0}\in H
and
k_{0}\in K
.
On the other hand, for the local tensor acting on the gauge field, we have

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$},\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$},

(VI.22)

which immediately follows from the definition of the local tensor.
By combining (
VI.21
) and (
VI.22
), we find that
\overleftarrow{X}^{(i)}_{h}
is transformed into
\left.\overleftarrow{X}^{(i)}_{h}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}
under the action of the gauging operator as in (
VI.18
).

S^{1}
-parameterized families of generalized cluster models.

An
S^{1}
-parameterized family of generalized cluster models with
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
symmetry is obtained by gauging the subgroup symmetry
H
in the
G/K
-SSB models (
VI.15
).
The gauged Hamiltonian
\hat{H}^{h}_{\text{g.f.}}(\theta)
is related to the Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{h}(\theta)
of the ungauged model as

\hat{H}^{h}_{\text{g.f.}}(\theta)\mathsf{D}_{H}=\mathsf{D}_{H}\hat{H}_{h}(\theta).

(VI.23)

The local Hamiltonian
\hat{H}^{h}_{\text{g.f.}}(\theta)
satisfying the above equation is uniquely given by

\hat{H}^{h}_{\text{g.f.}}(\theta)=U^{h}_{\text{g.f.}}(\theta)\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}U^{h}_{\text{g.f.}}(\theta)^{\dagger},

(VI.24)

where
\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}
is the Hamiltonian (
III.36
) of the generalized cluster model, and
U^{h}_{\text{g.f.}}(\theta)
is the dual of
U_{h}(\theta)
satisfying
U^{h}_{\text{g.f.}}(\theta)\mathsf{D}_{H}=\mathsf{D}_{H}U_{h}(\theta)
.
An explicit form of
U^{h}_{\text{g.f.}}(\theta)
is given by

U^{h}_{\text{g.f.}}(\theta)=\prod_{i\in P}\left.u_{h}^{(i)}(\theta)\right|_{\text{g.f.}},\qquad\left.u_{h}^{(i)}(\theta)\right|_{\text{g.f.}}\coloneq\exp\left(\frac{\theta}{2\pi}\log\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}\right).

(VI.25)

Here, we again choose the principal branch of the logarithm as

\log\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}=\sum_{s=0}^{\left|H\right|-1}i\frac{2\pi s}{\left|H\right|}P_{s}[\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}],

(VI.26)

where
P_{s}[\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}]
is the projection onto the eigenspace of
\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}
with eigenvalue
e^{i\frac{2\pi}{\left|H\right|}s}
.
52
52

52

The eigenvalues of
\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}
are quantized to the
|H|
-th roots of unity because
(\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.f.}})^{|H|}={\mathrm{id}}
.

The projection
P_{s}[\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}]
can be written as a polynomial of
\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}
as

P_{s}[\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.s.}}]=\frac{1}{\left|H\right|}\sum_{n=0}^{\left|H\right|-1}e^{-i\frac{2\pi s}{\left|H\right|}n}(\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.f.}})^{n}.

(VI.27)

In particular,
\left.u_{h}^{(i)}(\theta)\right|_{\text{g.f.}}
in (
VI.25
) is also a polynomial of
\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}
, and hence it follows from (
VI.18
) that

\left.u_{h}^{(i)}(\theta)\right|_{\text{g.f.}}\mathsf{D}_{H}=\mathsf{D}_{H}u_{h}^{(i)}(\theta),\qquad U^{h}_{\text{g.f.}}(\theta)\mathsf{D}_{H}=\mathsf{D}_{H}U_{h}(\theta).

(VI.28)

Equations (
VI.28
) and (
IV.11
) imply that the gauged Hamiltonian (
VI.24
) satisfies (
VI.23
).
We note that
\hat{H}^{h}_{\text{g.f.}}(\theta)
is
2\pi
-periodic because
U^{h}_{\text{g.f.}}(2\pi)=\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{h}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}
commutes with
\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}
by construction.

Tensor network representation of the ground state.

The ground state of the
\theta
-dependent Hamiltonian (
VI.24
) is obtained by applying the unitary operator
U^{h}_{\text{g.f.}}(\theta)
to the ground state of
\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}
, which is the generalized cluster state (
III.45
).
Furthermore, as shown in (
IV.18
), the generalized cluster state is obtained by applying the gauging operator
\mathsf{D}_{H}
to a
G/K
-SSB ground state
\bigotimes_{i\in P}\ket{K}_{i}
, where
\ket{K}_{i}\coloneq\frac{1}{\sqrt{|K|}}\sum_{k\in K}\ket{k}_{i}
.
Therefore, the ground state of
\hat{H}^{h}_{\text{g.f.}}(\theta)
in (
VI.24
) can be written as

U^{h}_{\text{g.f.}}(\theta)\ket{\text{cluster}(G;H;K)}=U^{h}_{\text{g.f.}}(\theta)\mathsf{D}_{H}\left(\bigotimes_{i\in P}\ket{K}_{i}\right)=\mathsf{D}_{H}\left(\bigotimes_{i\in P}u_{h}^{(i)}(\theta)\ket{K}_{i}\right),

(VI.29)

where the second equality follows from the duality relation (
VI.28
).
A tensor network representation of this ground state is given by

U^{h}_{\text{g.f.}}(\theta)\ket{\text{cluster}(G;H;K)}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$},

(VI.30)

where the white dot represents the multiplication tensor defined in (
III.47
), and the orange dot represents the following tensor:

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$}\;\;\coloneq\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$}.

(VI.31)

Here, the six-leg tensor on the right-hand side is the local tensor of
\mathsf{D}_{H}
, which is defined in (
IV.20
).

VI.2
Pumping of gapped interface modes

In this subsection, we study the generalized Thouless pumps associated with the
S^{1}
-parameterized families of
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
-symmetric models constructed in the previous subsection.
For simplicity, throughout this subsection, we suppose that our lattice models are defined on an infinite plane.
53
53

53

This is not an essential assumption: one can consider the generalized Thouless pumps on the lattice with periodic boundary conditions in the same way.

Generalized Thouless pump.

We first define the generalized Thouless pump associated with the
S^{1}
-parameterized family of
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
-symmetric Hamiltonians (
VI.24
).
To this end, we consider a
\theta
-dependend textured Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}^{h;\text{text.}}(\theta)
that satisfy the following properties:

•

On the left half of the plane, the local terms of
\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}^{h;\text{text.}}(\theta)
agree with those of
\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}^{h}(0)=\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}
.

•

On the right half of the plane, the local terms of
\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}^{h;\text{text.}}(\theta)
agree with those of
\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}^{h}(\theta)
.

•

Around the middle line at
x=0
, the local terms of
\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}^{h;\text{text.}}(\theta)
are arbitrary as long as the following conditions hold:

–

\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}^{h;\text{text.}}(\theta)
is continuous in
\theta\in[0,2\pi]
with the initial condition
\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}^{h;\text{text.}}(0)=\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}
.

–

\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}^{h;\text{text.}}(\theta)
preserves the symmetry
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
for all
\theta\in[0,2\pi]
.

–

\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}^{h;\text{text.}}(\theta)
has a unique gapped ground state for all
\theta\in[0,2\pi]
.

We note that
\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}^{h;\text{text.}}(\theta)
is not necessarily
2\pi
-periodic because the local terms around
x=0
are not.
Now, to define the generalized Thouless pump, we consider changing
\theta
adiabatically from
0
to
2\pi
.
Since the textured Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}^{h;\text{text.}}(\theta)
is not
2\pi
-periodic, its ground state does not generally go back to the initial state at the end of the adiabatic cycle.
Nevertheless, the ground states at
\theta=0
and
\theta=2\pi
differ only around
x=0
because
\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}^{h;\text{text.}}(0)
and
\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}^{h;\text{text.}}(2\pi)
differ only around
x=0
.
This means that the adiabatic evolution pumps a string-like excitation, which we refer to as the generalized Thouless pump.
The pumped excitation must be non-degenerate under the symmetry action because the ground state of the textured Hamiltonian is unique and hence preserves the symmetry.

In what follows, we take the
\theta
-dependent textured Hamiltonian to be

\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}^{h;\text{text.}}(\theta)\coloneq U_{\text{g.f.}}^{h;x>0}(\theta)\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}U_{\text{g.f.}}^{h;x>0}(\theta)^{\dagger},

(VI.32)

where
U_{\text{g.f.}}^{h;x>0}(\theta)
is the unitary operator
U_{\text{g.f.}}^{h}(\theta)
restricted to the right half of the plane, i.e.,

U_{\text{g.f.}}^{h;x>0}(\theta)\coloneq\prod_{i\in P_{>0}}\left.u_{h}^{(i)}(\theta)\right|_{\text{g.f.}}.

(VI.33)

Here,
P_{>0}
is the set of the plaquettes whose
x
-coordinates are greater than 0, and
\left.u_{h}^{(i)}(\theta)\right|_{\text{g.f.}}
is the local unitary operator defined in (
VI.25
).
We note that the textured Hamiltonian (
VI.32
) satisfies all the properties listed in the previous paragraph.
In particular, this Hamiltonian preserves the symmetry
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
for all
\theta\in[0,2\pi]
because it is obtained by gauging the subgroup symmetry
H
in the
G
-symmetric textured Hamiltonian (
VI.16
).
54
54

54

The subregion
P^{\prime}
in (
VI.16
) is chosen to be
P_{>0}
here.

The ground state of the Hamiltonian (
VI.32
) is obtained by applying
U_{\text{g.f.}}^{h;x>0}(\theta)
to the ground state of
\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}
, that is,

U_{\text{g.f.}}^{h;x>0}(\theta)\ket{\text{cluster}(G;H;K)}.

(VI.34)

The generalized Thouless pump can then be understood as the gapped excitation localized around
x=0
in the ground state at
\theta=2\pi
.
In the remainder of this subsection, we will show that this gapped excitation is given by the non-degenerate self-interface mode (
V.64
).

Tensor network representation.

To explicitly write down the gapped excitation localized around
x=0
, we use the following tensor network representation of the ground state (
VI.34
):

U_{\text{g.f.}}^{h;x>0}(\theta)\ket{\text{cluster}(G;H;K)}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$}.

(VI.35)

Here, the local tensors on the right-hand side are defined by (
III.47
) and (
VI.31
).
As is clear from the above tensor network representation, the left half of the plane is occupied by the ground state of
\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}
, while the right half of the plane is occupied by the ground state of
\hat{H}_{\text{g.f.}}^{h}(\theta)
.

To identify the pumped excitation, we look into the ground state (
VI.35
) at
\theta=2\pi
.
At
\theta=2\pi
, the local tensor at each vertex on the right half-plane reduces to

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$}\;=\;\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$}\;=\;\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$}\;=\;\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$}\;=\;\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$}.

(VI.36)

In the second equality, we used
\overleftarrow{X}_{h}\ket{K}=\overrightarrow{X}_{h}\ket{K}
, which follows from the condition
K=hKh^{-1}
.
On the other hand, the local tensor on each link (i.e., the multiplication tensor) commutes with the left multiplication
\overrightarrow{X}_{h}
acting on the virtual bonds.
Therefore, the PEPS tensor on each unit cell at
\theta=2\pi
is related to the one at
\theta=0
by the following gauge transformation:

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$}\;\;=\;\;\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$}\,.

(VI.37)

By plugging this into (
VI.35
), we find that changing
\theta
adiabatically from
0
to
2\pi
pumps a gapped excitation along the
y
-axis as follows:

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$}\longrightarrow\;\;\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$}

The pumped excitation is exactly the non-degenerate gapped self-interface mode
\ket{\psi(h^{-1})}
defined in (
V.64
).

Category-theoretical interpretation.

Let us comment on the relation to the category-theoretical classification of
S^{1}
-parameterized families.
From the category-theoretical viewpoint, it is expected that
S^{1}
-parameterized families of 2+1d gapped systems in an SPT phase are classified by monoidal natural automorphisms of the corresponding fiber 2-functor
Inamura:2024jke
.
In particular, for the
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
-symmetric SPT phase labeled by
K
, such monoidal natural automorphisms are in one-to-one correspondence with fiber functors of the strip 2-algebra
\mathcal{C}_{K,K}
due to the equivalence (
V.43
).
Physically, the fiber functors of
\mathcal{C}_{K,K}
should be interpreted as the gapped exciations pumped by the adiabatic evolution.
As shown in (
V.37
), the isomorphism classes of the fiber functors of
\mathcal{C}_{K,K}
are classified by

\{(h,[\xi])\mid h\in H\text{ s.t. }K=hKh^{-1},\penalty\ [\xi]\in H^{2}(K,\mathrm{U}(1))\}.

(VI.38)

On the other hand, in the lattice construction discussed above, we realized the generalized Thouless pump labeled by any
h\in H
satisfying
K=hKh^{-1}
.
Since the pumped interface mode (
V.64
) is a product state, the corresponding fiber functor labeled by
(h,[\xi])
should have trivial
[\xi]
.
Thus, we conclude that among the
S^{1}
-parameterized families classified by (
VI.38
), we constructed all those with trivial
[\xi]
.

VI.3
Example:
S^{1}
-parameterized families of the
G
-cluster states

As a simple example, let us consider
S^{1}
-parameterized families of
2\text{Rep}(G_{0})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}
-symmetric models in the same phase as the
G_{0}
-cluster model (
III.52
).
Recall that the
G_{0}
-cluster model is a special case of the generalized cluster model with

G=G_{0}\times G_{0},\quad H=G_{0}^{\text{left}},\quad K=G_{0}^{\text{diag}},\quad S_{H\backslash G}=G_{0}^{\text{right}}.

(VI.39)

S^{1}
-parameterized families.

As shown in Section
VI.1
, we can construct an
S^{1}
-parameterized family of generalized cluster states for each
h\in H
satisfying
K=hKh^{-1}
.
In the case of the
G_{0}
-cluster model, since
H=G_{0}^{\text{left}}
and
K=G_{0}^{\text{diag}}
, the condition
K=hKh^{-1}
reduces to

h\in\{(g,e)\mid g\in Z(G_{0})\}=Z(G_{0}^{\text{left}}),

(VI.40)

where
Z(G_{0})
is the center of
G_{0}
.
For each
h\in Z(G_{0}^{\text{left}})
, the Hamiltonian of an
S^{1}
-parameterized family is obtained by conjugating the
G_{0}
-cluster Hamilonian (
III.52
) with the following unitary operator parameterized by
\theta\in[0,2\pi]
:

U_{\text{g.f.}}^{h}(\theta)\coloneq\prod_{i\in P}\left.u_{h}^{(i)}(\theta)\right|_{\text{g.f.}},\qquad\left.u_{h}^{(i)}(\theta)\right|_{\text{g.f.}}\coloneq\exp\left(\frac{\theta}{2\pi}\log L_{h^{-1}}^{(\partial i)}\right).

(VI.41)

Here, we recall that
L_{h^{-1}}^{(\partial i)}
is the loop operator defined in (
III.12
), which acts only on the gauge fields around the plaquette
i
.
We choose the branch of the logarithm as

\log L_{h^{-1}}^{(\partial i)}=\sum_{s=0}^{\left|G_{0}\right|-1}i\frac{2\pi}{|G_{0}|}P_{s}[L_{h^{-1}}^{(\partial i)}],

(VI.42)

where
P_{s}[L_{h^{-1}}^{(\partial i)}]
is the projection operator onto the eigenspace of
L_{h^{-1}}^{(\partial i)}
with eigenvalue
e^{i\frac{2\pi s}{|G_{0}|}}
.
The above operator (
VI.41
) is obtained from the general formula (
VI.25
) by using the fact that the dual of the domain wall creation operator on each plaquette
i
is now given by

\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}=L_{h^{-1}}^{(\partial i)}.

(VI.43)

We note that
\left.u_{h}^{(i)}(\theta)\right|_{\text{g.f.}}
in (
VI.41
) interpolates between the identity operator and the loop operator
L_{h^{-1}}^{(\partial i)}
.
In particular, on a square lattice with periodic boundary conditions, the unitary operator (
VI.41
) is
2\pi
-periodic because
55
55

55

The
2\pi
-periodicity of
U_{\text{g.f.}}^{h}(\theta)
is a special property of the
G_{0}
-cluster model: in general, the unitary operator
U_{\text{g.f.}}^{h}(\theta)
defined by (
VI.25
) is not necessarily
2\pi
-periodic. Nevertheless, the conjugated Hamiltonian (
VI.24
) is
2\pi
-periodic.

U_{\text{g.f.}}^{h}(2\pi)=\prod_{i\in P}L_{h^{-1}}^{(\partial i)}=\prod_{[ij]\in E}\overrightarrow{X}_{h^{-1}}^{(ij)}\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(ij)}={\mathrm{id}},

(VI.44)

where the last equality follows from the fact that
h
is in the center of
G_{0}^{\text{left}}
.
Therefore, the
G_{0}
-cluster Hamiltonian conjugated by
U_{\text{g.f.}}^{h}(\theta)
is also
2\pi
-periodic, which means that the model constructed in this way is parameterized by a circle
S^{1}
.

The unitary operator
U_{\text{g.f.}}^{h}(\theta)
commutes with the
2\text{Rep}(G_{0})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}
symmetry, which implies that the
S^{1}
-family obtained by the conjugation with
U_{\text{g.f.}}^{h}(\theta)
preserves the symmetry.
To see this, we first recall that each local unitary
\left.u_{h}^{(i)}(\theta)\right|_{\text{g.f.}}
is a polynomial of
L_{h^{-1}}^{(\partial i)}
because
P_{s}[L_{h^{-1}}^{(\partial i)}]
can be written as a polynomical

P_{s}[L_{h^{-1}}^{(\partial i)}]=\frac{1}{|G_{0}|}\sum_{n=0}^{\left|G_{0}\right|-1}e^{-i\frac{2\pi s}{\left|G_{0}\right|}n}(L_{h^{-1}}^{(\partial i)})^{n}.

(VI.45)

Thus, it suffices to show that
L_{h^{-1}}^{(\partial i)}
commutes with the
2\text{Rep}(G_{0})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}
symmetry.
The commutativity of
L_{h^{-1}}^{(\partial i)}
and the
2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}
symmetry is obvious because
L_{h^{-1}}^{(\partial i)}
acts only on the gauge fields on the edges, while the
2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}
symmetry acts only on the matter fields on the plaquettes.
Furthermore, since
L_{h^{-1}}^{(\partial i)}
does not change the holonomy of the gauge fields (up to conjugation), it also commutes with the 1-form symmetry
\text{Rep}(G_{0})
and hence commutes with its condensation completion
2\text{Rep}(G_{0})
.

Generalized Thouless pumps.

Let us now discuss the generalized Thouless pump associated with the above
S^{1}
-parameterized family.
To this end, we consider the
\theta
-dependent textured Hamiltonian obtained by conjugating the
G_{0}
-cluster Hamiltonian (
III.52
) with the truncated unitary operator

U_{\text{g.f.}}^{h;x>0}\coloneq\prod_{i\in P_{>0}}\left.u_{h}^{(i)}(\theta)\right|_{\text{g.f.}}.

(VI.46)

Here,
P_{>0}
is the set of plaquettes on the right half of the plane.
56
56

56

Here, for simplicity, we consider the model on an infinite plane.

We note that the
\theta
-dependent textured Hamiltonian preserves the
2\text{Rep}(G_{0})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}
symmetry for all
\theta\in[0,2\pi]
.
By construction, the ground state of the textured Hamiltonian is given by

U_{\text{g.f.}}^{h;x>0}(\theta)\ket{\text{$G_{0}$-cluster}}.

(VI.47)

At
\theta=2\pi
, the truncated unitary operator (
VI.46
) reduces to a line operator along the
y
-axis, i.e.,

U_{\text{g.f.}}^{h;x>0}(2\pi)=\prod_{i\in P_{>0}}L_{h^{-1}}^{(\partial i)}=\prod_{e\in E|_{x=0}}\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(e)},

(VI.48)

where
E|_{x=0}
is the set of edges on the line with
x=0
.
Thus, the ground state (
VI.47
) at
\theta=2\pi
becomes

U_{\text{g.f.}}^{h;x>0}(2\pi)\ket{\text{$G_{0}$-cluster}}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$},

(VI.49)

where the black and white dots represent the local tensors (
III.57
) of the
G_{0}
-cluster state.
Since
h
is in the center of
G_{0}^{\text{left}}
, there is no difference between the left and right multiplications of
h
, that is,
\overleftarrow{X}_{h}^{(e)}=\overrightarrow{X}_{h}^{(e)}
for any edge
e\in E
.
Accordingly,
\overleftarrow{X}_{h}
acting on the physical legs in (
VI.49
) can freely pass through the multiplication tensor (i.e., the white dot) as follows:

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$}\;\;=\;\;\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$}\;\;=\;\;\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$}\;\;=\;\;\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$}.

(VI.50)

Plugging this into (
VI.49
), we find that the gapped excitation pumped by an adiabatic cycle is represented by the left multiplication of
h
acting on the virtual degrees of freedom at the interface.
This result agrees with the general result obtained in Section
VI.2
.

VII
Example 1:
2\text{Rep}(G_{0})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}
SPT phases

As our first examples, we consider 2+1d SPT phases with
2\text{Rep}(G_{0})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}
symmetry, where
G_{0}
is an arbitrary finite group.
We note that
2\text{Rep}(G_{0})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}
is a group-theoretical fusion 2-category because

2\text{Rep}(G_{0})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}\cong\mathcal{C}(G_{0}\times G_{0};G_{0}^{\text{left}}),

(VII.1)

where
G_{0}^{\text{left}}:=\{(g,e)\mid g\in G_{0}\}
.
The examples that we will discuss below include the SPT phase realized by the
G_{0}
-cluster state (
III.55
).

VII.1
Classification

We first provide the classification of SPT phases with
2\text{Rep}(G_{0})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}
symmetry.
These SPT phases are in one-to-one correspondence with fiber 2-functors of
2\text{Rep}(G_{0})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}
.
Due to the monoidal equivalence (
VII.1
), fiber 2-functors of
2\text{Rep}(G_{0})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}
are labeled by pairs
(K,\lambda)
, where
K
is a complement of
G_{0}^{\text{left}}
in
G_{0}\times G_{0}
, and
\lambda\in Z^{3}(K,\mathrm{U}(1))
is a 3-cocycle on
K
.
See Section
II.3
for the classification of fiber 2-functors of a general group-theoretical fusion 2-category.
By definition, the complement
K
satisfies

G_{0}^{\text{left}}K=G_{0}\times G_{0},\qquad G_{0}^{\text{left}}\cap K=\{(e,e)\}.

(VII.2)

The first equality implies that
K
must contain an element
(\bullet,g)
for any
g\in G_{0}
, where
\bullet
is an element of
G_{0}
.
Furthermore, the second equality implies that the element of the form
(\bullet,g)\in K
must be unique for every
g\in G_{0}
.
57
57

57

If
K
contains two elements
(g_{1},g)
and
(g_{2},g)
with
g_{1}\neq g_{2}
, it also contains
(g_{1},g)^{-1}\cdot(g_{2},g)=(g_{1}^{-1}g_{2},e)
because
K
is a group. This implies that
G_{0}^{\text{left}}\cap K
contains
(g_{1}^{-1}g_{2},e)\neq(e,e)
, which contradicts the second equality of (
VII.2
).

Therefore, the complement
K
must be of the form

K=G_{0}^{\text{diag}}(f):=\{(f(g),g)\mid g\in G_{0}\},

(VII.3)

where
f:G_{0}\to G_{0}
is a map.
Moreover, since
K
is a subgroup,
f
must satisfy

(f(g),g)\cdot(f(h),h)\in K,\qquad\forall g,h\in G_{0}.

(VII.4)

This condition implies that
f:G_{0}\to G_{0}
must be a group homomorphism.
Conversely, it is clear that
G_{0}^{\text{diag}}(f)
is a complement of
G_{0}^{\text{left}}
if
f
is a group homomorphism of
G_{0}
.
Therefore, a fiber 2-functor of
2\text{Rep}(G_{0})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}
is labeled by the pair
(f,\lambda)
, where
f
is a group homomorphism of
G_{0}
, and
\lambda
is a 3-cocycle on
G_{0}^{\text{diag}}(f)
.
In what follows, we will focus on fiber 2-functors with trivial
\lambda
.
In this case, each fiber 2-functor is labeled only by a group homomorphism
f:G_{0}\to G_{0}
.

The fiber 2-functors labeled by
f
and
f^{\prime}
are isomorphic to each other if and only if there exists an element
(g_{\ell},g_{r})\in G_{0}\times G_{0}
such that

G_{0}^{\text{diag}}(f^{\prime})=(g_{\ell},g_{r})G_{0}^{\text{diag}}(f)(g_{\ell},g_{r})^{-1}.

(VII.5)

Since
G_{0}^{\text{diag}}(f)
is a complement of
G_{0}^{\text{left}}
, we can take
(g_{\ell},g_{r})
to be an element of
G_{0}^{\text{left}}
without loss of generality.
Namely, we can choose
g_{r}=e
.
In this case, the above condition reduces to

\forall g\in G_{0},\penalty\ \exists h\in G_{0}\penalty\ \text{ s.t. }\penalty\ (f^{\prime}(g),g)=(g_{\ell}f(h)g_{\ell}^{-1},h).

(VII.6)

This condition is satisfied if and only if

f^{\prime}=\text{Ad}_{g_{\ell}}\circ f,

(VII.7)

where
\text{Ad}_{g_{\ell}}:G_{0}\to G_{0}
denotes the adjoint action of
g_{\ell}\in G_{0}
.
Thus, the fiber 2-functors labeled by
f
and
f^{\prime}
are isomorphic to each other if and only if there exists
g_{\ell}\in G_{0}
that satisfies (
VII.7
).
This shows that the isomorphism classes of fiber 2-functors with trivial
\lambda
are classified by

\text{End}(G_{0})/\text{Inn}(G_{0}),

(VII.8)

where
\text{End}(G_{0})
denotes the set of group endomorphisms of
G_{0}
, and
\text{Inn}(G_{0})
is the group of inner automorphisms of
G_{0}
.

VII.2
Generalized cluster states

The SPT phases labeled by fiber 2-functors with trivial
\lambda
are realized in the generalized cluster models defined in Section
III
.
In the case of
2\text{Rep}(G_{0})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}
symmetry, the input data of the model are

G=G_{0}\times G_{0},\quad H=G_{0}^{\text{left}},\quad K=G_{0}^{\text{diag}}(f).

(VII.9)

The generalized cluster model for the above input data is obtained by gauging the subgroup symmetry
H
in a
G
-symmetric model that spontaneously breaks the symmetry
G
down to
K
.
In what follows, we will write down the models both before and after the gauging.

G/K
-SSB model.

The state space of the
G
-symmetric model before the gauging is

\mathcal{H}=\bigotimes_{i\in P}\mathbb{C}^{|G_{0}\times G_{0}|},

(VII.10)

where
P
is the set of all plaquettes on a square lattice.
The on-site state space on each plaquette
i\in P
is spanned by
\{\ket{g_{i},g_{i}^{\prime}}_{i}\mid g_{i},g^{\prime}_{i}\in G_{0}\}
.
The Hamiltonian of the model is

H=-\sum_{i\in P}\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}-\sum_{[ij]\in E}\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij},

(VII.11)

where
\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}
and
\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}
are defined by

\displaystyle\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}\ket{g_{i},g_{i}^{\prime}}_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|G_{0}|}\sum_{g\in G_{0}}\ket{g_{i}f(g),g_{i}^{\prime}g},

(VII.12)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}\ket{g_{i},g_{i}^{\prime}}_{i}\otimes\ket{g_{j},g_{j}^{\prime}}_{j}

\displaystyle=\delta_{g_{i}^{-1}g_{j},f(g_{i}^{\prime})^{-1}f(g_{j}^{\prime})}\ket{g_{i},g_{i}^{\prime}}_{i}\otimes\ket{g_{j},g_{j}^{\prime}}_{j}.

(VII.13)

The symmetry of this model is generated by

U_{g}^{(L)}=\bigotimes_{i\in P}\overrightarrow{X}_{g}^{(i;L)},\qquad U_{g}^{(R)}=\bigotimes_{i\in P}\overrightarrow{X}_{g}^{(i;R)},\qquad\forall g\in G_{0},

(VII.14)

where the on-site operators
\overrightarrow{X}_{g}^{(i;L)}
and
\overrightarrow{X}_{g}^{(i;R)}
are defined by

\overrightarrow{X}_{g}^{(i;L)}\ket{g_{i},g_{i}^{\prime}}_{i}=\ket{gg_{i},g_{i}^{\prime}},\qquad\overrightarrow{X}_{g}^{(i;R)}\ket{g_{i},g_{i}^{\prime}}_{i}=\ket{g_{i},gg_{i}^{\prime}}.

(VII.15)

The Hamiltonian (
VII.11
) has
|G_{0}|
-fold degenerate ground states

\ket{\text{GS};g}=\bigotimes_{i\in P}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{|G_{0}|}}\sum_{g_{i}\in G_{0}}\ket{gf(g_{i}),g_{i}}_{i}\right),\qquad\forall g\in G.

(VII.16)

The symmetry operators (
VII.14
) acts on these ground states as

U_{g}^{(L)}\ket{\text{GS};g^{\prime}}=\ket{\text{GS};gg^{\prime}},\qquad U_{g}^{(R)}\ket{\text{GS};g^{\prime}}=\ket{\text{GS};g^{\prime}f(g)^{-1}}.

(VII.17)

We note that
\ket{\text{GS};g}
is invariant under the action of
U_{gf(g_{r})g^{-1}}^{(L)}U_{g_{r}}^{(R)}
for all
g_{r}\in G_{0}
.
Thus, the unbroken symmetry of
\ket{\text{GS};g}
is

\{(gf(g_{r})g^{-1},g_{r})\mid g_{r}\in G_{0}\}=(g,e)\cdot G_{0}^{\text{diag}}(f)\cdot(g,e,)^{-1}.

(VII.18)

In particular, the unbroken symmetry of
\ket{\text{GS};e}
is
K=G_{0}^{\text{diag}}(f)
.

Generalized cluster model.

The generalized cluster model with
2\text{Rep}(G_{0})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}
symmetry is obtained by gauging the subgroup symmetry
H=G_{0}^{\text{left}}
in the above model.
To describe the model, we choose a gauge so that the matter fields after the gauge fixing take values in

S_{H\backslash G}=G_{0}^{\text{right}}:=\{(e,g)\mid g\in G_{0}\}.

(VII.19)

The state space of the gauged model after the gauge fixing is given by

\mathcal{H}_{\text{g.f.}}=\left(\bigotimes_{i\in P}\mathbb{C}^{|G_{0}^{\text{right}}|}\right)\otimes\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}\left(\bigotimes_{[ij]\in E}\mathbb{C}^{|G_{0}^{\text{left}}|}\right),

(VII.20)

where
\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}
is the projector that imposes the flatness condition on the gauge fields.
The matter field on plaquette
i\in P
and the gauge field on edge
[ij]\in E
are labeled by
(e,g_{i})\in G_{0}^{\text{right}}
and
(g_{ij},e)\in G_{0}^{\text{left}}
, respectively.
The corresponding states are simply denoted by
\ket{g_{i}}_{i}
and
\ket{g_{ij}}_{ij}
.
The basis states of the above state space will be denoted by

\ket{\{g_{i};g_{ij}\}}:=\left(\bigotimes_{i\in P}\ket{g_{i}}_{i}\right)\otimes\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}\left(\bigotimes_{[ij]\in E}\ket{g_{ij}}_{ij}\right).

(VII.21)

The Hamiltonian of the model is

H_{\text{g.f.}}=-\sum_{i\in P}\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}^{\text{g.f.}}-\sum_{[ij]\in E}\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}^{\text{g.f.}},

(VII.22)

where
\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}^{\text{g.f.}}
and
\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}^{\text{g.f.}}
are defined by

\displaystyle\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}^{\text{g.f.}}\Ket{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|G_{0}|}\sum_{g\in G_{0}}\Ket{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}},

(VII.23)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}^{\text{g.f.}}\ket{g_{i}}_{i}\otimes\ket{g_{ij}}_{ij}\otimes\ket{g_{j}}_{j}

\displaystyle=\delta_{g_{ij},f(g_{i}^{-1}g_{j})}\ket{g_{i}}_{i}\otimes\ket{g_{ij}}_{ij}\otimes\ket{g_{j}}_{j}.

(VII.24)

The loop on the right-hand side of (
VII.23
) represents the loop operator acting on the boundary of the plaquette
i\in P
.
58
58

58

See (
III.12
) for the general definition of a loop operator.

The above Hamiltonian has a unique ground state on a square lattice with periodic boundary conditions.
The ground state can be written explicitly as

\ket{\text{$G_{0}$-cluster}(f)}:=\frac{1}{|G_{0}|^{|P|/2}}\sum_{\{g_{i}\in G_{0}\}}\ket{\{g_{i};f(g_{i}^{-1}g_{j})\}},

(VII.25)

where
|P|
is the number of plaquettes.
A tensor network representation of this ground state is

\ket{\text{$G_{0}$-cluster}(f)}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},

(VII.26)

where the virtual bonds take values in
G_{0}
, and the local tensors represented by the black and white dots are defined by

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|G_{0}|}}\prod_{i=1,2,3,4}\delta_{g_{i},f(g)},\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{g,g_{1}^{-1}g_{2}}.

(VII.27)

We note that
\ket{\text{$G_{0}$-cluster}(f)}
reduces to the
G_{0}
-cluster state (
III.50
) when
f={\mathrm{id}}
.
On the other hand,
\ket{\text{$G_{0}$-cluster}(f)}
reduces to the trivial product state when
f(g)=e
for all
g\in G_{0}
.

VII.3
Symmetry operators

The generalized cluster model in the previous subsection has
2\text{Rep}(G_{0})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}
symmetry by construction.
In what follows, we will write down the symmetry operators for this symmetry.
We will also compute the action tensors for the symmetry operators acting on the generalized cluster state (
VII.25
).
See Section
IV
for the symmetry of the generalized cluster model obtained from more general input data.

1-form symmetry.

The 1-form symmetry operators of the model correspond to 1-endomorphisms of the unit object
I\in 2\text{Rep}(G_{0})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}
.
These 1-morphisms form a symmetric fusion 1-category

\text{End}_{2\text{Rep}(G_{0})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}}(I)\cong\text{Rep}(G_{0}).

(VII.28)

The symmetry operator labeled by
\rho\in\text{Rep}(G_{0})
is the Wilson line
W_{\rho}
.
The action of
W_{\rho}
supported on an oriented loop
\gamma
on the dual lattice is given by

W_{\rho}(\gamma)\ket{\{g_{i};g_{ij}\}}=\text{tr}_{V_{\rho}}(\rho(\text{hol}_{\gamma}(\{g_{ij}\})))\ket{\{g_{i};g_{ij}\}},

(VII.29)

where
V_{\rho}
is the representation space of
\rho
, and
\text{hol}_{\gamma}(\{g_{ij}\})
is the holonomy of the gauge fields
\{g_{ij}\mid[ij]\in E\}
along the loop
\gamma
.

0-form symmetry.

The 0-form symmetry operators are labeled by objects of
2\text{Rep}(G_{0})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}
.
Any 0-form symmetry operator, up to condensation, can be written as

\mathsf{C}U_{g},\qquad\forall g\in G_{0},

(VII.30)

where
\mathsf{C}:=\mathsf{D}_{G_{0}^{\text{left}}}\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{G_{0}^{\text{left}}}
is the symmetry operator corresponding to a simple object of
2\text{Rep}(G_{0})
, while
U_{g}
is the symmetry operator corresponding to a simple object of
2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}
.
Here,
\mathsf{D}_{G_{0}^{\text{left}}}
and
\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{G_{0}^{\text{left}}}
denote the gauging and ungauging operators defined by

\mathsf{D}_{G_{0}^{\text{left}}}\ket{\{g_{i},g_{i}^{\prime}\}}=\ket{\{g_{i}^{\prime};g_{i}^{-1}g_{j}\}},\qquad\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{G_{0}^{\text{left}}}=\mathsf{D}_{G_{0}^{\text{left}}}^{\dagger}.

(VII.31)

The actions of
\mathsf{C}
and
U_{g}
are then given by

\mathsf{C}\ket{\{g_{i};g_{ij}\}}=|G_{0}|\delta_{\text{hol}}(\{g_{ij}\})\ket{\{g_{i};g_{ij}\}},\qquad U_{g}\ket{\{g_{i};g_{ij}\}}=\ket{\{gg_{i};g_{ij}\}},

(VII.32)

where
\delta_{\text{hol}}(\{g_{ij}\})
is equal to
1
if the holonomy of the gauge field is trivial and
0
otherwise.
We note that
\mathsf{C}U_{g}
can be also written as
\mathsf{D}_{G_{0}^{\text{left}}}U_{g}^{(R)}\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{G_{0}^{\text{left}}}
, where
U_{g}^{(R)}
is the symmetry operator (
VII.14
) of the ungauged model.

Tensor network representation.

The symmetry operator (
VII.30
) can be represented by a tensor network

\mathsf{C}U_{g}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},

(VII.33)

where the virtual bonds take values in
G_{0}
, and the local tensors represented by the filled and unfilled green dots are defined by

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{g^{\prime\prime},gg^{\prime}}\prod_{i,j=1,2,3,4}\delta_{g_{i},g_{j}},\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{g^{\prime},g_{1}^{-1}g_{2}}\delta_{g^{\prime\prime},g^{\prime}}.

(VII.34)

The other 0-form symmetry operators can be obtained by condensing line operators on (
VII.33
) as in (
IV.50
).
We refer the reader to Section
IV.1
for more details on the symmetry operators.

Action tensors.

The action of the symmetry operator
\mathsf{C}U_{g}
on the local tensors of the generalized cluster state
\ket{\text{$G_{0}$-cluster}(f)}
can be computed as

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\penalty\ =\sum_{h\in G_{0}}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\penalty\ =\sum_{h\in G_{0}}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},

(VII.35)

where the action tensors
\phi_{f}[\mathsf{C}U_{g}]_{h}
and
\overline{\phi_{f}}[\mathsf{C}U_{g}]_{h}
are given by

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{g_{1}g_{3}^{-1},h}\delta_{g_{2}g_{3}^{-1},f(g^{-1})h}.

(VII.36)

These action tensors are obtained by applying the general formula (
IV.60
) to the current example.
59
59

59

Using the notations in Section
IV
, we can write
\mathsf{C}U_{g}=\mathsf{D}[V_{G_{0}^{\text{left}}}^{(e,g)}]
and
\phi_{f}[\mathsf{C}U_{g}]_{h}=\phi_{G_{0}^{\text{diag}}(f)}[V_{G_{0}^{\text{left}}}^{(e,g)}]_{(h,e)}
.

One can also check the above equality by a direct computation using (
VII.27
) and (
VII.34
).

VII.4
Interfaces: the case of
G_{0}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}

Let us consider the interfaces of the generalized cluster states with
2\text{Rep}(G_{0})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}
symmetry.
For simplicity, we will focus on the case of
G_{0}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
In this case, the symmetry category is

2\text{Rep}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}},

(VII.37)

i.e., the product of a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
0-form symmetry and (the condensation completion of) a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
1-form symmetry.
The generalized cluster states with this symmetry are in one-to-one correspondence with group endomorphisms of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
The group
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
has only two group endomorphisms
f^{(0)}
and
f^{(1)}
, which are defined by

f^{(0)}(g):=e,\qquad f^{(1)}(g):=g,\qquad\forall g\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}.

(VII.38)

The complements
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{right}}(f)
corresponding to
f=f^{(0)}
and
f=f^{(1)}
are given by

\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{right}}(f^{(0)}):=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{right}},\qquad\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{right}}(f^{(1)}):=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{diag}}.

(VII.39)

In what follows, we will consider the interface of the generalized cluster states labeled by
f^{(m)}
and
f^{(m^{\prime})}
for each pair of
m,m^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
.

The models.

Let us first write down the generalized cluster models and their ground states for both choices of the group endomorphism
f
.
The state space is independent of
f
and is given by

\mathcal{H}_{\text{g.f.}}=\left(\bigotimes_{i\in P}\mathbb{C}^{2}\right)\otimes\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}\left(\bigotimes_{[ij]\in E}\mathbb{C}^{2}\right).

(VII.40)

Namely, we have a single qubit on each plaquette and on each edge.
The Hamiltonians for
f=f^{(0)}
and
f=f^{(1)}
are given by
60
60

60

We recall that we choose
S_{H\backslash G}
to be
G_{0}^{\text{right}}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{right}}
.

\displaystyle H_{\text{g.f.}}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i\in P}\frac{1}{2}(1+X^{(i)})-\sum_{[ij]\in E}\frac{1}{2}(1+Z^{(ij)}),

(VII.41)

\displaystyle H_{\text{g.f.}}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i\in P}\frac{1}{2}(1+X^{(i)}\prod_{[ij]\in\partial i}X^{(ij)})-\sum_{[ij]\in E}\frac{1}{2}(1+Z^{(i)}Z^{(ij)}Z^{(j)}),

(VII.42)

where
X^{(i)}
and
Z^{(i)}
are the Pauli operators acting on the qubit on the plaquette
i\in P
, and
X^{(ij)}
and
Z^{(ij)}
are those acting on the qubit on the edge
[ij]\in E
.
We note that
H^{(0)}_{\text{g.f.}}
is the trivial Hamiltonian, while
H^{(1)}_{\text{g.f.}}
is the cluster Hamiltonian.
The ground states of these Hamiltonians are

\displaystyle\ket{\text{$\mathbb{Z}_{2}$-cluster}(f^{(0)})}:=\ket{\text{trivial}}=\left(\bigotimes_{i\in P}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sum_{g_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\ket{g_{i}}_{i}\right)\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{[ij]\in E}\ket{e}_{ij}\right),

(VII.43)

\displaystyle\ket{\text{$\mathbb{Z}_{2}$-cluster}(f^{(1)})}:=\ket{\text{$\mathbb{Z}_{2}$-cluster}}=\frac{1}{2^{|P|/2}}\sum_{\{g_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\}}\ket{\{g_{i};g_{i}^{-1}g_{j}\}}.

(VII.44)

Category theoretical description of interfaces.

As discussed in Section
V.1
, the symmetry at the interface of the generalized cluster states is generally described by the strip 2-algebra (
V.7
).
The strip 2-algebra for the interface of the SPT phases labeled by the complements
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
is denoted by
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
.
In the case of
2\text{Rep}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
SPT phases,
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
are either
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{right}}
or
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{diag}}
as shown in (
VII.39
).
Based on the results in Section
V.1.1
, one can compute the strip 2-algebra
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
for each choice of
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{right}},\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{right}}}

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{diag}},\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{diag}}}\cong\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\oplus\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}},

(VII.45)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{right}},\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{diag}}}

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{diag}},\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{right}}}\cong\text{Mat}_{2}(\text{Vec}).

(VII.46)

Namely, the symmetry at the interface of the same SPT phase is
\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\oplus\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
, while the symmetry at the interface of different SPT phases is
\text{Mat}_{2}(\text{Vec})
.
The former should be regarded as the categorical description of a non-anomalous
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{[0]}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{[1]}
symmetry in 1+1d, where the superscript denotes the form degree.
On the other hand, the latter should be regarded as the categorical description of a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{[0]}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{[1]}
symmetry with a mixed anomaly in 1+1d.

Tensor network description of interfaces.

Let us now provide a tensor network description of the above interface symmetries.
For simplicity, as in Section
V.2
, we will focus on the interfaces that can be represented by tensor networks of the form (
V.51
).
To obtain the symmetry operators at the interface, we first write down the action tensors.
Due to (
VII.36
), the action tensors for the symmetry operator
\mathsf{C}U_{g}
acting on
\ket{\text{$\mathbb{Z}_{2}$-cluster}(f^{(m)})}
are given by

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{g_{1}g_{3},h}\delta_{g_{2}g_{3},g^{m}h},

(VII.47)

where
g,h,g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
Using these action tensors, we can construct the symmetry operators acting on the interface of
\ket{\text{$\mathbb{Z}_{2}$-cluster}(f^{(m)})}
and
\ket{\text{$\mathbb{Z}_{2}$-cluster}(f^{(m^{\prime})})}
as

\hat{\mathsf{L}}(\mathsf{C}U_{g};h,h^{\prime}):=\bigotimes_{i:\text{sites}}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}(\mathsf{C}U_{g};h,h^{\prime}),

(VII.48)

where the on-site operator
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}(\mathsf{C}U_{g};h_{1},h_{2})
is defined by

\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}(\mathsf{C}U_{g};h,h^{\prime}):=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\frac{1+\text{sgn}(hh^{\prime})Z_{\ell}^{(i)}Z_{r}^{(i)}}{2}(X_{\ell}^{(i)})^{m\delta_{g,\eta}}(X_{r}^{(i)})^{m^{\prime}\delta_{g,\eta}}.

(VII.49)

Here,
\eta
denotes the generator of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, and
sgn
denotes the sign representation of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
The operator on the right-hand side acts on the middle two legs in the middle diagram.
61
61

61

The small gap in the middle of the diagram corresponds to the interface.

More specifically,
X_{\ell}^{(i)}
and
Z_{\ell}^{(i)}
are the Pauli operators acting on the left leg, while
X_{r}^{(i)}
and
Z_{r}^{(i)}
are those acting on the right leg.
By plugging (
VII.49
) into (
VII.48
), we find

\hat{\mathsf{L}}(\mathsf{C}U_{g};h,h^{\prime})=P_{\text{sgn}(hh^{\prime})}X_{\ell}^{m\delta_{g,\eta}}X_{r}^{m^{\prime}\delta_{g,\eta}},

(VII.50)

where
P_{\pm}:=\bigotimes_{i:\text{sites}}\frac{1}{2}(1\pm Z_{\ell}^{(i)}Z_{r}^{(i)})
,
X_{\ell}:=\bigotimes_{i:\text{sites}}X_{\ell}^{(i)}
, and
X_{r}:=\bigotimes_{i:\text{sites}}X_{r}^{(i)}
.

Equation (
VII.50
) shows that the set of the symmetry operators at the interface of
\ket{\text{$\mathbb{Z}_{2}$-cluster}(f^{(m)})}
and
\ket{\text{$\mathbb{Z}_{2}$-cluster}(f^{(m^{\prime})})}
is given by

\{P_{+},\qquad P_{-},\qquad P_{+}X_{\ell}^{m}X_{r}^{m^{\prime}},\qquad P_{-}X_{\ell}^{m}X_{r}^{m^{\prime}}\}.

(VII.51)

Let us compute the algebra of these symmetry operators for each pair of
m,m^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
.

•

When
(m,m^{\prime})=(0,0)
, the set of the symmetry operators reduces to

\left\{P_{+},\quad P_{-}\right\}.

(VII.52)

The algebra of these symmetry operators can be computed as

P_{+}P_{+}=P_{+},\quad P_{-}P_{-}=P_{-},\quad P_{+}P_{-}=P_{-}P_{+}=0.

(VII.53)

This suggests that the symmetry operators at the interface form a multifusion category
\text{Vec}\oplus\text{Vec}
, whose simple objects correspond to
P_{+}
and
P_{-}
.
The action of the symmetry category (
VII.45
) is thus implemented via a tensor functor
\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\oplus\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\to\text{Vec}\oplus\text{Vec}
.
62
62

62

We note that the symmetry action is unfaithful. This is because we focused on the interface of the simple form (
V.51
). The symmetry action can become faithful if we consider more general interfaces.

•

When
(m,m^{\prime})=(0,1)
, the set of the symmetry operators is

\left\{P_{+},\quad P_{-},\quad P_{+}X_{r},\quad P_{-}X_{r}\right\}.

(VII.54)

The algebra of these symmetry operators can be computed as

(P_{+}X_{r})P_{+}=(P_{-}X_{r})P_{-}=0,\quad(P_{+}X_{r})P_{-}=P_{+}X_{r},\quad(P_{-}X_{r})P_{+}=P_{-}X_{r}.

(VII.55)

The other products can be computed by using (
VII.53
) and
X_{r}^{2}=1
.
The above equation suggests that the symmetry operators at the interface form a multifusion category
\text{Mat}_{2}(\text{Vec})
, which agrees with (
VII.46
).
The simple objects of the diagonal components correspond to
P_{+}
and
P_{-}
, while those of the off-diagonal components correspond to
P_{+}X_{r}
and
P_{-}X_{r}
.

•

When
(m,m^{\prime})=(1,0)
, the set of the symmetry operators is

\left\{P_{+},\quad P_{-},\quad P_{+}X_{\ell},\quad P_{-}X_{\ell}\right\}.

(VII.56)

The algebra of these symmetry operators is isomorphic to (
VII.55
).

•

When
(m,m^{\prime})=(1,1)
, the set of the symmetry operators is

\left\{P_{+},\quad P_{-},\quad P_{+}X_{\ell}X_{r},\quad P_{-}X_{\ell}X_{r}\right\}.

(VII.57)

The algebra of these symmetry operators can be computed as

(P_{+}X_{\ell}X_{r})P_{-}=(P_{-}X_{\ell}X_{r})P_{+}=0,\quad(P_{+}X_{\ell}X_{r})P_{+}=P_{+}X_{\ell}X_{r},\quad(P_{-}X_{\ell}X_{r})P_{-}=P_{-}X_{\ell}X_{r}.

(VII.58)

The other products can be computed by using (
VII.53
) and
X_{\ell}^{2}=X_{r}^{2}=1
.
The above equation suggests that the symmetry operators at the interface form
\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\oplus\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
, which agrees with (
VII.45
).
The simple objects of the first component are
P_{+}
and
P_{+}X_{\ell}X_{r}
, while those of the second component are
P_{-}
and
P_{-}X_{\ell}X_{r}
.

Interface modes.

Let us finally discuss the non-degenerate interface modes between
\ket{\text{$\mathbb{Z}_{2}$-cluster}(f^{(m)})}
and
\ket{\text{$\mathbb{Z}_{2}$-cluster}(f^{(m^{\prime})})}
for each pair of
m,m^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
.

•

When
(m,m^{\prime})=(0,0)
, the interface symmetry is generated by
P_{+}
and
P_{-}
, which are the projectors onto the subspaces with
Z_{\ell}^{(i)}Z_{r}^{(i)}=+1
and
Z_{\ell}^{(i)}Z_{r}^{(i)}=-1
for all
i
, respectively.
Since they are orthogonal projectors, we can divide the Hilbert space of interface modes into two sectors labeled by the eigenvalues of
Z_{\ell}^{(i)}Z_{r}^{(i)}
.
In the sector with
Z_{\ell}^{(i)}Z_{r}^{(i)}=+1
, we have infinitely many interface modes given by the tensor product states

\bigotimes_{i:\text{sites}}(\alpha_{i}\ket{e,e}_{i}+\beta_{i}\ket{\eta,\eta}_{i}),

(VII.59)

where
\eta
is the generator of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
.
However, these modes are continuously connected to each other by varying
\alpha_{i}
and
\beta_{i}
without breaking the symmetry.
Thus, we think of them as equivalent interface modes.
As a representative of this sector, we can choose

\ket{\psi(e)}=\bigotimes_{i:\text{sites}}\left(\sum_{g_{\ell}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\ket{g_{\ell},g_{\ell}}_{i}\right).

(VII.60)

Similarly, in the sector with
Z_{\ell}^{(i)}Z_{r}^{(i)}=-1
, we also have infinitely many interface modes given by

\bigotimes_{i:\text{sites}}(\alpha_{i}\ket{e,\eta}_{i}+\beta_{i}\ket{\eta,e}_{i}),

(VII.61)

where
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
.
Again, these modes are continuously connected to each other, and as a representative of this sector, we can choose

\ket{\psi(\eta)}=\bigotimes_{i:\text{sites}}\left(\sum_{g_{\ell}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\ket{g_{\ell},\eta g_{\ell}}_{i}\right).

(VII.62)

The symmetry operators
P_{+}
and
P_{-}
act on the above interface modes as

P_{+}\ket{\psi(e)}=\ket{\psi(e)},\qquad P_{-}\ket{\psi(\eta)}=\ket{\psi(\eta)},\qquad P_{-}\ket{\psi(e)}=P_{+}\ket{\psi(\eta)}=0,

(VII.63)

which shows that
\ket{\psi(e)}
and
\ket{\psi(\eta)}
are non-degenerate.

From a category theoretical point of view, non-degenerate interface modes are classified by the fiber functors of the fusion category generated by the symmetry operators at the interface.
The symmetry operators
P_{+}
and
P_{-}
generate a multifusion category
\text{Vec}\oplus\text{Vec}
and it has two fiber functors
F_{\pm}:\text{Vec}\oplus\text{Vec}\to\text{Vec}
.
Here,
F_{+}
and
F_{-}
are projections onto the first and second components, respectively.
The symmetry action (
VII.63
) implies that the two non-degenerate interface modes
\ket{\psi(e)}
and
\ket{\psi(\eta)}
correspond to
F_{+}
and
F_{-}
, respectively.

•

When
(m,m^{\prime})=(1,1)
, the interface symmetry is generated by
P_{\pm}
and
P_{\pm}X_{\ell}X_{r}
, where
P_{\pm}
is the projector onto the subspaces with
Z_{\ell}^{(i)}Z_{r}^{(i)}=\pm 1
for all
i
.
Since
P_{+}
and
P_{-}
are orthogonal projectors, we can divide the Hilbert space of interface modes into two sectors labeled by the eigenvalues of
Z_{\ell}^{(i)}Z_{r}^{(i)}
.
The operator
P_{\pm}X_{\ell}X_{r}
acts as
X_{\ell}X_{r}
within the sector with
Z_{\ell}^{(i)}Z_{r}^{(i)}=\pm 1
, while it acts as zero on the other sector.
In the sector with
Z_{\ell}^{(i)}Z_{r}^{(i)}=+1
, there are infinitely many interface modes given by the tensor product states

\bigotimes_{i:\text{sites}}\alpha_{i}(\ket{e,e}_{i}+(-1)^{n_{i}}\ket{\eta,\eta}_{i}),

(VII.64)

where
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
and
n_{i}\in\{0,1\}
.
Contrary to the previous case, we cannot continuously connect the modes with
n_{i}=0
and
n_{i}=1
by only varying
\alpha_{i}
.
However, by a more general continuous deformation, we can change
n_{i}
’s in pairs without breaking the symmetry.
Indeed, for any pair
(i_{1},i_{2})
, we can change
n_{i_{1}}
and
n_{i_{2}}
simultaneously by applying
Z_{r}^{(i_{1})}Z_{r}^{(i_{2})}
, which can be continuously deformed into the identity operator via

e^{i\frac{\theta}{4}(1-Z_{r}^{(i_{1})}\otimes Z_{r}^{(i_{2})})},\qquad 0\leq\theta\leq 2\pi.

(VII.65)

Since this operator commutes with
P_{\pm}
and
P_{\pm}X_{\ell}X_{r}
, the deformation does not break the symmetry.
By repeating this procedure, we can deform any state of the form (
VII.64
) to the one with at most one site with
n_{i}=1
.
63
63

63

We note that the intermediate state of this deformation is not of the form (
VII.64
).

The remaining site with
n_{i}=1
cannot be removed by the above procedure.
This is interpreted as a point-like excitation on the interface.
This excitation can be moved freely by continuous deformations, and hence it is topological up to continuous deformation.
Therefore, the interface modes (
VII.64
) are all equivalent up to topological point-like excitation.
As a representative of this sector, we can choose

\ket{\psi(e)}=\bigotimes_{i:\text{sites}}\left(\sum_{g_{\ell}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\ket{g_{\ell},g_{\ell}}_{i}\right).

(VII.66)

Similarly, in the sector with
Z_{\ell}^{(i)}Z_{r}^{(i)}=-1
, there are also infinitely many interface modes given by

\bigotimes_{i:\text{sites}}\alpha_{i}(\ket{e,\eta}_{i}+(-1)^{n_{i}}\ket{\eta,e}_{i}),

(VII.67)

where
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
and
n_{i}\in\{0,1\}
.
For the same reason as above, these interface modes are all equivalent up to point-like excitation.
As a representative of this sector, we can choose

\ket{\psi(\eta)}=\bigotimes_{i:\text{sites}}\left(\sum_{g_{\ell}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\ket{g_{\ell},\eta g_{\ell}}_{i}\right).

(VII.68)

The symmetry operators
P_{\pm}
and
P_{\pm}X_{\ell}X_{r}
act on the above interface modes as

\displaystyle P_{+}\ket{\psi(e)}

\displaystyle=P_{+}X_{\ell}X_{r}\ket{\psi(e)}=\ket{\psi(e)},\qquad P_{-}\ket{\psi(\eta)}=P_{-}X_{\ell}X_{r}\ket{\psi(\eta)}=\ket{\psi(\eta)},

(VII.69)

\displaystyle P_{-}\ket{\psi(e)}

\displaystyle=P_{-}X_{\ell}X_{r}\ket{\psi(e)}=P_{+}\ket{\psi(\eta)}=P_{+}X_{\ell}X_{r}\ket{\psi(\eta)}=0,

which shows that
\ket{\psi(e)}
and
\ket{\psi(\eta)}
are non-degenerate.

From a category theoretical point of view, non-degenerate interface modes are classified by the fiber functors of the fusion category generated by the symmetry operators at the interface.
The symmetry operators
P_{\pm}
and
P_{\pm}X_{\ell}X_{r}
generate a multifusion category
\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\oplus\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
and it has two fiber functors
F_{\pm}:\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\oplus\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\to\text{Vec}
.
Here,
F_{+}
and
F_{-}
are the unique fiber functor of the first and second
\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
, respectively.
The symmetry action (
VII.69
) implies that the two non-degenerate interface modes
\ket{\psi(e)}
and
\ket{\psi(\eta)}
correspond to
F_{+}
and
F_{-}
, respectively.

Moreover, the topological point-like excitations on the non-degenerate interface corresponding to
F_{\pm}
are classified by natural endomorphisms of
F_{\pm}
.
Given that both
F_{+}
and
F_{-}
are the unique fiber functor of
\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
, the natural endomorphisms of
F_{\pm}
are in one-to-one correspondence with objects of the dual category
(\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}})_{\text{Vec}}^{*}\cong\text{Rep}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})
.
This category has two simple objects, i.e., the trivial representation and the sign representation of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
Since both of these objects are invertible, the topological point-like excitations on the non-degenerate interface are classified by
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
The above discussion suggests that the non-trivial point-like excitation
n_{i}=1
corresponds to the sign representation, while the trivial point-like excitation
n_{i}=0
corresponds to the trivial representation.

•

When
(m,m^{\prime})=(0,1)\text{ or }(1,0)
, we cannot find any non-degenerate interface modes.
This is consistent with the fact that the multifusion category
\text{Mat}_{2}(\text{Vec})
generated by the symmetry operators at the interface does not have any fiber functors.

VII.5
Parameterized families

In this subsection, we consider
S^{1}
-parameterized families of
2\text{Rep}(G_{0})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}
-symmetric states in the same phase as
\ket{\text{$G_{0}$-cluster}(f)}
.

Classification.

Due to the general classification mentioned in Section
VI
, the
S^{1}
-parameterized families are expected to be classified by

\{(h,[\xi])\mid h\in H\text{ s.t. }K=hKh^{-1},\penalty\ [\xi]\in H^{2}(K,\mathrm{U}(1))\},

(VII.70)

where
H=G_{0}^{\text{left}}
and
K=G_{0}^{\text{diag}}(f)
.
In what follows, we will only consider
S^{1}
-parameterized families with trivial
[\xi]
.
The condition
K=hKh^{-1}
for
h=(g,e)\in G_{0}^{\text{left}}
means that for any
(f(g^{\prime}),g^{\prime})\in G_{0}^{\text{diag}}(f)
, there exists
(f(g^{\prime\prime}),g^{\prime\prime})\in G_{0}^{\text{diag}}(f)
such that

(g,e)(f(g^{\prime}),g^{\prime})(g,e)^{-1}=(f(g^{\prime\prime}),g^{\prime\prime}).

(VII.71)

This condition is equivalent to

gf(g^{\prime})g^{-1}=f(g^{\prime}),\qquad\forall g^{\prime}\in G_{0}.

(VII.72)

Therefore,
h=(g,e)
satisfies
K=hKh^{-1}
if and only if
g
commutes with the image of
f
.
In other words, the condition
K=hKh^{-1}
holds if and only is
g
is in the centralizer of the image of
f
in
G_{0}
, i.e.,

g\in C_{G_{0}}(\mathrm{Im}(f)).

(VII.73)

In particular, if
f=\mathrm{id}
, the condition holds for
g\in Z(G_{0})
.
On the other hand, if
f
is the trivial homomorphism, the condition holds for every
g\in G_{0}
.
Below, we will simply write
h=(g,e)\in H
as
g
.

S^{1}
-parameterized families.

For each
g\in C_{G_{0}}(\mathrm{Im}(f))
, we can construct an
S^{1}
-parameterized family of
2\text{Rep}(G_{0})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}
-symmetric models by conjugating the Hamiltonian (
VII.22
) with the following
\theta
-dependent unitary operator:

U_{\text{g.f.}}^{g}(\theta)\coloneq\prod_{i\in P}\left.u_{g}^{(i)}(\theta)\right|_{\text{g.f.}},\qquad\left.u_{g}^{(i)}(\theta)\right|_{\text{g.f.}}\coloneq\exp\left(\frac{\theta}{2\pi}\log L_{g^{-1}}^{(\partial i)}\right),

(VII.74)

Here,
\theta
runs from
0
to
2\pi
and
L_{g^{-1}}^{(\partial i)}
denotes the loop operator defined as in (
III.12
).
We choose the branch of the logarithm as

\log L_{g^{-1}}^{(\partial i)}=\sum_{s=0}^{\left|G_{0}\right|-1}i\frac{2\pi s}{\left|G_{0}\right|}P_{s}[L_{g^{-1}}^{(\partial i)}],

(VII.75)

where
P_{s}[L_{g^{-1}}^{(\partial i)}]
is the projection onto the eigenspace of
L_{g^{-1}}^{(\partial i)}
with eigenvalue
e^{i\frac{2\pi s}{\left|G_{0}\right|}}
.
We note that
\left.u_{g}^{(i)}(\theta)\right|_{\text{g.f.}}
interpolates between the identity operator and the loop operator
L_{g^{-1}}^{(\partial i)}
.
By a direct computation, we can show that
U_{\text{g.f.}}^{g}(\theta)
at
\theta=2\pi
commutes with the Hamiltonian (
VII.22
) due to (
VII.73
).
Therefore, conjugating (
VII.22
) with
U_{\text{g.f.}}^{g}(\theta)
gives us a
2\pi
-periodic Hamiltonian.
This
2\pi
-periodic Hamiltonian preserves the
2\text{Rep}(G_{0})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}
symmetry because
U_{\text{g.f.}}^{g}(\theta)
commutes with the symmetry operators for all
\theta\in[0,2\pi]
: see Section
VI.3
for more details.

Generalized Thouless pumps.

We can argue the non-triviality of the above
S^{1}
-parameterized families by considering the corresponding generalized Thouless pumps.
We will not study this here because the discussion is almost parallel to the case of the
G_{0}
-clutser state detailed in Section
VI.3
.

VIII
Example 2: Tambara-Yamagami SPT phases

As another class of examples, we consider 2+1d SPT phases with Tambara-Yamagami symmetry
2\text{TY}(A,1)
, where
A
is a finite abelian group.
As we reviewed in Section
II.4
,
2\text{TY}(A,1)
is a group-theoretical fusion 2-category defined by

2\text{TY}(A,1):=\mathcal{C}(A\wr\mathbb{Z}_{2};A_{L}),

(VIII.1)

where
A\wr\mathbb{Z}_{2}:=(A\times A)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is the wreath product of
A
by
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, and
A_{L}
is the subgroup (
II.19
) of
A\wr\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
We recall that
2\text{TY}(A,1)
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded fusion 2-category

2\text{TY}(A,1)\cong 2\text{Rep}(A)\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{A}\oplus 2\text{Vec},

(VIII.2)

where
2\text{Rep}(A)\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{A}
is the trivially graded component.
Throughout this subsection, the elements of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
will be denoted additively, i.e.,
\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\{0,1\}
, while the elements of
A
