will be denoted multiplicatively.

VIII.1
Classification

We first provide the classification of SPT phases with Tambara-Yamagami symmetry
2\text{TY}(A,1)
.
The SPT phases with
2\text{TY}(A,1)
symmetry are in one-to-one correspondence with fiber 2-functors of
2\text{TY}(A,1)
.
Due to (
VIII.1
), fiber 2-functors of
2\text{TY}(A,1)
are labeled by pairs
(K,\lambda)
, where
K
is a complement of
A_{L}
in
A\wr\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, and
\lambda\in Z^{3}(K,\mathrm{U}(1))
is a 3-cocycle on
K
.
We refer the reader to Section
II.3
for the classification of fiber 2-functors of general group-theoretical fusion 2-categories.

As we will see below, a complement
K
of
A_{L}
in
A\wr\mathbb{Z}_{2}
can always be written as

K=K(f):=\{(f_{n}(a),a,n)\mid a\in A,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\},

(VIII.3)

where
f_{n}:A\to A
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is a map that satisfies the following conditions:

•

f_{0}:A\to A
is an involutive group homomorphism

•

f_{1}(e)\in A
is a fixed point of
f_{0}
, i.e.,
f_{0}(f_{1}(e))=f_{1}(e)

•

f_{1}:A\to A
is determined by
f_{1}(a)=f_{0}(a)f_{1}(e)
for all
a\in A

We note that
2\text{TY}(A,1)
always admits a fiber 2-functor because the identity map
f_{0}=f_{1}={\mathrm{id}}
satisfies the above conditions.
In what follows, we will focus on fiber 2-functors with trivial
\lambda
.
In this case, each fiber 2-functor is labeled only by a complement
K(f)
.

The fiber 2-functors labeled by
K(f)
and
K(f^{\prime})
are isomorphic to each other if and only if there exists
a\in A
such that

f^{\prime}_{0}=f_{0},\quad f^{\prime}_{1}(e)=af_{0}(a)f_{1}(e).

(VIII.4)

A proof will be provided shortly.
Therefore, fiber 2-functors with trivial
\lambda
are classified by the set

\coprod_{f_{0}\in\text{Aut}(A)[2]}A^{f_{0}}/(A^{f_{0}})_{0},

(VIII.5)

where
\text{Aut}(A)[2]
is the 2-torsion subgroup of the automorphism group
\text{Aut}(A)
,
A^{f_{0}}:=\{a\in A\mid f_{0}(a)=a\}
is the group of fixed points of
f_{0}
, and
(A^{f_{0}})_{0}:=\{af_{0}(a)\mid a\in A\}
is a subgroup of
A^{f_{0}}
.

Now, we show that any complement
K
of
A_{L}
in
A\wr\mathbb{Z}_{2}
can be written as (
VIII.3
).
First of all, since a complement
K
satisfies
A_{L}K=A\wr\mathbb{Z}_{2}
by definition, it must contain an element of the form
(\bullet,a,n)
for any
a\in A
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, where
\bullet
denotes some element of
A
.
In addition, since
K
satisfies
A_{L}\cap K=\{(e,e,0)\}\in A\wr\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, the element
(\bullet,a,n)\in K
must be unique for every
a\in A
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
Thus, there exists a map
f:A\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to A
such that
K=\{(f(a,n),a,n)\mid a\in A,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\}
.
If we define
f_{n}(a):=f(a,n)
, we find
K=K(f)
.
The set
K(f)
is a complement of
A_{L}
if and only if it is a subgroup of
A\wr\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, i.e., if and only

(f_{n}(a),a,n)\cdot(f_{m}(b),b,m)\in K(f),\qquad\forall a,b\in A,\penalty\ \forall n,m\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}.

(VIII.6)

Let us look into the above condition for each choice of
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

•

When
(n,m)=(0,0)
, the left-hand side of (
VIII.6
) is equal to
(f_{0}(a)f_{0}(b),ab,0)
.
This element is in
K(f)
if and only if
f_{0}
satisfies

f_{0}(a)f_{0}(b)=f_{0}(ab).

(VIII.7)

Thus, we find that
f_{0}:A\to A
must be a group homomorphism.

•

When
(n,m)=(0,1)
, the left-hand side of (
VIII.6
) is equal to
(f_{0}(a)f_{1}(b),ab,1)
.
This element is in
K(f)
if and only if
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
satisfy

f_{0}(a)f_{1}(b)=f_{1}(ab).

(VIII.8)

If we choose
b=e
, this condition reduces to

f_{0}(a)f_{1}(e)=f_{1}(a).

(VIII.9)

Conversely, if equation (
VIII.9
) holds, then equation (
VIII.8
) also holds because
f_{0}
is a group homomorphism due to (
VIII.7
).
Thus, the condition (
VIII.6
) for
(n,m)=(0,1)
reduces to (
VIII.9
) for all
a\in A
.

•

When
(n,m)=(1,0)
, the left-hand side of (
VIII.6
) is equal to
(f_{1}(a)b,af_{0}(b),1)
.
This element is in
K(f)
if and only if
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
satisfy

f_{1}(a)b=f_{1}(af_{0}(b)).

(VIII.10)

Due to (
VIII.7
) and (
VIII.9
), the above equation reduces to

f_{0}(f_{0}(b))=b.

(VIII.11)

Thus, we find that
f_{0}:A\to A
must be an involution.

•

When
(n,m)=(1,1)
, the left-hand side of (
VIII.6
) is equal to
(f_{1}(a)b,af_{1}(b),0)
.
This element is in
K(f)
if and only if
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
satisfy

f_{1}(a)b=f_{0}(af_{1}(b)).

(VIII.12)

Due to (
VIII.7
), (
VIII.9
), and (
VIII.11
), the above equation reduces to

f_{0}(f_{1}(e))=f_{1}(e).

(VIII.13)

Thus, we find that
f_{1}(e)
must be a fixed point of
f_{0}
.

This shows that
K(f)
is a complement of
A_{L}
in
A\wr\mathbb{Z}_{2}
if and only if the three conditions below (
VIII.3
) are satisfied.

Due to the general result reviewed in Section
II.3
, the complements
K(f)
and
K(f^{\prime})
correspond to isomorphic fiber 2-functors if and only if there exists
g\in A\wr\mathbb{Z}_{2}
such that

K(f^{\prime})=gK(f)g^{-1}.

(VIII.14)

Since
K(f)
is a complement of
A_{L}
, we can take
g
to be an element of
A_{L}
without loss of generality.
For
g=(a,e,0)\in A_{L}
, the above condition is equivalent to
64
64

64

Equation (
VIII.15
) is equivalent to
gK(f)g^{-1}\subset K(f^{\prime})
. On the other hand,
gK(f)g^{-1}
and
K(f^{\prime})
have the same order. Thus, the relation
gK(f)g^{-1}\subset K(f^{\prime})
implies
gK(f)g^{-1}=K(f^{\prime})
.

(a,e,0)\cdot(f_{n}(b),b,n)\cdot(a,e,0)^{-1}\in K(f^{\prime}),\qquad\forall b\in A,\penalty\ \forall n\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}.

(VIII.15)

Let us look into the above condition for each choice of
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

•

When
n=0
, the left-hand side of (
VIII.15
) is equal to
(f_{0}(b),b,0)
.
This element is in
K(f^{\prime})
if and only if
f_{0}
and
f_{0}^{\prime}
satisfy

f_{0}(b)=f_{0}^{\prime}(b).

(VIII.16)

Thus, the condition (
VIII.15
) for
n=0
reduces to the equality
f_{0}=f_{0}^{\prime}
.

•

When
n=1
, the left-hand side of (
VIII.15
) is equal to
(af_{1}(b),ba^{-1},1)
.
This element is in
K(f^{\prime})
if and only if
f_{1}
and
f_{1}^{\prime}
satisfy

af_{1}(b)=f_{1}^{\prime}(ba^{-1}).

(VIII.17)

Due to (
VIII.7
), (
VIII.9
) and (
VIII.16
), the above equation reduces to

f_{1}^{\prime}(e)=af_{0}(a)f_{1}(e).

(VIII.18)

This shows that
K(f)
and
K(f^{\prime})
correspond to isomorphic fiber 2-functors if and only if there exists
a\in A
such that the condition (
VIII.4
) is satisfied.

VIII.2
Generalized cluster states

The SPT phases labeled by fiber 2-functors with trivial
\lambda
are realized in the generalized cluster models defined in Section
III
.
In the case of the Tambara-Yamagami symmetry
2\text{TY}(A,1)
, the input data of the model are given by

G=A\wr\mathbb{Z}_{2},\quad H=A_{L},\quad K=K(f).

(VIII.19)

The generalized cluster model for the above input data is obtained by gauging the subgroup symmetry
H
in a
G
-symmetric model that spontaneously breaks the symmetry
G
down to
K
.
In what follows, we will write down the models both before and after the gauging.

G/K
-SSB model.

The state space of the
G
-symmetric model before the gauging is

\mathcal{H}=\bigotimes_{i\in P}\mathbb{C}^{|A\wr\mathbb{Z}_{2}|}.

(VIII.20)

The on-site state space on each plaquette
i\in P
is spanned by
\{\ket{a_{i},b_{i},n_{i}}\mid a_{i},b_{i}\in A,n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\}
.
The Hamiltonian of the model is

H=-\sum_{i\in P}\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}-\sum_{[ij]\in E}\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij},

(VIII.21)

where
\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}
and
\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}
are defined by

\displaystyle\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}\ket{a_{i},b_{i},n_{i}}_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2|A|}\sum_{a\in A}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\ket{(a_{i},b_{i},n_{i})\cdot(f_{n}(a),a,n)}_{i},

(VIII.22)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}\ket{a_{i},b_{i},n_{i}}_{i}\otimes\ket{a_{j},b_{j},n_{j}}_{j}

\displaystyle=\delta_{(a_{i},b_{i},n_{i})^{-1}\cdot(a_{j},b_{j},n_{j})\in K(f)}\ket{a_{i},b_{i},n_{i}}_{i}\otimes\ket{a_{j},b_{j},n_{j}}_{j}.

(VIII.23)

Here,
(a,b,n)\cdot(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},n^{\prime})
denotes the group multiplication in
A\wr\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, which is defined by (
II.20
).
The symmetry of the above model is generated by

U_{a}^{(L)}:=\bigotimes_{i\in P}X_{a}^{(i;L)},\qquad U_{a}^{(R)}:=\bigotimes_{i\in P}X_{a}^{(i;R)},\qquad U_{\text{SWAP}}:=\bigotimes_{i\in P}\sigma_{x}^{(i)}\text{SWAP}_{A}^{(i)},

(VIII.24)

where the on-site operators
X_{a}^{(i;L)}
,
X_{a}^{(i;R)}
,
\sigma_{x}^{(i)}
, and
\text{SWAP}_{A}^{(i)}
are defined by

\displaystyle X_{a}^{(i;L)}\ket{a_{i},b_{i},n_{i}}_{i}

\displaystyle=\ket{aa_{i},b_{i},n_{i}}_{i},

\displaystyle X_{a}^{(i;R)}\ket{a_{i},b_{i},n_{i}}_{i}

\displaystyle=\ket{a_{i},ab_{i},n_{i}}_{i},

(VIII.25)

\displaystyle\sigma_{x}^{(i)}\ket{a_{i},b_{i},n_{i}}_{i}

\displaystyle=\ket{a_{i},b_{i},n_{i}+1}_{i},

\displaystyle\text{SWAP}_{A}^{(i)}\ket{a_{i},b_{i},n_{i}}_{i}

\displaystyle=\ket{b_{i},a_{i},n_{i}}_{i}.

The Hamiltonian (
VIII.21
) has
|A|
-fold degenerate ground states

\ket{\text{GS};a}=\bigotimes_{i\in P}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2|A|}}\sum_{a_{i}\in A}\sum_{n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\ket{af_{n_{i}}(a_{i}),a_{i},n_{i}}_{i}\right),\qquad\forall a\in A.

(VIII.26)

The symmetry operators (
VIII.24
) act on these ground states as

U_{a}^{(L)}\ket{\text{GS};a^{\prime}}=\ket{\text{GS};aa^{\prime}},\quad U_{a}^{(R)}\ket{\text{GS};a^{\prime}}=\ket{\text{GS};f_{0}(a)^{-1}a^{\prime}},\quad U_{\text{SWAP}}\ket{\text{GS};a^{\prime}}=\ket{\text{GS};f_{1}(a^{\prime})^{-1}}.

(VIII.27)

We note that
\ket{\text{GS};a}
is invariant under the action of
U_{(af_{0}(a))^{n}f_{n}(a_{r})}^{(L)}U_{a_{r}}^{(R)}U_{\text{SWAP}}^{n}
for all
a_{r}\in A
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
Thus, the unbroken symmetry of
\ket{\text{GS};a}
is

\{((af_{0}(a))^{n}f_{n}(a_{r}),a_{r},n)\mid a_{r}\in A,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\}=(a,e,0)\cdot K(f)\cdot(a,e,0)^{-1}.

(VIII.28)

In particular, the unbroken symmetry of
\ket{\text{GS};e}
is
K=K(f)
.

Generalized cluster model.

The generalized cluster model with Tambara-Yamagami symmetry
2\text{TY}(A,1)
is obtained by gauging the subgroup symmetry
H=A_{L}
in the above model.
To describe the model, we choose a gauge so that the matter fields after the gauge fixing take values in
65
65

65

This choice of a gauge guarantees that the symmetry operators of the gauged model can be written as tensor networks with small bond dimensions. We refer the reader to Section
IV.1.3
for more details on this point. Another choice of a gauge will be discussed in Appendix
F
.

S_{H\backslash G}=A^{\text{diag}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}:=\{(a,a,n)\mid a\in A,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\}.

(VIII.29)

The state space of the gauged model after the gauge fixing is

\mathcal{H}_{\text{g.f.}}=\left(\bigotimes_{i\in P}\mathbb{C}^{|A^{\text{diag}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}|}\right)\otimes\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}\left(\bigotimes_{[ij]\in E}\mathbb{C}^{|A_{L}|}\right),

(VIII.30)

where
\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}
is the projector that imposes the flatness condition on the gauge fields.
The matter field on plaquette
i\in P
and the gauge field on edge
[ij]\in E
are labeled by
(a_{i},a_{i},n_{i})\in A^{\text{diag}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
(a_{ij},e,0)\in A_{L}
.
The corresponding states are simply written as
\ket{a_{i},n_{i}}_{i}
and
\ket{a_{ij}}_{ij}
, respectively.
The basis states of (
VIII.30
) will be denoted by

\ket{\{a_{i},n_{i};a_{ij}\}}:=\left(\bigotimes_{i\in P}\ket{a_{i},n_{i}}_{i}\right)\otimes\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}\left(\bigotimes_{[ij]\in E}\ket{a_{ij}}_{ij}\right).

(VIII.31)

The Hamiltonian of the model is

H_{\text{g.f.}}=-\sum_{i\in P}\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}^{\text{g.f.}}-\sum_{[ij]\in E}\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}^{\text{g.f.}},

(VIII.32)

where
\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}^{\text{g.f.}}
and
\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}^{\text{g.f.}}
are defined by

\displaystyle\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}^{\text{g.f.}}\Ket{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2|A|}\sum_{a\in A}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\Ket{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}},

(VIII.33)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}^{\text{g.f.}}\ket{a_{i},n_{i}}_{i}\otimes\ket{a_{ij}}_{ij}\otimes\ket{a_{j},n_{j}}_{j}

\displaystyle=\delta_{a_{ij},\widetilde{f}_{n_{i}}(a_{i})^{-1}\widetilde{f}_{n_{j}}(a_{j})}\ket{a_{i},n_{i}}_{i}\otimes\ket{a_{ij}}_{ij}\otimes\ket{a_{j},n_{j}}_{j}.

(VIII.34)

Here, the map
\widetilde{f}_{n}:A\to A
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is defined by

\widetilde{f}_{n}(a):=f_{n}(a)a^{-1}.

(VIII.35)

The above Hamiltonian has a unique ground state on a square lattice with periodic boundary conditions.
The ground state can be written explicitly as

\ket{\text{2TY-cluster}(A;f)}:=\frac{1}{(2|A|)^{|P|/2}}\sum_{\{a_{i}\in A\}}\sum_{\{n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\}}\ket{\{a_{i},n_{i};\widetilde{f}_{n_{i}}(a_{i})^{-1}\widetilde{f}_{n_{j}}(a_{j})\}}.

(VIII.36)

A tensor network representation of the above ground state is

\ket{\text{2TY-cluster}(A;f)}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},

(VIII.37)

where the local tensors represented by the black and white dots are defined by

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2|A|}}\prod_{i=1,2,3,4}\delta_{a_{i},\widetilde{f}_{n}(a)},\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{a,a_{1}^{-1}a_{2}}.

(VIII.38)

VIII.3
Symmetry operators

In this subsection, we write down the symmetry operators of the Tambara-Yamagami cluster model defined above.
We will also compute the action tensors for the symmetry operators acting on the Tambara-Yamagami cluster state (
VIII.36
).

1-form symmetry.

The 1-form symmetry operators of the model are labeled by 1-endomorphisms of the unit object
I\in 2\text{TY}(A,1)
.
The equivalence (
VIII.2
) implies that these 1-morphisms form a symmetric fusion 1-category

\text{End}_{2\text{TY}(A,1)}(I)\cong\text{Rep}(A).

(VIII.39)

The symmetry operator corresponding to a simple object
\widehat{a}\in\text{Rep}(A)
is the Wilson line for
\widehat{a}
.
The Wilson line
W_{\widehat{a}}(\gamma)
supported on an oriented loop
\gamma
on the dual lattice is given by

W_{\widehat{a}}(\gamma)=\prod_{[ij]\in E_{\gamma}}(Z_{\widehat{a}}^{(ij)})^{s_{ij}},

(VIII.40)

where
E_{\gamma}
is the set of the edges that intersect
\gamma
, and
s_{ij}
is either
+1
or
-1
depending on the relative orientation of
\gamma
and
[ij]
, cf. (
IV.4
).

0-form symmetry.

The 0-form symmetry operators are labeled by objects of
2\text{TY}(A,1)
.
The equivalence (
VIII.2
) implies that any 0-form symmetry operator, up to condensation, can be written as

\mathsf{C}U_{a}\quad\text{or}\quad\mathsf{D},\qquad\forall a\in A,

(VIII.41)

where
\mathsf{C}
,
U_{a}
, and
\mathsf{D}
are the symmetry operators corresponding to simple objects of
2\text{Rep}(A)
,
2\text{Vec}_{A}
, and
2\text{Vec}
in (
VIII.2
).
Each operator in (
VIII.41
) is given by
66
66

66

The expression of the invertible symmetry operator
U_{a}
generally depends on a gauge choice. Nevertheless, the product
\mathsf{C}U_{a}
has a gauge-independent expression
\mathsf{C}U_{a}=\mathsf{D}_{A_{L}}\left(\bigotimes_{i\in P}X_{a}^{(i;R)}\right)\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{A_{L}}
. We note that the action of
\mathsf{C}U_{a}
still depends on a gauge choice because
\mathsf{D}_{A_{L}}
and
\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{A_{L}}
do. Similarly, the action of
\mathsf{D}
also depends on a gauge.

\mathsf{C}:=\mathsf{D}_{A_{L}}\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{A_{L}},\qquad U_{a}:=\bigotimes_{i\in P}X_{a}^{(i)},\qquad\mathsf{D}:=\mathsf{D}_{A_{L}}\left(\bigotimes_{i\in P}\sigma_{x}^{(i)}\text{SWAP}_{A}^{(i)}\right)\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{A_{L}}.

(VIII.42)

Here,
\mathsf{D}_{A_{L}}
and
\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{A_{L}}
denote the gauging and ungauging operators defined by

\mathsf{D}_{A_{L}}\ket{\{a_{i},b_{i},n_{i}\}}=\ket{\{b_{i},n_{i};b_{i}a_{i}^{-1}a_{j}b_{j}^{-1}\}},\qquad\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{A_{L}}=\mathsf{\mathsf{D}}_{A_{L}}^{\dagger}.

(VIII.43)

The actions of the above symmetry operators can then be computed explicitly as

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}U_{a}\ket{\{a_{i},n_{i};a_{ij}\}}

\displaystyle=|A|\delta_{\text{hol}}(\{a_{ij}\})\ket{\{aa_{i},n_{i};a_{ij}\}},

(VIII.44)

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}\ket{\{a_{i},n_{i};a_{ij}\}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\{b_{i}\in A\}}\left(\prod_{[ij]\in E}\delta_{a_{ij},b_{i}^{-1}b_{j}}\right)\ket{\{a_{i}b_{i},n_{i}+1;a_{ij}^{-1}\}},

where
\delta_{\text{hol}}(\{a_{ij}\})
is equal to
1
if the holonomy of the gauge field is trivial and
0
otherwise.

Tensor network representation.

The above symmetry operators can be represented by the tensor networks of the form

\mathsf{C}U_{a}\penalty\ \text{or}\penalty\ \mathsf{D}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},

(VIII.45)

where the virtual bonds take values in
A
.
The local tensors of
\mathsf{C}U_{a}
are given by

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{a^{\prime\prime},aa^{\prime}}\delta_{n^{\prime\prime},n^{\prime}}\prod_{i,j=1,2,3,4}\delta_{a_{i},a_{j}},\quad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{a^{\prime},a_{1}^{-1}a_{2}}\delta_{a^{\prime\prime},a^{\prime}}.

(VIII.46)

On the other hand, the local tensors of
\mathsf{D}
are given by

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{a^{\prime\prime},a_{1}a^{\prime}}\delta_{n^{\prime\prime},n^{\prime}+1}\prod_{i,j=1,2,3,4}\delta_{a_{i},a_{j}},\quad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{a^{\prime},a_{1}^{-1}a_{2}}\delta_{a^{\prime\prime},(a^{\prime})^{-1}}.

(VIII.47)

The other symmetry operators can be obtained by condensing line operators on
\mathsf{C}U_{a}
as in (
IV.50
).
When
A=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, the tensor network representation of
\mathsf{D}
has been obtained in
Cao:2025qhg
.

Action tensors.

The actions of the symmetry operators
\mathsf{C}U_{a}
and
\mathsf{D}
on the local tensors of the Tambara-Yamagami cluster state (
VIII.36
) can be computed as

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\penalty\ =\sum_{b\in A}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\penalty\ =\sum_{b\in A}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},

(VIII.48)

where the action tensors
\phi_{f}[\mathsf{X}]_{b}
and
\overline{\phi_{f}}[\mathsf{X}]_{b}
for
\mathsf{X}=\mathsf{C}U_{a}
and
\mathsf{X}=\mathsf{D}
are given by

\displaystyle=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{a_{1}a_{3}^{-1},ba^{-1}}\delta_{a_{2}a_{3}^{-1},bf_{0}(a^{-1})},

(VIII.49)

\displaystyle=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{a_{1}a_{3},b}\delta_{f_{1}(a_{3}a_{2}^{-1}),b}.

(VIII.50)

These action tensors are obtained by applying the general formula (
IV.60
) to the current example.
67
67

67

Using the notations in Section
IV
, we can write
\mathsf{C}U_{a}=\mathsf{D}[V_{A_{L}}^{(e,a,0)}]
,
\mathsf{D}=\mathsf{D}[V_{A_{L}}^{(e,e,1)}]
,
\phi_{f}[\mathsf{C}U_{a}]_{b}=\phi_{K(f)}[V_{A_{L}}^{(e,a,0)}]_{(b,e,0)}
, and
\phi_{f}[\mathsf{D}]_{b}=\phi_{K(f)}[V_{A_{L}}^{(e,e,1)}]_{(b,e,0)}
.

VIII.4
Interfaces: the case of
A=\mathbb{Z}_{2}

Let us consider the interfaces between the generalized cluster states with
2\text{TY}(A,1)
symmetry.
For simplicity, we will focus on the case of
A=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
In this case, the symmetry category is
Decoppet:2023bay
;
Bhardwaj:2022maz
;
Bartsch:2022ytj
;
Bartsch:2022mpm

2\text{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2},1)\cong 2\text{Rep}((\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{[1]}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{[1]})\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{[0]}).

(VIII.51)

The generalized cluster states with this symmetry are classified by the equivalence classes of pairs of maps
(f_{0},f_{1})
that satisfy the following conditions:

•

f_{0}:\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is an involutive group homomorphism

•

f_{1}(e)\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is a fixed point of
f_{0}
, i.e.,
f_{0}(f_{1}(e))=f_{1}(e)

•

f_{1}:\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is determined by
f_{1}(a)=f_{0}(a)f_{1}(e)
for all
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

The above conditions have only two solutions
f^{[0]}
and
f^{[1]}
, which are defined by

f^{[0]}_{n}(a)=a,\qquad f_{n}^{[1]}(a)=a\eta^{n}.

(VIII.52)

Here,
\eta
denotes the generator of
A=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
68
68

68

We recall that the elements of
A
are denoted multiplicatively, i.e.,
\eta^{2}=e
.

The corresponding complements of
A_{L}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
in
A\wr\mathbb{Z}_{2}\cong D_{8}
are

\displaystyle K(f^{[0]})

\displaystyle=\{(a,a,n)\mid a\in\mathbb{Z}_{2},n\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{diag}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2},

(VIII.53)

\displaystyle K(f^{[1]})

\displaystyle=\{(a\eta^{n},a,n)\mid a\in\mathbb{Z}_{2},n\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{4}.

(VIII.54)

The above solutions are not equivalent to each other because there does not exist
a\in A
that satisfies (
VIII.4
).
Therefore, the corresponding generalized cluster states are in different SPT phases.
69
69

69

Lattice models for these two SPT phases have already been constructed in
Choi:2024rjm
. One of these models is the ordinary
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-cluster model, which realizes the SPT phase corresponding to
K=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{diag}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. The other model is called the cluster
′
model in
Choi:2024rjm
, which is slightly different from our generalized cluster model. As we will see in Appendix
G
, the cluster
′
model realizes the SPT phase corresponding to
K=\mathbb{Z}_{4}
. It was shown in
Furukawa:2025flp
that the interface of these models must be degenerate, which is consistent with our results. We also mention that Ref.
Cao:2025qhg
studied an interface of
2\text{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2},1)
SPT phases with different cocycles
\lambda
, which we do not discuss in this paper.

In what follows, we will consider the interface between these two SPT phases, as well as the self-interface of each of them.

The models.

Let us first write down the generalized cluster models and their ground states for
K=K(f^{[0]})
and
K=K(f^{[1]})
.
To this end, as in Section
VIII.2
, we choose a gauge so that the matter fields take values in

S_{H\backslash G}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{diag}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}.

(VIII.55)

The state space of the model for both
K=K(f^{[0]})
and
K=K(f^{[1]})
is given by

\mathcal{H}_{\text{g.f.}}=\left(\bigotimes_{i\in P}\mathbb{C}^{|\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}|}\right)\otimes\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}\left(\bigotimes_{[ij]\in E}\mathbb{C}^{|\mathbb{Z}_{2}|}\right).

(VIII.56)

We note that each plaquette
i\in P
has a pair of qubits
(a_{i},n_{i})\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, while each edge
[ij]\in E
has a single qubit
a_{ij}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
The Hamiltonians for
K=K(f^{[0]})
and
K=K({f^{[1]}})
are given by

\displaystyle H_{\text{g.f.}}^{[0]}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i\in P}\frac{1}{4}(1+X^{(i)})(1+\sigma_{x}^{(i)})-\sum_{[ij]\in E}\frac{1}{2}(1+Z^{(ij)}),

(VIII.57)

\displaystyle H_{\text{g.f.}}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i\in P}\frac{1}{4}(1+X^{(i)})(1+\sigma_{x}^{(i)}\prod_{[ij]\in\partial i}X^{(ij)})-\sum_{[ij]\in E}\frac{1}{2}(1+\sigma_{z}^{(i)}Z^{(ij)}\sigma_{z}^{(j)}).

Here,
X^{(i)}
and
Z^{(i)}
are the Pauli operators acting on the qubit
a_{i}
on the plaquette
i
,
\sigma_{x}^{(i)}
and
\sigma_{z}^{(i)}
are those acting on the qubit
n_{i}
on the plaquette
i
, and
X^{(ij)}
and
Z^{(ij)}
are those acting on the qubit
a_{ij}
on the edge
[ij]
.
The ground states of the above Hamiltonians are

\displaystyle\ket{\text{$2\text{TY}$-cluster}(\mathbb{Z}_{2};f^{[0]})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{|P|}}\sum_{\{a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\}}\sum_{\{n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\}}\ket{\{a_{i},n_{i};e\}},

(VIII.58)

\displaystyle\ket{\text{$2\text{TY}$-cluster}(\mathbb{Z}_{2};f^{[1]})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{|P|}}\sum_{\{a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\}}\sum_{\{n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\}}\ket{\{a_{i},n_{i};\eta^{n_{i}+n_{j}}\}}.

We note that
\ket{\text{$2\text{TY}$-cluster}(\mathbb{Z}_{2};f^{[0]})}
is the trivial product state,
70
70

70

This is because we chose a gauge so that
S_{H\backslash G}=K(f^{[0]})
. See Appendix
F
for the models in another gauge.
while
\ket{\text{$2\text{TY}$-cluster}(\mathbb{Z}_{2};f^{[1]})}
is the tensor product of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-cluster state and the trivial product state.

Category theoretical description of interfaces.

As discussed in Section
V.1
, the symmetry at the interface of the generalized cluster states is generally described by the strip 2-algebra (
V.7
).
The strip 2-algebra for the interface of the SPT phases labeled by the complements
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
is denoted by
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
.
In the case of
2\text{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2},1)
SPT phases,
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
are either
K(f^{[0]})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{diag}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
or
K(f^{[1]})=\mathbb{Z}_{4}
.
Based on the results in Section
V.1.1
, we can compute
\mathcal{C}_{K_{1},K_{2}}
for each pair of
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{K(f^{[0]}),K(f^{[0]})}

\displaystyle\cong\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\oplus\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}},

(VIII.59)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{K(f^{[0]}),K(f^{[1]})}

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{C}_{K(f^{[1]}),K(f^{[0]})}\cong\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\boxtimes\text{Mat}_{2}(\text{Vec}),

(VIII.60)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{K(f^{[1]}),K(f^{[1]})}

\displaystyle\cong\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}\oplus\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}.

(VIII.61)

We note that
\mathcal{C}_{K(f^{[0]}),K(f^{[0]})}\not\cong\mathcal{C}_{K(f^{[1]}),K(f^{[1]})}
, which implies that the symmetry at the self-interface depends on the phase.
This is in contrast to the case of SPT phases with invertible symmetries.

Tensor network description of interfaces.

We now provide the tensor network description of the above interface symmetries.
For simplicity, we will focus on the interfaces represented by the tensor networks of the form (
V.51
).
To obtain the symmetry operators at the interface, we first write down the action tensors.
Due to (
VIII.49
) and (
VIII.50
), the action tensors for the symmetry operators
\mathsf{C}U_{a}
and
\mathsf{D}
acting on
\ket{\text{$2\text{TY}$-cluster}(\mathbb{Z}_{2};f^{[m]})}
are given by

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{a_{1},a_{2}}\delta_{a_{3},a_{1}ab},\quad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{a_{1},a_{2}\eta^{m}}\delta_{a_{3},a_{1}b}.

(VIII.62)

Using these action tensors, we can construct the symmetry operators acting on the interface of
\ket{\text{$2\text{TY}$-cluster}(\mathbb{Z}_{2};f^{[m]})}
and
\ket{\text{$2\text{TY}$-cluster}(\mathbb{Z}_{2};f^{[m^{\prime}]})}
as

\hat{\mathsf{L}}(\mathsf{C}U_{a};b,b^{\prime})=\bigotimes_{i:\text{sites}}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}(\mathsf{C}U_{a};b,b^{\prime}),\qquad\hat{\mathsf{L}}(\mathsf{D};b,b^{\prime})=\bigotimes_{i:\text{sites}}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}(\mathsf{D};b,b^{\prime}),

(VIII.63)

where the on-site operators
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}(\mathsf{C}U_{a};b,b^{\prime})
and
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}(\mathsf{D};b,b^{\prime})
are defined by

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}(\mathsf{C}U_{a};b,b^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\frac{1+\text{sgn}(bb^{\prime})Z^{(i)}_{\ell}Z^{(i)}_{r}}{2},

(VIII.64)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}(\mathsf{D};b,b^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\frac{1+\text{sgn}(bb^{\prime})Z^{(i)}_{\ell}Z^{(i)}_{r}}{2}(X^{(i)}_{\ell})^{m}(X^{(i)}_{r})^{m^{\prime}}.

(VIII.65)

The operators on the right-hand side act on the middle two legs of the middle diagram.
71
71

71

The small gap in the middle represents the interface.

More specifically,
X^{(i)}_{\ell}
and
Z^{(i)}_{\ell}
are the Pauli operators acting on the left leg, while
X^{(i)}_{r}
and
Z^{(i)}_{r}
are those acting on the right leg.
The sign
\text{sgn}(bb^{\prime})=\pm 1
is the sign representation of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
By plugging (
VIII.64
) and (
VIII.65
) into (
VIII.63
), we obtain

\hat{\mathsf{L}}(\mathsf{C}U_{a};b,b^{\prime})=P_{\text{sgn}(bb^{\prime})},\qquad\hat{\mathsf{L}}(\mathsf{D};b,b^{\prime})=P_{\text{sgn}(bb^{\prime})}X_{\ell}^{m}X_{r}^{m^{\prime}},

(VIII.66)

where
P_{\pm}:=\bigotimes_{i:\text{sites}}\frac{1}{2}(1\pm Z_{\ell}^{(i)}Z_{r}^{(i)})
,
X_{\ell}:=\bigotimes_{i:\text{sites}}X_{\ell}^{(i)}
, and
X_{r}:=\bigotimes_{i:\text{sites}}X_{r}^{(i)}
.

Equation (
VIII.66
) shows that the set of the symmetry operators acting on the interface of
\ket{\text{$2\text{TY}$-cluster}(\mathbb{Z}_{2};f^{[m]})}
and
\ket{\text{$2\text{TY}$-cluster}(\mathbb{Z}_{2};f^{[m^{\prime}]})}
is given by

\left\{P_{+},\quad P_{-},\quad P_{+}X_{\ell}^{m}X_{r}^{m^{\prime}},\quad P_{-}X_{\ell}^{m}X_{r}^{m^{\prime}}\right\}.

(VIII.67)

This set agrees with the set of symmetry operators (
VII.51
) at the interface of
\ket{\text{$\mathbb{Z}_{2}$-cluster}(f^{(m)})}
and
\ket{\text{$\mathbb{Z}_{2}$-cluster}(f^{(m^{\prime})})}
.
The algebra of these symmetry operators is already computed in Section
VII.4
.
Nevertheless, for completeness, let us describe it again for each pair of
m,m^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
.

•

When
(m,m^{\prime})=(0,0)
, the set of the symmetry operators reduces to

\left\{P_{+},\quad P_{-}\right\}.

(VIII.68)

The algebra of these symmetry operators is given by (
VII.53
), which suggests that the symmetry operators form a multifusion category
\text{Vec}\oplus\text{Vec}
.
The action of the symmetry category (
VIII.59
) is thus implemented via a tensor functor
\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\oplus\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\to\text{Vec}\oplus\text{Vec}
.

•

When
(m,m^{\prime})=(0,1)
, the set of the symmetry operators is

\left\{P_{+},\quad P_{-},\quad P_{+}X_{r},\quad P_{-}X_{r}\right\}.

(VIII.69)

The algebra of these symmetry operators is given by (
VII.55
), which suggests that the symmetry operators form a multifusion category
\text{Mat}_{2}(\text{Vec})
.
The action of the symmetry category (
VIII.60
) is thus implemented via a tensor functor
\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\boxtimes\text{Mat}_{2}(\text{Vec})\to\text{Mat}_{2}(\text{Vec})
.

•

When
(m,m^{\prime})=(1,0)
, the set of symmetry operators is

\left\{P_{+},\quad P_{-},\quad P_{+}X_{\ell},\quad P_{-}X_{\ell}\right\}.

(VIII.70)

The algebra of these symmetry operators is isomorphic to that of (
VIII.69
).

•

When
(m,m^{\prime})=(1,1)
, the set of the symmetry operators is

\left\{P_{+},\quad P_{-},\quad P_{+}X_{\ell}X_{r},\quad P_{-}X_{\ell}X_{r}\right\}.

(VIII.71)

The algebra of these symmetry operators is given by (
VII.58
), which suggests that the symmetry operators form a multifusion category
\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\boxtimes\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
.
The action of the symmetry category (
VIII.61
) is thus implemented via a tensor functor
\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}\oplus\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}\to\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\oplus\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
.

VIII.5
Parameterized families

In this subsection, we consider
S^{1}
-parameterized families of
2\text{TY}(A,1)
-symmetric states in the same phase as
\ket{\text{$2\text{TY}$-cluster}(A;f)}
.

Classification.

Due to the general classification mentioned in Section
VI
, the
S^{1}
-parameterized families are expected to be classified by

\{(h,[\xi])\mid h\in H\text{ s.t. }K=hKh^{-1},\penalty\ [\xi]\in H^{2}(K,\mathrm{U}(1))\},

(VIII.72)

where
H=A_{L}
and
K=K(f)
.
In what follows, we will only consider
S^{1}
-parameterized families with trivial
[\xi]
.
The condition
K=hKh^{-1}
for
h=(b,e,0)\in A_{L}
means that for any
(f_{n}(a),a,n)\in K(f)
, there exists
(f_{n^{\prime}}(a^{\prime}),a^{\prime},n^{\prime})\in K(f)
such that

(b,e,0)(f_{n}(a),a,n)(b,e,0)^{-1}=(f_{n^{\prime}}(a^{\prime}),a^{\prime},n^{\prime}).

(VIII.73)

When
n=0
, this condition produces no constraint on
b\in A
.
When
n=1
, the above condition is equivalent to

(bf_{1}(a),ab^{-1},1)=(f_{1}(ab^{-1}),ab^{-1},1)\Longleftrightarrow f_{0}(b)=b^{-1}.

(VIII.74)

Therefore,
h=(b,e,0)
satisfies
K=hKh^{-1}
if and only if
f_{0}(b)=b^{-1}
.
Below, we will simply write
h=(b,e,0)\in H
as
b
and
(f_{n}(a),a,n)\in K(f)
as
(a,n)
.

S^{1}
-parameterized families.

For each
b\in A
satisfying
f_{0}(b)=b^{-1}
, we can construct an
S^{1}
-parameterized family of
2\text{TY}(A,1)
-symmetric models by conjugating the Hamiltonian (
VIII.32
) with the following
\theta
-dependent unitary operator:

U_{\text{g.f.}}^{b}(\theta)\coloneq\prod_{i\in P}\left.u_{b}^{(i)}(\theta)\right|_{\text{g.f.}},\qquad\left.u_{b}^{(i)}(\theta)\right|_{\text{g.f.}}\coloneq\exp\left(\frac{\theta}{2\pi}\log\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{b}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}\right),

(VIII.75)

Here,
\theta
runs from
0
to
2\pi
, and
\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{b}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}
is defined by
72
72

72

This is an example of the dual of a domain wall creation operator, which we introduced in Section
VI
. See (
VI.20
) for the general expression of the dual of a domain wall creation operator.

\displaystyle\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{b}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}

\displaystyle=L^{(\partial i)}_{b^{-1}}\otimes P_{0}^{(i)}+L^{(\partial i)}_{b}\otimes X_{b}^{(i)}P_{1}^{(i)},

(VIII.76)

where
P_{n}^{(i)}
for
n=0,1
is the projection onto the subspace with
n_{i}=n
, i.e.,
P_{n}^{(i)}\coloneq\frac{1}{2}(1+(-1)^{n}\sigma_{z}^{(i)})
.
We choose the branch of the logarithm as

\log\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{b}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}=\sum_{s=0}^{\left|A\right|-1}i\frac{2\pi s}{\left|A\right|}P_{s}[\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{b}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}],

(VIII.77)

where
P_{s}[\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{b}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}]
is the projection onto the eigenspace of
\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{b}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}
with eigenvalue
e^{i\frac{2\pi s}{\left|A\right|}}
.
We note that
u_{b}^{(i)}(\theta)
interpolates between the identity operator and
\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{b}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}
.
In particular, at
\theta=2\pi
, the unitary operator in (
VIII.75
) reduces to

U_{\text{g.f.}}^{b}(2\pi)=\prod_{i\in P}\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{b}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}.

(VIII.78)

By a direct computation, we can show that
U_{\text{g.f.}}^{b}(2\pi)
commutes with the Hamiltonian (
VIII.32
) due to the condition
f_{0}(b)=b^{-1}
.
Therefore, conjugating (
VIII.32
) with
U_{\text{g.f.}}^{b}(\theta)
gives us a
2\pi
-periodic Hamiltonian.
This
2\pi
-periodic Hamiltonian preserves the
2\text{TY}(A,1)
symmetry because
U_{\text{g.f.}}^{b}(\theta)
commutes with the symmetry operators for all
\theta\in[0,2\pi]
.

Generalized Thouless pumps.

Let us discuss the generalized Thouless pump associated with the above
S^{1}
-parameterized family.
To this end, we consider a
\theta
-dependent textured Hamiltonian on an infinite plane, which is obtained by conjugating the Hamiltonian (
VIII.32
) by the truncated unitary operator

U_{\text{g.f.}}^{b;x>0}(\theta)\coloneq\prod_{i\in P_{>0}}\left.u_{b}^{(i)}(\theta)\right|_{\text{g.f.}}.

(VIII.79)

Here,
P_{>0}
denotes the set of plaquettes on the right half-plane.
The textured Hamiltonian commutes with the
2\text{TY}(A,1)
symmetry for any
\theta\in[0,2\pi]
because each local unitary
\left.u_{b}^{(i)}(\theta)\right|_{\text{g.f.}}
commutes with this symmetry.
A continuous family of the unique ground states of the textured Hamiltonians is simply given by

U_{\text{g.f.}}^{b;x>0}(\theta)\ket{\text{$2\text{TY}$-cluster}(A;f)}.

(VIII.80)

In particular, at
\theta=2\pi
, this ground state reduces to

\left(\prod_{i\in P_{>0}}\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{b}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}\right)\ket{\text{$2\text{TY}$-cluster}(A;f)},

(VIII.81)

To identify the pumped excitation at
\theta=2\pi
, we compute the action of
\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{b}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}
for each
i\in P_{>0}
by using the tensor networks.
To this end, we first recall that the local tensors of
\ket{\text{$2\text{TY}$-cluster}(A;f)}
are given by

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2|A|}}\prod_{i=1,2,3,4}\delta_{a_{i},\widetilde{f}_{n}(a)},\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{a,a_{1}^{-1}a_{2}},

(VIII.82)

where
\widetilde{f}_{0}(a)=f_{0}(a)a^{-1}
.
When the qubit on the plaquette
i
is in the state with
n_{i}=0
, the operator
\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{b}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}
acts only on the gauge field as the loop operator
L_{b^{-1}}^{(\partial i)}
.
This action can be pushed onto the virtual legs as

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$}\;\;=\;\;\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$}\;\;=\;\;\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$},

(VIII.83)

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$}\;\;=\;\;\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$}\;\;=\;\;\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$}.

On the other hand, when the qubit on the plaquette
i
is in the state with
n_{i}=1
, the operator
\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{b}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}
acts on both the gauge field and the matter field.
The action on the gauge field is given by
L_{b}^{(\partial i)}
, which can be pushed onto the virtual legs as in (
VIII.83
).
The action on the matter field is given by
X_{b}
, which can be pushed onto the virtual legs as

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2|A|}}\prod_{i=1,2,3,4}\delta_{a_{i},\widetilde{f}_{1}(a)b^{-2}}=\;\;\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$}.

(VIII.84)

Combining these actions, we obtain

\left.\overleftarrow{X}_{b}^{(i)}\right|_{\text{g.f.}}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$}\;\;=\;\;\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{1}[1]{{\hbox{\hskip-10.0pt\raise-10.0pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}}$}.

(VIII.85)

We note that this equation holds for both
n_{i}=0
and
n_{i}=1
.
By plugging this into (
VIII.81
), we find that the pumped excitation is given by the tensor product of
X_{b}
’s on the virtual bonds along
x=0
.
This agrees with the general result obtained in Section
VI
.
