Appendix A
Remarks on gauge choice

The generalized cluster model was defined by gauging a subgroup symmetry
H
in a
G
-symmetric model and then fixing the gauge.
The Hamiltonian and the symmetry operators of the model depend on the gauge choice.
In this appendix, we discuss this dependency.

We first recall that the gauge fixing is specified by the choice of representatives of right
H
-cosets in
G
.
As in the main text, we denote the set of representatives by
S_{H\backslash G}
.
As discussed in Section
III.1
, the state space
\mathcal{H}_{\text{g.f.}}
and the Hamiltonian
H_{\text{g.f.}}
after the gauge fixing are given by

\mathcal{H}_{\text{g.f.}}=\left(\bigotimes_{i\in P}\mathbb{C}^{|S_{H\backslash G}|}\right)\otimes\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}\left(\bigotimes_{[ij]\in E}\mathbb{C}^{|H|}\right),\qquad H_{\text{g.f.}}=\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}H_{\text{gauged}}\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}^{-1},

(A.1)

where
H_{\text{gauged}}
is the gauged Hamiltonian (
III.16
) before the gauge fixing, and
\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}:\mathcal{H}_{\text{gauged}}\to\mathcal{H}_{\text{g.f.}}
is an isomorphism (
III.25
) between the state spaces before and after the gauge fixing.

Now, let us consider changing the gauge, i.e., we choose another set of representatives of right
H
-cosets in
G
.
The new set of representatives is denoted by
S^{\prime}_{H\backslash G}
.
Due to (
A.1
), the Hamiltonian
H^{\prime}_{\text{g.f.}}
for this gauge is related to that for the previous gauge as

H^{\prime}_{\text{g.f.}}=\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{\text{g.f.}}H_{\text{gauged}}(\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{\text{g.f.}})^{-1}=\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{\text{g.f.}}\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}^{-1}H_{\text{g.f.}}(\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{\text{g.f.}}\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}^{-1})^{-1},

(A.2)

where
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{\text{g.f.}}
is the gauge-fixing isomorphism (
III.25
) for the new gauge.
The above equation shows that the Hamiltonians for different gauges are related by the gauge-changing operator
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{\text{g.f.}}\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}^{-1}
.
Using (
III.25
), we can compute the action of the gauge-changing operator explicitly as

\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{\text{g.f.}}\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}^{-1}\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}\ket{\{r_{i};h_{ij}\}}=\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}\ket{\{r_{i}^{\prime};\delta r_{i}h_{ij}\delta r_{j}^{-1}\}},

(A.3)

where
r_{i}\in S_{H\backslash G}
and
r_{i}^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}_{H\backslash G}
are the representatives of the same right
H
-coset in
G
, and
\delta r_{i}\in H
is the unique element of
H
that satisfies

r_{i}^{\prime}=\delta r_{i}r_{i}
.
Equation (
A.2
) implies that the ground state of
H^{\prime}_{\text{g.f.}}
is related to that of
H_{\text{g.f.}}
as

\ket{\text{cluster}(G;H;K)}^{\prime}=\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{\text{g.f.}}\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}^{-1}\ket{\text{cluster}(G;H;K)}.

(A.4)

The symmetry operators are also conjugated by
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{\text{g.f.}}\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}^{-1}
upon changing the gauge.
We emphasize that the symmetry category does not depend on the gauge choice, although the symmetry operators do.

Example.

As an example, let us consider the gauge-changing operator for

G=G_{0}\times G_{0},\qquad H=G_{0}^{\text{left}},\qquad S_{H\backslash G}=G_{0}^{\text{right}},\qquad S^{\prime}_{H\backslash G}=G_{0}^{\text{diag}},

(A.5)

where
G_{0}
is an arbitrary finite group.
As in the main text, the elements
(g,e)\in G_{0}^{\text{left}}
,
(e,g)\in G_{0}^{\text{right}}
, and
(g,g)\in G_{0}^{\text{diag}}
will be simply denoted by
g
.
Due to (
A.3
), the action of the gauge-changing operator in this case can be computed as

\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{\text{g.f.}}\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}^{-1}\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}\ket{\{g_{i};g_{ij}\}}=\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}\ket{\{g_{i};g_{i}g_{ij}g_{j}^{-1}\}}.

(A.6)

We note that this operator maps the
G_{0}
-cluster state (
III.55
) to the trivial product state.
A 1+1d analogue of such an operator was discussed in
Fechisin:2023odt
.

The symmetry of the gauged model is described by
2\text{Rep}(G_{0})\boxtimes 2\text{Vec}_{G_{0}}
irrespective of the gauge choice.
Nevertheless, the symmetry operators generally depend on the gauge choice.
For example, the invertible symmetry operators for the gauge choices
S_{H\backslash G}
and
S^{\prime}_{H\backslash G}
are given by

U_{g}=\bigotimes_{i\in P}\overrightarrow{X}_{g}^{(i)},\qquad U^{\prime}_{g}=\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{\text{g.f.}}\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}^{-1}U_{g}(\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{\text{g.f.}}\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}^{-1})^{-1}=\left(\bigotimes_{i\in P}\overrightarrow{X}_{g}^{(i)}\right)\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{[ij]\in E}\overrightarrow{X}_{g}^{(ij)}\overleftarrow{X}_{g^{-1}}^{(ij)}\right),

(A.7)

where
\overrightarrow{X}_{g}
and
\overleftarrow{X}_{g^{-1}}
are the left multiplication of
g
and the right multiplication of
g^{-1}
, respectively.
The above equation shows that
U_{g}=U^{\prime}_{g}
if and only if
G_{0}
is abelian.
On the other hand, the non-invertible condensation operator
\mathsf{C}
defined by (
VII.32
) commutes with the gauge-changing operator (
A.6
) for any
G_{0}
, and hence it does not depend on the gauge choice.

When
G_{0}
is abelian, the gauge-changing operator
\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}^{\prime}\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}^{-1}
is a symmetric disentangler for the
G_{0}
-cluster state.
73
73

73

On the other hand, when
G_{0}
is non-abelian,
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{\text{g.f.}}\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}^{-1}
is not a symmetric disentagler for the
G_{0}
-cluster state because it does not commute with the symmetry operators. When
G_{0}
is non-abelian, it is natural to expect that there is no symmetric disentangler for the
G_{0}
-cluster state because the symmetry category at the self-interface of the
G_{0}
-cluster state is not equivalent to that of the trivial product state. A similar argument for the non-existence of symmetric (dis)entanglers in 1+1d is found in
You:2025uxo
.

In particular, this operator changes the SPT phase: that is, the SPT phase realized in the gauged model depends on the gauge choice.
More specifically, in the gauge specified by
S_{H\backslash G}=G_{0}^{\text{right}}
, the model with
K=G_{0}^{\text{right}}
realizes the trivial phase,
74
74

74

The trivial phase is well-defined because the symmetry is invertible (up to condensation) when
G_{0}
is abelian.
while the model with
K=G_{0}^{\text{diag}}
realizes a non-trivial SPT phase.
On the other hand, in the gauge specified by
S_{H\backslash G}^{\prime}=G_{0}^{\text{diag}}
, the model with
K=G_{0}^{\text{right}}
realizes a non-trivial SPT phase, while the model with
K=G_{0}^{\text{diag}}
realizes the trivial phase.

Appendix B
Condensation surfaces

In this appendix, we consider the tensor network representations of condensation surfaces of the group-theoretical fusion 2-category
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
.
We will also discuss the condensation of line operators on some condensation surfaces.

B.1
Tensor network representations

The condensation surfaces are obtained by condensing line operators on the identity surface.
The condensed lines form a
\Delta
-separable symmetric Frobenius algebra in the endomorphism 1-category

\text{End}_{\mathcal{C}(G;H)}(I)\cong\text{Rep}(H),

(B.1)

where
I
denotes the unit object of
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
.
The surface operator obtained by condensing
A\in\text{Rep}(H)
will be denoted by
\mathsf{D}[{\mathrm{id}};A]
.
We note that
A
is a semisimple
H
-equivariant algebra.

The condensation surface
\mathsf{D}[{\mathrm{id}};A]
acting on the state space (
III.23
) can be represented by the following tensor network
Delcamp:2023kew
;
Vancraeynest-DeCuiper:2025wkh
:

\mathsf{D}[{\mathrm{id}};A]=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(B.2)

Here, the virtual Hilbert space is
A
, and the local tensors represented by the filled and unfilled dots are defined by

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},

(B.3)

where
m:A\otimes A\to A
and
\Delta:A\to A\otimes A
are the multiplication and comultiplication of
A
, and
\rho:H\to\text{Aut}(A)
is the action of
H
on
A
.
The physical legs for the matter fields are omitted in (
B.2
) because
\mathsf{D}[{\mathrm{id}};A]
acts only on the gauge fields.

When
A\in\text{Rep}(H)
is the dual group algebra
\mathbb{C}[H]^{*}
equipped with the regular
H
-action, the condensation surface
\mathsf{D}[{\mathrm{id}};A]
agrees with the symmetry operator
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{e}]
, i.e., we have

\mathsf{D}[{\mathrm{id}};\mathbb{C}[H]^{*}]=\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{e}].

(B.4)

Here, we recall that
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{e}]=\mathsf{D}_{H}\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{H}
, where
\mathsf{D}_{H}
and
\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{H}
are the gauging operator (
IV.15
) and the ungauging operator (
IV.22
).
To show (
B.4
), let us first spell out the
H
-equivariant algebra structure on
\mathbb{C}[H]^{*}
.

•

The multiplication
m
and comultiplication
\Delta
for
\mathbb{C}[H]^{*}
are given by

m(v^{h_{1}}\otimes v^{h_{2}})=\delta_{h_{1},h_{2}}v^{h_{1}},\qquad\Delta(v^{h_{1}})=v^{h_{1}}\otimes v^{h_{1}},

(B.5)

where
\{v^{h}\mid h\in H\}
is a basis of
\mathbb{C}[H]^{*}
.

•

The
H
-action
\rho:H\to\text{Aut}(\mathbb{C}[H]^{*})
is given by

\rho(h)(v^{h_{1}})=v^{h_{1}h^{-1}},

(B.6)

with respect to which
m
and
\Delta
in (
B.5
) are equivariant.

For the above choice of
A\in\text{Rep}(H)
, the local tensors defined by (
B.3
) reduce to

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{h,h_{1}^{-1}h_{2}}\delta_{h^{\prime},h},\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\prod_{i,j=1,2,3,4}\delta_{h_{i},h_{j}}.

(B.7)

These tensors agree with the local tensors (
IV.40
) and (
IV.38
) of the PEPO representation of
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]
with
g=e
.
75
75

75

The derivation of the local tensors (
IV.38
) and (
IV.40
) for
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]
with general
g\in G
is based on the assumption that
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
is non-anomalous. Nevertheless, when
g=e
, these expressions are applicable to any
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
.

Thus, we conclude that
\mathsf{D}[{\mathrm{id}};\mathbb{C}[H]^{*}]=\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{e}]
, which shows (
B.4
).

B.2
Condensation on a condensation surface

One can recover the identity surface by condensing line operators on the condensation surfaces.
In what follows, we will show that condensing the line operators forming the group algebra
\mathbb{C}[H]
on the condensation surface
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{e}]
gives us back the identity surface.
Namely, we will show

\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{e};\mathbb{C}[H]]=\mathsf{D}[{\mathrm{id}}],

(B.8)

where
\mathsf{D}[{\mathrm{id}}]
is the identity operator acting on the state space (
III.23
).

To show (
B.8
), we first recall that
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{e};\mathbb{C}[H]]
can be represented by the following PEPO (cf. (
IV.52
)):

\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{e};\mathbb{C}[H]]=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(B.9)

Here, all virtual bonds take values in
H
, and the local tensors in the above equation are defined by

\displaystyle=\delta_{r,r^{\prime}}\prod_{i,j=1,2,3,4}\delta_{h_{i},h_{j}},\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{h,h_{1}^{-1}h_{2}}\delta_{h,h^{\prime}},

(B.10)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|H|}\delta_{h_{1}h_{4},h_{2}h_{3}},\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(B.11)

Due to (
B.11
), the symmerty operator (
B.9
) can be decomposed into a sum

\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{e};\mathbb{C}[H]]=\frac{1}{|H|}\sum_{h_{x},h_{y}\in H}\delta_{h_{x}h_{y},h_{y}h_{x}}\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{e};\mathbb{C}[H]]_{h_{x},h_{y}},

(B.12)

where the summand on the right-hand side is defined by

\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{e};\mathbb{C}[H]]_{h_{x},h_{y}}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(B.13)

The action of
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{e};\mathbb{C}[H]]_{h_{x},h_{y}}
on a state
\ket{\{r_{i};h_{ij}\}}
can be computed as

\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{e};\mathbb{C}[H]]_{h_{x},h_{y}}\ket{\{r_{i};h_{ij}\}}=\sum_{h\in H}\delta_{\text{hol}_{x;*}(\{h_{ij}\}),h^{-1}h_{x}h}\delta_{\text{hol}_{y;*}(\{h_{ij}\}),h^{-1}h_{y}h}\ket{\{r_{i};h_{ij}\}},

(B.14)

where
\text{hol}_{a;*}(\{h_{ij}\})
for
a=x,y
is the holonomy of the gauge fields along the
a
direction with the base point
*
.
The above equation shows that the summand (
B.13
) is the projection to the subspace in which the gauge fields have the holonomy
h^{-1}h_{x}h
and
h^{-1}h_{y}h
along the
x
and
y
directions, where
h\in H
is arbitrary.
By substituting (
B.14
) into (
B.12
), we find

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{e};\mathbb{C}[H]]\ket{\{r_{i};h_{ij}\}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|H|}\sum_{h_{x},h_{y},h\in H}\delta_{h_{x}h_{y},h_{y}h_{x}}\delta_{\text{hol}_{x;*}(\{h_{ij}\}),h^{-1}h_{x}h}\delta_{\text{hol}_{y;*}(\{h_{ij}\}),h^{-1}h_{y}h}\ket{\{r_{i};h_{ij}\}}

(B.15)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|H|}\sum_{h\in H}\left(\sum_{h_{x},h_{y}\in H}\delta_{h_{x}h_{y},h_{y}h_{x}}\delta_{\text{hol}_{x;*}(\{h_{ij}\}),h_{x}}\delta_{\text{hol}_{y;*}(\{h_{ij}\}),h_{y}}\right)\ket{\{r_{i};h_{ij}\}}

\displaystyle=\ket{\{r_{i};h_{ij}\}}.

The last equality follows from
\sum_{h_{x},h_{y}\in H}\delta_{h_{x}h_{y},h_{y}h_{x}}\delta_{\text{hol}_{x;*}(\{h_{ij}\}),h_{x}}\delta_{\text{hol}_{y;*}(\{h_{ij}\}),h_{y}}=1
.
The above equation implies that
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{e}]
acts as the identity on the state space, which shows (
B.8
).
76
76

76

More specifically, the PEPO (
B.9
) acts as the projector
\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}
on the vector space
\left(\bigotimes_{i\in P}\mathbb{C}^{|S_{H\backslash G}|}\right)\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{[ij]\in E}\mathbb{C}^{|H|}\right)
and thus reduces to the identity operator on the state space (
III.23
).

Intuitively, one may think of the insertion of the line operators in (
B.9
) as summing over all possible holonomies of the gauged fields, which amounts to turning
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{e}]
into the identity surface
\mathsf{D}[{\mathrm{id}}]
.

Appendix C
Derivations

In this appendix, we derive several equalities of tensor network operators.
We will follow the notations used in the main text.

C.1
Fractionalized symmetries (
IV.55
)

To derive the fractionalized symmetry operator (
IV.55
), we first compute the action of
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]
on the following local patch of the generalized cluster PEPS (
III.46
):

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(C.1)

Since
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]
is given by the composition (
IV.9
) of three operators
\mathsf{D}_{H}
,
U_{g}
, and
\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{H}
, it suffices to compute the action of each of these operators consecutively.
We will later decompose the action of
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]
on (
C.1
) into the symmetry actions on the local tensors represented by the black and white dots.

Action of
\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{H}
.

The action of the ungauging operator
\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{H}
on the local patch (
C.1
) can be computed as

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},\qquad\text{where }\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}:=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(C.2)

The non-zero components of the gray tensor are given by

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|K|}},\qquad\forall h\in H,\penalty\ \forall k\in K.

(C.3)

We note that the PEPS generated by this tensor is the symmetric linear combination of the
G/K
-SSB ground states (
III.7
).
Thus, equation (
C.2
) is consistent with the global action (
IV.27
) of
\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{H}
on the generalized cluster state.

Action of
U_{g}
.

The action of the symmetry operator
U_{g}
on the gray tensor in (
C.2
) can be computed as

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},

(C.4)

where
U_{g;K}:H\to H
is defined by the condition

U_{g;K}(h)gh^{-1}\in K.

(C.5)

We note that
U_{g;K}(h)
satisfying the above condition is unique for any
h\in H
because
K
is a complement of
H
in
G
.
The map
U_{g;K}:H\to H
is invertible (cf. footnote
26
), and its inverse is denoted by
U_{g;K}^{-1}
.
Equation (
C.4
) makes it manifest that the PEPS generated by the gray tensor is
G
-symmetric.

Action of
\mathsf{D}_{H}
.

The action of the gauging operator
\mathsf{D}_{H}
on the gray tensor can be computed as

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(C.6)

The virtual bonds on the loop, as well as the other virtual bonds, have the bond dimension
|H|
, and the magenta dot on the right-hand side represents the copy tensor.

Action of
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]
.

Combining (
C.2
), (
C.4
), and (
C.6
), we find that the action of
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]
on the local patch (
C.1
) is given by

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(C.7)

The local tensors on the right-hand side are defined by

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}:=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\,,\qquad\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}:=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(C.8)

Equation (
C.7
) is not yet satisfactory in that it does not provide the symmetry action on each local tensor of the generalized cluster state.
In what follows, we obtain the action of
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]
on each local tensor by reducing the bond dimension of the symmetry PEPO as in (
IV.39
).

Reduction of bond dimension.

As we discussed in Section
IV.1.3
, one can reduce the bond dimension of the symmetry PEPO by using the three-leg tensors in (
IV.38
).
77
77

77

We recall that
\mathcal{C}(G;H)
is supposed to be non-anomalous, and hence the reduction of the bond dimension works.

Reducing the bond dimensions on both sides of (
C.7
) leads to

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(C.9)

The filled and unfilled green dots on the left-hand side are given by (
IV.38
) and (
IV.40
), while
\phi_{K}[V_{H}^{g}]
and
\overline{\phi_{K}}[V_{H}^{g}]
on the right-hand side are defined by (
IV.56
).
When the physical legs on the edges are labeled by the unit element
e\in H
, the above equation reduces to

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(C.10)

Here, we used the following equations:

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(C.11)

Equation (
C.10
) shows the action of
\mathsf{D}[V_{H}^{g}]
on the local tensor represented by the black dot.
Similarly, the symmetry action on the local tensor represented by the white dot is given by

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},

(C.12)

which follows from a direct computation as follows:

\displaystyle\text{LHS}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq: completeness}}{=}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\overset{\eqref{eq: unfilled}}{=}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}

(C.13)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq: fractionalization MPO}}{=}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\overset{\eqref{eq: fractionalization MPO}}{=}\text{RHS}.

In the first equality, we used the completeness relation (
C.15
), which we will show in the next subsection.
Equations (
C.10
) and (
C.12
) agree with (
IV.55
), and hence this completes the derivation.

C.2
Orthogonality and completeness (
IV.62
)

In this subsection, we show the following orthogonality and completeness relations for the action tensors:

\displaystyle=\delta_{h_{1},h_{2}}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},

(C.14)

\displaystyle\sum_{h\in H}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}

\displaystyle=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(C.15)

We recall that the action tensors
\phi_{K}[V_{H}^{g}]_{h}
and
\overline{\phi_{K}}[V_{H}^{g}]_{h}
are defined by (
IV.60
).

The orthogonality relation (
C.14
) can be verified by a direct computation as

\displaystyle\text{LHS}

\displaystyle=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}

(C.16)

\displaystyle=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{h_{1},h_{2}}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\text{RHS}.

Here, the first equality on the second line follows from the fact that the last diagram on the first line is non-zero only when the middle vertical line is labeled by the unit element
e\in H
.

The completeness relation (
C.15
) automatically follows from the orthogonality relation (
C.14
).
To see this, we note that (
C.14
) and (
C.15
) can be written respectively as

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\,,

(C.17)

where the four-leg tensors
\Phi_{K}[V_{H}^{g}]
and
\overline{\Phi_{K}}[V_{H}^{g}]
are defined by

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\coloneq\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$},\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\coloneq\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\,.

(C.18)

The first equality (i.e., the orthogonality relation) of (
C.17
) shows that
\overline{\Phi_{K}}[V_{H}^{g}]
is the right inverse of
\Phi_{K}[V_{H}^{g}]\in\text{End}(\mathbb{C}[H]^{\otimes 2})
.
In general, the right inverse of a linear map
f\in\text{End}(V)
for a finite dimensional vector space
V
is also the left inverse of
f
.
Therefore,
\overline{\Phi_{K}}[V_{H}^{g}]
is also the left inverse of
\Phi_{K}[V_{H}^{g}]
.
This shows the second equality (i.e., the completeness relation) of (
C.17
).

C.3
Fusion rules (
V.58
)

In this subsection, we derive the fusion rules (
V.58
) of the symmetry operators acting on the interface of the generalized cluster states.
Since the symmetry operators at the interface are given by the tensor product of on-site operators as in (
V.54
), it suffices to compute the fusion rules of the on-site operators.
Using the tensor network representation (
V.55
), we can compute the fusion rules of the on-site operators as follows:

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}(g;h_{1},h_{2})\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}(g^{\prime};h_{1}^{\prime},h_{2}^{\prime})=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}

(C.19)

\displaystyle=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{h_{1}^{\prime\prime},h_{2}^{\prime\prime}}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}

\displaystyle=\delta_{h_{1}^{\prime\prime},h_{2}^{\prime\prime}}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

Here,
U_{g;K}
and
\widetilde{U}_{g}:=\widetilde{U}_{g;S_{H\backslash G}}
are defined by (
V.57
).
The unlabeled black and white dots in the above diagrams represent the copy and multiplication tensors.
On the second line of (
C.19
), we defined

h_{1}^{\prime\prime}:=(h_{1}^{\prime})^{-1}U^{-1}_{g;K_{1}}(h_{1}),\qquad h_{2}^{\prime\prime}:=(h_{2}^{\prime})^{-1}U^{-1}_{g;K_{2}}(h_{2}).

(C.20)

The operators
\overrightarrow{h}
and
\overleftarrow{h}
denote the left multiplication and right multiplication of
h
.
Using the relations

\widetilde{U}_{g}\circ\overrightarrow{h}\circ\widetilde{U}_{g^{\prime}}=\widetilde{U}_{ghg^{\prime}},\qquad U_{g;K}\circ\overleftarrow{h}\circ U_{g^{\prime};K}=U_{gh^{-1}g^{\prime};K},

(C.21)

we find that (
C.19
) reduces to

\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}(g;h_{1},h_{2})\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}(g^{\prime};h_{1}^{\prime},h_{2}^{\prime})=\delta_{h_{1}^{\prime\prime},h_{2}^{\prime\prime}}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\delta_{h_{1}^{\prime\prime},h_{2}^{\prime\prime}}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}(g^{\prime\prime};h_{1},h_{2}),

(C.22)

where
g^{\prime\prime}:=g(h_{1}^{\prime\prime})^{-1}g^{\prime}
.
This equation implies the fusion rules (
V.58
).

C.4
Symmetry operators for isomorphic objects (
V.62
)

In this subsection, we show (
V.62
), i.e., for any
h_{1},h_{2},h_{1}^{\prime},h_{2}^{\prime}\in H
and
g,g^{\prime}\in G
, we show

(h_{2}h_{1}^{-1},h_{1}gH)=(h_{2}^{\prime}(h_{1}^{\prime})^{-1},h_{1}^{\prime}g^{\prime}H)\penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \hat{\mathsf{L}}(g;h_{1},h_{2})=\hat{\mathsf{L}}(g^{\prime};h_{1}^{\prime},h_{2}^{\prime}),

(C.23)

where
\hat{\mathsf{L}}(g;h_{1},h_{2})
is the symmetry operator (
V.54
) acting on the interface of the generalized cluster states.
To show the above equation, we choose
(g,h_{1},h_{2})
and
(g^{\prime},h_{1}^{\prime},h_{2}^{\prime})
so that they satisfy

h_{2}h_{1}^{-1}=h_{2}^{\prime}(h_{1}^{\prime})^{-1},\qquad h_{1}gH=h_{1}^{\prime}g^{\prime}H.

(C.24)

We note that this equation also implies
h_{2}gH=h_{2}^{\prime}g^{\prime}H
.
Due to (
V.56
), the equality
\hat{\mathsf{L}}(g;h_{1},h_{2})=\hat{\mathsf{L}}(g^{\prime};h_{1}^{\prime},h_{2}^{\prime})
reduces to

\displaystyle U_{g;K_{2}}^{-1}(h_{2})(U_{g;K_{1}}^{-1}(h_{1}))^{-1}

\displaystyle=U_{g^{\prime};K_{2}}^{-1}(h_{2}^{\prime})(U_{g^{\prime};K_{1}}^{-1}(h_{1}^{\prime}))^{-1}

(C.25)

\displaystyle h_{1}\widetilde{U}_{g;S_{H\backslash G}}((U_{g;K_{1}}^{-1}(h_{1}))^{-1}h_{\ell})

\displaystyle=h_{1}^{\prime}\widetilde{U}_{g^{\prime};S_{H\backslash G}}((U_{g^{\prime};K_{1}}^{-1}(h_{1}^{\prime}))^{-1}h_{\ell}),\quad\forall h_{\ell}\in H,

(C.26)

where
U_{g;K}:H\to H
and
\widetilde{U}_{g;S_{H\backslash G}}:H\to H
are defined by (
V.57
).
Thus, it suffices to show (
C.25
) and (
C.26
) under the assumption (
C.24
).

We first show the first equality (
C.25
).
To this end, we recall that due to the definition of
U_{g;K}
, there exist unique
k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime}\in K_{1}
and
k_{2},k_{2}^{\prime}\in K_{2}
such that

\displaystyle h_{1}g

\displaystyle=k_{1}U_{g;K_{1}}^{-1}(h_{1}),

\displaystyle\qquad h_{2}g

\displaystyle=k_{2}U_{g;K_{2}}^{-1}(h_{2}),

(C.27)

\displaystyle h_{1}^{\prime}g^{\prime}

\displaystyle=k_{1}^{\prime}U_{g^{\prime};K_{1}}^{-1}(h_{1}^{\prime}),

\displaystyle h_{2}^{\prime}g^{\prime}

\displaystyle=k_{2}^{\prime}U_{g^{\prime};K_{2}}^{-1}(h_{2}^{\prime}).

(C.28)

Therefore, the left-hand side and the right-hand side of (
C.25
) can be written as

\text{LHS}=k_{2}^{-1}h_{2}h_{1}^{-1}k_{1},\qquad\text{RHS}=(k_{2}^{\prime})^{-1}h_{2}^{\prime}(h_{1}^{\prime})^{-1}k_{1}^{\prime}.

(C.29)

On the other hand, since
h_{1}gH=h_{1}^{\prime}g^{\prime}H
and
h_{2}gH=h_{2}^{\prime}g^{\prime}H
by assumption, it follows that

k_{1}=k_{1}^{\prime},\qquad k_{2}=k_{2}^{\prime},

(C.30)

Here, we used the fact that
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
are complements of
H
in
G
.
Assuming that
h_{2}h_{1}^{-1}=h_{2}^{\prime}(h_{1}^{\prime})^{-1}
as in (
C.24
), equations (
C.29
) and (
C.30
) lead to (
C.25
).

Next, we show the second equality (
C.26
).
To this end, we notice that the assumption
h_{1}gH=h_{1}^{\prime}g^{\prime}H
implies that there exists a unique
h\in H
such that

h_{1}^{\prime}g^{\prime}=h_{1}gh.

(C.31)

Due to (
C.27
) and (
C.30
), the element
h\in H
in (
C.31
) satisfies

U_{g^{\prime};K_{1}}^{-1}(h_{1}^{\prime})=U_{g;K_{1}}^{-1}(h_{1})h.

(C.32)

Using this relation, one can compute the right-hand side of (
C.26
) as

\displaystyle\text{RHS}

\displaystyle=h_{1}^{\prime}\widetilde{U}_{g^{\prime};S_{H\backslash G}}(h^{-1}(U_{g;K_{1}}^{-1}(h_{1}))^{-1}h_{\ell})=h_{1}^{\prime}\widetilde{U}_{g^{\prime}h^{-1};S_{H\backslash G}}((U_{g;K_{1}}^{-1}(h_{1}))^{-1}h_{\ell})

(C.33)

\displaystyle=h_{1}^{\prime}\widetilde{U}_{(h_{1}^{\prime})^{-1}h_{1}g;S_{H\backslash G}}((U_{g;K_{1}}^{-1}(h_{1}))^{-1}h_{\ell})=h_{1}\widetilde{U}_{g;S_{H\backslash G}}((U_{g;K_{1}}^{-1}(h_{1}))^{-1}h_{\ell})=\text{LHS}.

Here, the second equality follows from
\widetilde{U}_{g^{\prime};S_{H\backslash G}}(h^{-1}\bullet)=\widetilde{U}_{g^{\prime}h^{-1};S_{H\backslash G}}(\bullet)
, and the last equality follows from
\widetilde{U}_{(h_{1}^{\prime})^{-1}h_{1}g;S_{H\backslash G}}(\bullet)=(h_{1}^{\prime})^{-1}h_{1}\widetilde{U}_{g;S_{H\backslash G}}(\bullet)
, where
\bullet
denotes an arbitrary element of
H
.
The above equation shows (
C.26
).

Appendix D
Uniqueness of the action tensors

In this appendix, we show that the action tensors in (
IV.61
) that satisfy the orthogonality and completeness relations (
IV.62
) are essentially unique.
78
78

78

As shown in Appendix
C.2
, the completeness relation automatically follows from the orthogonality relation.

To this end, let us suppose that there exist two action tensors
\phi_{K}[V_{H}^{g}]
and
\psi_{K}[V_{H}^{g}]
that satisfy (
IV.61
), i.e.,

\displaystyle=\sum_{h\in H}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\sum_{h\in H}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\,,

(D.1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{h\in H}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\sum_{h\in H}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\,.

(D.2)

Due to the orthogonality relation for the action tensors, the above equations imply

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\,,\quad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\,,\quad\forall h_{1},h_{2}\in H,

(D.3)

where the two-leg tensor
(\phi\overline{\psi})^{g}_{h_{1},h_{2}}
is defined by

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\coloneq\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}.

(D.4)

In particular, the two-leg tensor
(\phi\overline{\psi})^{g}_{h_{1},h_{2}}
satisfies

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\,.

(D.5)

We note that the composite tensor consisting of the middle black dot and the four white dots surrounding it is injective (as a linear map from the virtual bonds to the physical legs).
79
79

79

It immediately follows from (
III.47
) that the composite tensor in (
D.5
) is surjective as a linear map from the physical legs to the virtual bonds. This in turn implies that it is injective as a linear map from the virtual bonds to the physical legs.

Therefore, the above equation implies that
(\phi\overline{\psi})^{g}_{h_{1},h_{2}}
is a scalar multiple of the identity map, i.e.,

(\phi\overline{\psi})^{g}_{h_{1},h_{2}}=f^{g}_{h_{1},h_{2}}{\mathrm{id}}_{\mathbb{C}[H]},\qquad f^{g}_{h_{1},h_{2}}\in\mathbb{C}.

(D.6)

Thus, by applying the completeness relation for the action tensors to (
D.4
), we find that
\phi_{K}[V_{H}^{g}]
and
\psi_{K}[V_{H}^{g}]
are related by

\phi_{K}[V_{H}^{g}]_{h_{1}}=\sum_{h_{2}\in H}f^{g}_{h_{1},h_{2}}\psi_{K}[V_{H}^{g}]_{h_{2}},\qquad\overline{\psi_{K}}[V_{H}^{g}]_{h_{2}}=\sum_{h_{1}\in H}f^{g}_{h_{1},h_{2}}\overline{\phi_{K}}[V_{H}^{g}]_{h_{1}}.

(D.7)

Similarly, by swapping the role of
\phi
and
\psi
in the above discussion, we can show that there exists a set of scalars
\overline{f}^{g}_{h_{1},h_{2}}\in\mathbb{C}
such that

\psi_{K}[V_{H}^{g}]_{h_{2}}=\sum_{h_{1}\in H}\overline{f}^{g}_{h_{2},h_{1}}\phi_{K}[V_{H}^{g}]_{h_{1}},\qquad\overline{\phi_{K}}[V_{H}^{g}]_{h_{1}}=\sum_{h_{2}\in H}\overline{f}^{g}_{h_{2},h_{1}}\overline{\psi_{K}}[V_{H}^{g}]_{h_{2}}.

(D.8)

The orthogonality relation for the action tensors implies that
f^{g}_{h_{1},h_{2}}
and
\overline{f}^{g}_{h_{2},h_{1}}
are related by

\sum_{h_{2}\in H}f^{g}_{h_{1},h_{2}}\overline{f}^{g}_{h_{2},h_{1}^{\prime}}=\delta_{h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime}}\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \overline{f}^{g}=(f^{g})^{-1}.

(D.9)

In particular,
f^{g}
is invertible as an
|H|\times|H|
matrix.
Equations (
D.7
) and (
D.8
) show that the action tensor
\phi_{K}[V_{H}^{g}]
is unique up to the basis transformation
f^{g}
.
Using the four-leg tensors defined by (
C.18
), we can write (
D.7
) and (
D.8
) more concisely as

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\,,\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}\,.

(D.10)

Appendix E
Multifusion categories

In this appendix, we review the basic properties of multifusion categories.

E.1
Basic facts

A multifusion category
\mathcal{D}
is a finite semsimple
\mathbb{C}
-linear rigid monoidal category, whose unit object is not necessarily simple.
The simple decomposition of the unit object
I\in\mathcal{D}
is given by

I=\bigoplus_{i=1,2,\cdots,n}I_{i},\qquad n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}.

(E.1)

The simple summands
\{I_{i}\mid i=1,2,\cdots,n\}
satisfy
I_{i}\otimes I_{j}\cong\delta_{i,j}I_{i}
.
The multifusion category
\mathcal{D}
is called a fusion category when
I
is simple, i.e., when
n=1
.

As a semisimple category,
\mathcal{D}
can be decomposed as

\mathcal{D}=\bigoplus_{i,j=1,2,\cdots,n}\mathcal{D}_{ij},

(E.2)

where
\mathcal{D}_{ij}
is the full subcategory consisting of objects
X\in\mathcal{D}
that satisfy
I_{i}\otimes X\cong X\otimes I_{j}\cong X
.
Any object
X_{ij}\in\mathcal{D}_{ij}
is isomorphic to an object of the form
I_{i}\otimes X\otimes I_{j}
for
X\in\mathcal{D}
.
The diagonal component
\mathcal{D}_{ii}
is a fusion category, while the off-diagonal component
\mathcal{D}_{ij}
is a
(\mathcal{D}_{ii},\mathcal{D}_{jj})
-bimodule category, with the bimodule action given by the tensor product in
\mathcal{D}
.
The decomposition (
E.2
) implies that an object of
\mathcal{D}
can be thought of as an
n\times n
matrix whose
(i,j)
-component is an object of
\mathcal{D}_{ij}
.
The tensor product in
\mathcal{D}
then corresponds to matrix multiplication.

A multifusion category
\mathcal{D}
is said to be indecomposable if it cannot be decomposed into a direct sum of two non-zero multifusion categories.
Any component
\mathcal{D}_{ij}
of an indecomposable multifusion category
\mathcal{D}
is not empty due to
(
EGNO2015
, Theorem 7.21.12)
.

E.2
Fiber functors

In this subsection, we show that an indecomposable multifusion category
\mathcal{D}
is a fusion category if it admits a fiber functor.
To this end, let us suppose that
\mathcal{D}
admits a fiber functor.
Equivalently, we suppose that
\mathcal{D}
admits a left module category
Vec
.
The left action of
\mathcal{D}
on
Vec
is denoted by

\vartriangleright:\mathcal{D}\times\text{Vec}\to\text{Vec}.

(E.3)

The unit object
I\in\mathcal{D}
acts as the identity up to isomorphism, i.e.,
I\vartriangleright M\cong M
for any
M\in\text{Vec}
.
In particular, for the unique simple object
\mathbb{C}\in\text{Vec}
, we have

I\vartriangleright\mathbb{C}=\bigoplus_{i=1,2,\cdots,n}I_{i}\vartriangleright\mathbb{C}\cong\mathbb{C}.

(E.4)

This equation implies that there exists
i_{0}\in\{1,2,\cdots,n\}
such that

I_{i}\vartriangleright\mathbb{C}\cong\delta_{i,i_{0}}\mathbb{C}.

(E.5)

This in turn implies that for any non-zero object
X_{ij}\in\mathcal{D}_{ij}
,
80
80

80

The category
\mathcal{D}_{ij}
is not empty for any
i
and
j
because
\mathcal{D}
is indecomposable.
we have

X_{ij}\vartriangleright\mathbb{C}\cong X_{ij}\vartriangleright(I_{j}\vartriangleright\mathbb{C})\cong\delta_{j,i_{0}}X_{ij}\vartriangleright\mathbb{C}.

(E.6)

On the other hand, for the dual object
\overline{X_{ij}}\in\mathcal{D}_{ji}
, we have

X_{ij}\vartriangleright(\overline{X_{ij}}\vartriangleright\mathbb{C})\cong(X_{ij}\otimes\overline{X_{ij}})\vartriangleright\mathbb{C}\supset I_{i}\vartriangleright\mathbb{C}\cong\delta_{i,i_{0}}\mathbb{C}.

(E.7)

If we choose
i=i_{0}
in (
E.6
), we find
X_{i_{0}j}\vartriangleright(\overline{X_{i_{0}j}}\vartriangleright\mathbb{C})\supset\mathbb{C}
, which shows that
X_{i_{0}j}\vartriangleright\mathbb{C}
is non-zero for any
j\in\{1,2,\cdots,n\}
.
However, equation (
E.6
) shows that
X_{i_{0}j}\vartriangleright\mathbb{C}
can be non-zero only when
j=i_{0}
.
Therefore, it follows that
n
must be one, which means that
\mathcal{D}
must be fusion.

Appendix F
Tambara-Yamagami cluster model in another gauge

In Section
VIII.2
, the generalized cluster model with Tambara-Yamagami symmetry
2\text{TY}(A,1)
is defined in a gauge where the matter fields take values in
S_{H\backslash G}=A^{\text{diag}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
In this appendix, we describe the model in a different gauge where the matter fields take values in
81
81

81

We note that
S^{\prime}_{H\backslash G}
in (
F.1
) is not a complement of
A_{L}
in
A\wr\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Thus, there is no obvious way to reduce the bond dimensions of the symmetry PEPOs as in
IV.1.3
.

S^{\prime}_{H\backslash G}=A^{\text{right}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}:=\{(e,a,n)\mid a\in A,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\}.

(F.1)

We will also discuss the relation between the Tambara-Yamagami cluster model in this gauge and the ordinary
A
-cluster model.

F.1
The model

The state space of the Tambara-Yamagami cluster model in the above gauge is

\mathcal{H}_{\text{g.f.}}=\left(\bigotimes_{i\in P}\mathbb{C}^{|A^{\text{right}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}|}\right)\otimes\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}\left(\bigotimes_{[ij]\in E}\mathbb{C}^{|A_{L}|}\right).

(F.2)

The matter field on plaquette
i\in P
is labeled by
(e,a_{i},n_{i})\in A^{\text{right}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, while the matter field on edge
[ij]\in E
is labeled by
(a_{ij},e,0)\in A_{L}
.
The corresponding states are denoted by
\ket{a_{i},n_{i}}_{i}
and
\ket{a_{ij}}_{ij}
, respectively.
The Hamiltonian of the model for
K=K(f)
is

H_{\text{g.f.}}=-\sum_{i\in P}\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}^{\text{g.f.}}-\sum_{[ij]\in E}\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}^{\text{g.f.}},

(F.3)

where
\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}
and
\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}
are defined by

\displaystyle\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{i}^{\text{g.f.}}\Ket{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2|A|}\sum_{a\in A}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\Ket{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{\hskip-10.03749pt\raise-10.03749pt\hbox{{}}}}}}}$}},

(F.4)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathsf{h}}_{ij}^{\text{g.f.}}\ket{a_{i},n_{i}}_{i}\otimes\ket{a_{ij}}_{ij}\otimes\ket{a_{j},n_{j}}_{j}

\displaystyle=\delta_{a_{ij},f_{n_{i}}(a_{i})^{-1}f_{n_{j}}(a_{j})}\ket{a_{i},n_{i}}_{i}\otimes\ket{a_{ij}}_{ij}\otimes\ket{a_{j},n_{j}}_{j}.

(F.5)

We note that the above Hamiltonian is obtained by replacing
\widetilde{f}
with
f
in (
VIII.33
) and (
VIII.34
).
Accordingly, the ground state of the Hamiltonian (
F.3
) is also obtained by replacing
\widetilde{f}
with
f
in (
VIII.36
), i.e.,

\ket{\text{$2\text{TY}$-cluster}(A;f)}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{(2|A|)^{|P|/2}}\sum_{\{a_{i}\in A\}}\sum_{\{n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\}}\ket{\{a_{i},n_{i};f_{n_{i}}(a_{i})^{-1}f_{n_{j}}(a_{j})\}}.

(F.6)

The Hamiltonian (
F.3
) is related to (
VIII.32
) by the gauge-changing operator (cf. Appendix
A
)

\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}^{\prime}\mathcal{U}_{\text{g.f.}}^{-1}\ket{a_{i},n_{i};a_{ij}}=\ket{a_{i},n_{i};a_{i}^{-1}a_{ij}a_{j}},

(F.7)

which is the entangler for the
A
-cluster state.

Symmetry operators.

The symmetry operators (
VIII.41
) in a general gauge can be written as

\mathsf{C}U_{a}=\mathsf{D}_{A_{L}}U_{a}^{(R)}\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{A_{L}},\qquad\mathsf{D}=\mathsf{D}_{A_{L}}U_{\text{SWAP}}\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{A_{L}},

(F.8)

where
\mathsf{D}_{A_{L}}
and
\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{A_{L}}
are the gauging and ungauging operators for
A_{L}\subset A\wr\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, and
U_{a}^{(R)}
and
U_{\text{SWAP}}
are the symmetry operators (
VIII.24
) of the ungauged model.
If we choose a gauge as in (
F.1
), the gauging and ungauging operators can be written explicitly as
82
82

82

We note that (
F.9
) differs from (
VIII.43
), meaning that the gauging operator depends on a gauge choice.

\mathsf{D}_{A_{L}}\ket{\{a_{i},b_{i},n_{i}\}}=\ket{\{b_{i},n_{i};a_{i}^{-1}a_{j}\}},\qquad\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{A_{L}}=\mathsf{D}_{A_{L}}^{\dagger}.

(F.9)

The action of the symmetry operators (
F.8
) can then be computed as

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}U_{a}\ket{\{a_{i},n_{i};a_{ij}\}}

\displaystyle=|A|\delta_{\text{hol}}(\{a_{ij}\})\ket{\{aa_{i},n_{i};a_{ij}\}},

(F.10)

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}\ket{\{a_{i},n_{i};a_{ij}\}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\{b_{i}\in A\}}\left(\prod_{[ij]\in E}\delta_{a_{ij},b_{i}^{-1}b_{j}}\right)\ket{b_{i},n_{i}+1;a_{i}^{-1}a_{j}},

(F.11)

where
\delta_{\text{hol}}(\{a_{ij}\})
is one if the gauge fields have the trivial holonomy and zero otherwise.
The above symmetry operators can be expressed more concisely as

\mathsf{C}=\mathsf{D}_{A_{L}}\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{A_{L}},\qquad U_{a}=\bigotimes_{i\in P}X_{a}^{(i)},\qquad\mathsf{D}=\mathsf{D}_{A_{R}}\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{A_{L}}\left(\bigotimes_{i\in P}\sigma_{x}^{(i)}\right),

(F.12)

where
\mathsf{D}_{A_{R}}
is the gauging operator for
A_{R}\subset A\wr\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, which is defined by

\mathsf{D}_{A_{R}}\ket{\{a_{i},b_{i},n_{i}\}}=\ket{\{a_{i},n_{i};b_{i}^{-1}b_{j}\}}.

(F.13)

We note that the composition
\mathsf{D}_{A_{R}}\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{A_{L}}
can be regarded as the gauging operator for the 2-group symmetry
A^{[0]}\times A^{[1]}
.

Example:
A=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

When
A=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, the state space (
F.2
) of the Tambara-Yamagami cluster model reduces to

\mathcal{H}_{\text{g.f.}}=\left(\bigotimes_{i\in P}\mathbb{C}^{|\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}|}\right)\otimes\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}\left(\bigotimes_{[ij]\in E}\mathbb{C}^{|\mathbb{Z}_{2}|}\right).

(F.14)

That is, we have a pair of qubits on each plaquette and a single qubit on each edge.
If we choose a gauge as in (
F.1
), the Hamiltonians for the two SPT phases corresponding to the complements (
VIII.53
) and (
VIII.54
) are given by

\displaystyle H_{\text{g.f.}}^{[0]}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i\in P}\frac{1}{4}(1+X^{(i)}\prod_{[ij]\in\partial i}X^{(ij)})(1+\sigma_{x}^{(i)})-\sum_{[ij]\in E}\frac{1}{2}(1+Z^{(i)}Z^{(ij)}Z^{(j)}),

(F.15)

\displaystyle H_{\text{g.f.}}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i\in P}\frac{1}{4}(1+X^{(i)}\prod_{[ij]\in\partial i}X^{(ij)})(1+\sigma_{x}^{(i)}\prod_{[ij]\in\partial i}X^{(ij)})-\sum_{[ij]\in E}\frac{1}{2}(1+\sigma_{z}^{(i)}Z^{(i)}Z^{(ij)}Z^{(j)}\sigma_{z}^{(j)}).

(F.16)

The ground states of these Hamiltonians can be written explicitly as

\displaystyle\ket{\text{$2\text{TY}$-cluster}(\mathbb{Z}_{2};f^{[0]})}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2^{|P|}}\sum_{\{a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\}}\sum_{\{n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\}}\ket{\{a_{i},n_{i};a_{i}^{-1}a_{j}\}},

(F.17)

\displaystyle\ket{\text{$2\text{TY}$-cluster}(\mathbb{Z}_{2};f^{[1]})}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2^{|P|}}\sum_{\{a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\}}\sum_{\{n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\}}\ket{\{a_{i},n_{i};a_{i}^{-1}a_{j}\eta^{n_{i}+n_{j}}\}},

(F.18)

where
\eta
is the generator of
A=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
We note that neither of these states is the trivial product state, as opposed to the case of the gauge choice (
VIII.55
).

F.2
Relation to the
A
-cluster model

If we choose a gauge as in (
F.1
), the Tambara-Yamagami cluster model for
K=A^{\text{diag}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
becomes almost the same as the ordinary
A
-cluster model.
Indeed, in this case, the Hamiltonian (
F.3
) reduces to

H_{\text{g.f}}=-\sum_{i\in P}\left(\frac{1}{|A|}\sum_{a\in A}X_{a}^{(i)}L_{a^{-1}}^{(\partial i)}\right)\frac{1+\sigma_{x}^{(i)}}{2}-\sum_{[ij]\in E}\frac{1}{|A|}\sum_{\widehat{a}\in\widehat{A}}Z_{\widehat{a}}^{(i)}Z_{\widehat{a}}^{(ij)}Z_{\widehat{a}^{-1}}^{(j)}.

(F.19)

Here,
\widehat{A}
is the Pontryagin dual of
A
,
\sigma_{x}
is the Pauli-X operator acting on the qubit on a plaquette,
L_{a^{-1}}
is the counterclockwise loop operator labeled by
a^{-1}
(cf. (
III.12
)), and
X_{a}
and
Z_{\widehat{a}}
are the generalized Pauli operators acting on the
A
-qudits as

X_{a}\ket{b}=\ket{ab}=\ket{ba},\qquad Z_{\widehat{a}}\ket{b}=\widehat{a}(b)\ket{b}.

(F.20)

The above Hamiltonian agrees with the Hamiltonian of the
A
-cluster model, except that the first term in (
F.19
) has an additional projector
\frac{1}{2}(1+\sigma_{x}^{(i)})
for all
i\in P
.
Thus, the ground state of this Hamiltonian is given by

\ket{\text{$A$-cluster}}\otimes\ket{\text{$\mathbb{Z}_{2}$-trivial}}=\frac{1}{(2|A|)^{|P|/2}}\sum_{\{a_{i}\in A\}}\sum_{\{n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\}}\ket{\{a_{i},n_{i};a_{i}^{-1}a_{j}\}},

(F.21)

where
\ket{\text{$A$-cluster}}:=\frac{1}{|A|^{|P|/2}}\sum_{\{a_{i}\in A\}}\ket{\{a_{i};a_{i}^{-1}a_{j}\}}
is the
A
-cluster state formed by the
A
-qudits on the plaquettes and edges, and
\ket{\text{$\mathbb{Z}_{2}$-trivial}}:=\bigotimes_{i}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sum_{n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\ket{n_{i}}_{i}
is the trivial product state formed by the qubits on the plaquettes.

It is clear from (
F.19
) that the model reduces to the
A
-cluster model if we project out the qubits on the plaquettes by the projector

\hat{\pi}_{\text{qubit}}:=\bigotimes_{i\in P}\frac{1}{2}(1+\sigma_{x}^{(i)}).

(F.22)

This projector commutes with the symmetry operators (
F.10
) and (
F.11
).
Therefore, the model after the projection, i.e., the
A
-cluster model, also has the Tambara-Yamagami symmetry
2\text{TY}(A,1)
.
This fact was originally pointed out in
Choi:2024rjm
in the case of
A=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
The symmetry operator
\mathsf{D}
on the projected subspace
\hat{\pi}_{\text{qubit}}\mathcal{H}_{\text{g.f.}}\cong\left(\bigotimes_{i\in P}\mathbb{C}^{|A|}\right)\otimes\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}\left(\bigotimes_{[ij]\in E}\mathbb{C}^{|A|}\right)
reduces to

\left.\mathsf{D}\right|_{\hat{\pi}_{\text{qubit}}\mathcal{H}_{\text{g.f.}}}=\mathsf{D}_{A_{R}}\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{A_{L}},

(F.23)

whose action can be written as

\mathsf{D}_{A_{R}}\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{A_{L}}\ket{\{a_{i};a_{ij}\}}=\sum_{\{b_{i}\in A\}}\left(\prod_{[ij]\in E}\delta_{a_{ij},b_{i}^{-1}b_{j}}\right)\ket{\{b_{i};a_{i}^{-1}a_{j}\}}.

(F.24)

On the other hand,
\mathsf{C}U_{a}
does not change under the projection.
When
A=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, the operator (
F.23
) agrees with the symmetry operator constructed in
Choi:2024rjm
(see Appendix
G.3
for more details).

Appendix G
Lattice models for
2\text{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2},1)
SPT phases in
Choi:2024rjm

Ref.
Choi:2024rjm
constructed two lattice models for 2+1d SPT phases with Tambara-Yamagami symmetry
2\text{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2},1)
.
One of them is the ordinary
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-cluster model, while the other one is called the cluster
′
model in
Choi:2024rjm
.
83
83

83

The cluster
′
model is called the
\beta
model in
Furukawa:2025flp
.

In what follows, we will review these models and identify the SPT phases.
We will also discuss the relation between the non-invertible swap operator defined in
Choi:2024rjm
and our symmetry operator (
F.23
).
Throughout this appendix, the elements of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
will be denoted additively, i.e.,
\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\{0,1\}
.

G.1
Cluster model

Let us first recall the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-cluster model on a square lattice with periodic boundary conditions.
The state space of the model is given by

\mathcal{H}=\left(\bigotimes_{i\in P}\mathbb{C}^{2}\right)\otimes\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}\left(\bigotimes_{[ij]\in P}\mathbb{C}^{2}\right),

(G.1)

where
P
is the set of the plaquettes,
E
is the set of the edges, and
\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}
is the projector that imposes the flatness condition on the qubits on the edges.
84
84

84

In
Choi:2024rjm
, the flatness condition is not imposed at the level of the state space. As a result, the 1-form symmetry operators on the lattice become non-topological. In what follows, we impose the flatness condition so that the 1-form symmetry operators become topological.

The Hamiltonian of the model is given by

H_{\text{cluster}}=-\sum_{i\in P}X^{(i)}\prod_{[ij]\in\partial i}X^{(ij)}-\sum_{[ij]\in E}Z^{(i)}Z^{(ij)}Z^{(j)},

(G.2)

where
X^{(i)}
and
Z^{(i)}
are the Pauli operators acting on the qubit on plaquette
i\in P
, while
X^{(ij)}
and
Z^{(ij)}
are those acting on the qubit on edge
[ij]\in E
.
The above Hamiltonian has a unique ground state given by

\ket{\text{cluster}}:=\frac{1}{2^{|P|/2}}\sum_{\{n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\}}\ket{\{n_{i};n_{i}+n_{j}\bmod 2\}},

(G.3)

where
|P|
is the number of plaquettes, and
\ket{\{n_{i};n_{ij}\}}:=\left(\bigotimes_{i\in P}\ket{n_{i}}_{i}\right)\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{[ij]\in E}\ket{n_{ij}}_{ij}\right)
for
n_{i},n_{ij}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
In what follows,
n_{i}+n_{j}\bmod 2
will be written as
n_{i}+n_{j}
when no confusion can arise.

The Hamiltonian (
G.2
) has a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
0-form and a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
1-form symmetries, which are generated by

X:=\bigotimes_{i\in P}X^{(i)},\qquad W(\gamma):=\bigotimes_{[ij]\in E_{\gamma}}Z^{(ij)},

(G.4)

where
\gamma
is an arbitrary oriented loop on the dual lattice, and
E_{\gamma}
is the set of the edges that intersect
\gamma
.
In addition to these symmetry operators, the Hamiltonian (
G.2
) also commutes with a non-invertible symmetry operator defined by
Choi:2024rjm
(see also Appendix
G.3
)

\mathsf{D}=\mathsf{D}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{[0]}}\mathsf{D}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{[1]}},

(G.5)

where
\mathsf{D}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{[0]}}
and
\mathsf{D}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{[1]}}
are the gauging operators for the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
0-form and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
1-form symmetries.
The action of
\mathsf{D}
can be written explicitly as

\mathsf{D}\ket{\{n_{i};n_{ij}\}}=\sum_{\{m_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\}}\left(\prod_{[ij]\in E}\delta_{n_{ij},m_{i}+m_{j}}\right)\ket{\{m_{i};n_{i}+n_{j}\}}.

(G.6)

The symmetry generated by (
G.4
) and (
G.5
) is described by the Tambara-Yamagami fusion 2-category

2\text{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2},1)\cong 2\text{Rep}((\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{[1]}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{[1]})\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{[0]}).

(G.7)

Thus, the cluster model can be regarded as a model of an SPT phase with Tambara-Yamagami symmetry.

Gauging
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
1-form symmetry.

Let us gauge the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
1-form symmetry to identify the SPT phase realized in the above model.
The state space of the gauged model is given by

\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}=\bigotimes_{i\in P}(\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}).

(G.8)

Namely, we have a pair of qubits on each plaquette.
The Hamiltonian of the gauged model is

\widetilde{H}_{\text{cluster}}=-\sum_{i\in P}X^{(i)}\widetilde{X}^{(i)}-\sum_{[ij]\in E}Z^{(i)}\widetilde{Z}^{(i)}Z^{(j)}\widetilde{Z}^{(j)},

(G.9)

where
\widetilde{X}^{(i)}
and
\widetilde{Z}^{(i)}
are the Pauli operators acting on the second qubit on the plaquette
i\in P
.
85
85

85

The gauging of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
1-form symmetry effectively changes
\prod_{[ij]\in\partial i}X^{(ij)}
and
Z^{(ij)}
into
\widetilde{X}^{(i)}
and
\widetilde{Z}^{(i)}\widetilde{Z}^{(j)}
.

This model has an invertible 0-form symmetry generated by

X=\bigotimes_{i\in P}X^{(i)},\qquad\widetilde{X}=\bigotimes_{i\in P}\widetilde{X}^{(i)},\qquad S=\bigotimes_{i\in P}\mathrm{SWAP}^{(i)},

(G.10)

where
\mathrm{SWAP}^{(i)}
is defined by

\mathrm{SWAP}^{(i)}\ket{n_{i},\widetilde{n}_{i}}_{i}=\ket{\widetilde{n}_{i},n_{i}}_{i}.

(G.11)

The algebra of the symmetry operators (
G.10
) is

X^{2}=\widetilde{X}^{2}=S^{2}=1,\qquad X\widetilde{X}=\widetilde{X}X,\qquad XS=S\widetilde{X}.

(G.12)

This implies that the symmetry group of the gauged model is
(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}\cong D_{8}
, where the first two
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
’s are generated by
X
and
\widetilde{X}
, while the third
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is generated by
S
.

The Hamiltonian (
G.9
) has twofold degenerate ground states

\ket{\text{GS};1}:=\bigotimes_{i\in P}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket{00}_{i}+\ket{11}_{i}),\qquad\ket{\text{GS};2}:=\bigotimes_{i\in P}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket{01}_{i}+\ket{10}_{i}).

(G.13)

The symmetry operators (
G.10
) act on these ground states as

X\ket{\text{GS};1}=\widetilde{X}\ket{\text{GS};1}=\ket{\text{GS};2},\qquad S\ket{\text{GS};1}=\ket{\text{GS};1},\qquad S\ket{\text{GS};2}=\ket{\text{GS};2}.

(G.14)

Therefore, the symmetry
(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is spontaneouly broken down to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{diag}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
generated by
X\widetilde{X}
and
S
.
This shows that the
2\text{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2},1)
SPT phase realized in the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-cluster model (
G.2
) corresponds to
K=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\text{diag}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

G.2
Cluster
′
model

We now review the clutser
′
model in
Choi:2024rjm
, which realizes another SPT phase with Tambara-Yamagami symmetry
2\text{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2},1)
.
The state space of the model is again given by

\mathcal{H}=\left(\bigotimes_{i\in P}\mathbb{C}^{2}\right)\otimes\hat{\pi}_{\text{flat}}\left(\bigotimes_{[ij]\in E}\mathbb{C}^{2}\right).

(G.15)

The Hamiltonian of the model is

H_{\text{cluster${}^{\prime}$}}=\sum_{i\in P}X^{(i)}\prod_{[ij]\in\partial i}X^{(ij)}-\sum_{[ij]\in E}Y^{(i)}Z^{(ij)}Y^{(j)}+\Delta_{\text{cluster}^{\prime}},

(G.16)

where the last term
\Delta_{\text{cluster}^{\prime}}
is defined by

\Delta_{\text{cluster}^{\prime}}=-\sum_{[ij]\in E}\left(X^{(i)}\prod_{[ii^{\prime}]\in\partial i}X^{(ii^{\prime})}\right)Y^{(i)}Z^{(ij)}Y^{(j)}\left(X^{(j)}\prod_{[jj^{\prime}]\in\partial j}X^{(jj^{\prime})}\right).

(G.17)

We note that each local term of
\Delta_{\text{cluster}^{\prime}}
is the product of the local terms in the first two sums in (
G.16
).
This implies that
\Delta_{\text{cluster}^{\prime}}
does not affect the ground state because the ground state is uniquely determined as the stabilizer of the first two terms.
As shown in
Choi:2024rjm
, the Hamiltonian (
G.16
) commutes with the symmetry operators (
G.4
) and (
G.5
).
Thus, the cluster
′
model realizes an SPT phase with Tambara-Yamagami symmetry
2\text{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2},1)
.

To find the ground state, we note that the cluster
′
Hamiltonian (
G.16
) is related to the cluster Hamiltonian (
G.2
) as

H_{\text{cluster}^{\prime}}=U(H_{\text{cluster}}+\Delta_{\text{cluster}})U^{-1},

(G.18)

where
U
and
\Delta_{\text{cluster}}
are defined by

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle=\bigotimes_{i\in P}Z^{(i)}e^{i\pi X^{(i)}/4},

(G.19)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\text{cluster}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{[ij]\in E}\left(X^{(i)}\prod_{[ii^{\prime}]\in\partial i}X^{(ii^{\prime})}\right)Z^{(i)}Z^{(ij)}Z^{(j)}\left(X^{(j)}\prod_{[jj^{\prime}]\in\partial j}X^{(jj^{\prime})}\right).

(G.20)

Each local term of
\Delta_{\text{cluster}}
is the product of the local terms of the cluster Hamiltonian (
G.2
).
Hence, the ground state of
H_{\text{cluster}}+\Delta_{\text{cluster}}
agrees with that of
H_{\text{cluster}}
, which is the cluster state (
G.3
).
Thus, due to (
G.18
), the ground state of
H_{\text{cluster}^{\prime}}
is given by

\ket{\text{cluster}^{\prime}}:=U\ket{\text{cluster}}.

(G.21)

Since
U
is the tensor product of on-site unitary operators, the ground state
\ket{\text{cluster}^{\prime}}
has the same entanglement structure as the cluster state.
Nevertheless, as shown in
Choi:2024rjm
, the cluster
′
state (
G.21
) and the cluster state (
G.3
) are in different SPT phases with
2\text{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2},1)
symmetry.

Gauging
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
1-form symmetry.

Let us gauge the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
1-form symmetry to identify the SPT phase realized in the cluster
′
model.
The state space of the gauge model is given by

\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}=\bigotimes_{i\in P}(\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}).

(G.22)

The Hamiltonian of the gauge model is

\widetilde{H}_{\text{cluster}^{\prime}}=U(\widetilde{H}_{\text{cluster}}+\widetilde{\Delta}_{\text{cluster}})U^{-1},

(G.23)

where
U
is the unitary operator (
G.19
),
86
86

86

The conjugation by
U
commutes with the gauging operation because
U
does not act on the qubits on the edges.

\widetilde{H}_{\text{cluster}}
is the gauged cluster Hamiltonian (
G.9
), and
\widetilde{\Delta}_{\text{cluster}}
is the gauged version of (
G.20
), which is given by

\widetilde{\Delta}_{\text{cluster}}=-\sum_{[ij]\in E}X^{(i)}\widetilde{X}^{(i)}Z^{(i)}\widetilde{Z}^{(i)}Z^{(j)}\widetilde{Z}^{(j)}X^{(j)}\widetilde{X}^{(j)}.

(G.24)

The Hamiltonian
\widetilde{H}_{\text{cluster}}+\widetilde{\Delta}_{\text{cluster}}
has the twofold degenerate ground states (
G.13
).
Therefore, the ground states of
\widetilde{H}_{\text{cluster}^{\prime}}
are given by

\displaystyle\ket{\text{GS}^{\prime};1}:=U\ket{\text{GS};1}=\bigotimes_{i\in P}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}U^{(i)}(\ket{00}_{i}+\ket{11}_{i}),

(G.25)

\displaystyle\ket{\text{GS}^{\prime};2}:=U\ket{\text{GS};2}=\bigotimes_{i\in P}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}U^{(i)}(\ket{01}_{i}+\ket{10}_{i}),

where
U^{(i)}:=Z^{(i)}e^{i\pi X^{(i)}/4}
is the on-site unitary of
U
.
One can compute the action of the symmetry operators (
G.10
) on the ground states (
G.25
) by using the commutation relations

\displaystyle(U^{(i)})^{-1}X^{(i)}U^{(i)}

\displaystyle=-X^{(i)},\qquad(U^{(i)})^{-1}\widetilde{X}^{(i)}U^{(i)}=\widetilde{X}^{(i)},

(G.26)

\displaystyle(U^{(i)})^{-1}\mathrm{SWAP}^{(i)}U^{(i)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(1-X^{(i)}\widetilde{X}^{(i)}-Z^{(i)}\widetilde{Y}^{(i)}-Y^{(i)}\widetilde{Z}^{(i)}).

Here, the last equality follows from the following expression of the SWAP operator:

\mathrm{SWAP}^{(i)}=\frac{1}{2}(1+X^{(i)}\widetilde{X}^{(i)}+Y^{(i)}\widetilde{Y}^{(i)}+Z^{(i)}\widetilde{Z}^{(i)}).

(G.27)

Based on (
G.25
), (
G.26
), and (
G.14
), we can compute the symmetry action on the ground states as

\displaystyle X\ket{\text{GS}^{\prime};1}

\displaystyle=U(U^{-1}XU)\ket{\text{GS};1}=(-1)^{|P|}\ket{\text{GS}^{\prime};2},

(G.28)

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}\ket{\text{GS}^{\prime};1}

\displaystyle=U(U^{-1}\widetilde{X}U)\ket{\text{GS};1}=\ket{\text{GS}^{\prime};2},

(G.29)

\displaystyle S\ket{\text{GS}^{\prime};1}

\displaystyle=U(U^{-1}SU)\ket{\text{GS};1}=(-i)^{|P|}\ket{\text{GS}^{\prime};2}.

(G.30)

This shows that the unbroken symmetry operators are
\{1,XS,\widetilde{X}S,X\widetilde{X}\}
, which form
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
because

(XS)^{2}=X\widetilde{X},\qquad(XS)^{3}=\widetilde{X}S,\qquad(XS)^{4}=1.

(G.31)

Thus, the symmetry
(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is spontaneously broken down to
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
, which implies that the
2\text{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2},1)
SPT phase realized in the cluster
′
model (
G.16
) corresponds to
K=\mathbb{Z}_{4}
.

G.3
Non-invertible swap operator

In
Choi:2024rjm
, it was shown that the Hamiltonians (
G.2
) and (
G.16
) commute with the following operator called a non-invertible swap operator:

\mathsf{D}_{\text{SWAP}}=\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{C}_{U}\mathsf{C}_{\eta}\mathsf{S}.

(G.32)

Here, the operators on the right-hand side are defined by
87
87

87

The operator (
G.32
) is the Hermitian conjugate of the original non-invertible swap operator defined in
Choi:2024rjm
. It was shown in
Choi:2024rjm
that this operator is Hermitian.

\mathsf{C}_{U}=1+\prod_{i\in P}X^{(i)},\qquad\mathsf{C}_{\eta}=\frac{1}{2^{|P|}}\sum_{\gamma:\text{loops}}W(\gamma),\qquad\mathsf{S}=S_{i_{0}}\left(\prod_{i\in P\setminus\{i_{0}\}}S_{i}\right),

(G.33)

where
i_{0}\in P
is an arbitrary plaquette and
S_{i}
is defined by

S_{i}=\frac{1}{2}\left(1+G_{i}+\left(1-G_{i}\right)W^{(i_{0}i)}Z^{(i)}\right),\qquad G_{i}=X^{(i)}\prod_{[ij]\in\partial i}X^{(ij)}.

(G.34)

The operator
W^{(i_{0}i)}
is the Wilson line on a path between
i_{0}
and
i
(cf. Figure
5
), and
W(\gamma)
is the Wilson line on the loop
\gamma
.

Figure 5:
The Wilson line
W^{(i_{0}i)}
is the product of the Pauli-Z operators on the edges intersecting a path between
i_{0}
and
i
. This operator does not depend on the choice of a path because we imposed the flatness condition on the state space.

The summation
\sum_{\gamma}
in (
G.33
) is taken over all (unoriented) loops on the dual lattice.
We note that
\mathsf{D}_{\text{SWAP}}
does not depend on the choice of
i_{0}

Choi:2024rjm
.
In what follows, we will show that the non-invertible swap operator (
G.32
) agrees with our symmetry operator (
F.23
), i.e.,

\mathsf{D}_{\text{SWAP}}=\mathsf{D}_{(\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{R}}\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{(\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{L}},

(G.35)

where
\mathsf{D}_{(\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{R}}=\mathsf{D}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{[0]}}
and
\overline{\mathsf{D}}_{(\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{L}}=\mathsf{D}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{[1]}}
are the gauging operators for the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
0-form and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
1-form symmetries.

To show (
G.35
), we first compute the action of
\mathsf{S}
on a state
\ket{\{n_{i};n_{ij}\}}
.
To this end, we notice that the action of each
S_{i}
for any
i\in P
can be written as

S_{i}\ket{\{n_{i};n_{ij}\}}=G_{i}^{n_{i_{0}i}+n_{i}}\ket{\{n_{i};n_{ij}\}},

(G.36)

where
n_{i_{0}i}
is the eigenvalue of
W^{(i_{0}i)}
, that is,
n_{i_{0}i}=\sum_{[jk]}n_{jk}\bmod 2
, where the summation is taken over all edges that intersect the path between
i_{0}
and
i
.
In particular, we define
n_{i_{0}i_{0}}=0
.
Using (
G.36
), we can compute the action of
\mathsf{S}
on
\ket{\{n_{i};n_{ij}\}}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}\ket{\{n_{i};n_{ij}\}}

\displaystyle=S_{i_{0}}\left(\prod_{i\in P\setminus\{i_{0}\}}G_{i}^{n_{i_{0}i}+n_{i}}\right)\ket{\{n_{i};n_{ij}\}}=S_{i_{0}}G_{i_{0}}^{n_{i_{0}}}\left(\prod_{i\in P}G_{i}^{n_{i_{0}i}+n_{i}}\right)\ket{\{n_{i};n_{ij}\}}

(G.37)

\displaystyle=S_{i_{0}}G_{i_{0}}^{n_{i_{0}}}\ket{\{n_{i_{0}i};n_{i}+n_{j}\}}=\frac{1}{2}\left(1+G_{i_{0}}+(1-G_{i_{0}})(-1)^{n_{i_{0}}}\right)G_{i_{0}}^{n_{i_{0}}}\ket{\{n_{i_{0}i};n_{i}+n_{j}\}}

\displaystyle=\ket{\{n_{i_{0}i};n_{i}+n_{j}\}}.

The action of the non-invertible swap operator (
G.32
) can then be computed as

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{\text{SWAP}}\ket{\{n_{i};n_{ij}\}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{C}_{U}\mathsf{C}_{\eta}\ket{\{n_{i_{0}i};n_{i}+n_{j}\}}=\ket{\{n_{i_{0}i};n_{i}+n_{j}\}}+\ket{n_{i_{0}i}+1;n_{i}+n_{j}\}}

(G.38)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\{m_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\}}\left(\prod_{[ij]\in E}\delta_{m_{i}+m_{j},n_{ij}}\right)\ket{\{m_{i};n_{i}+n_{j}\}}.

Here, the last equality follows from the fact that
\{m_{i}=n_{i_{0}i}\mid i\in P\}
and
\{m_{i}=n_{i_{0}i}+1\mid i\in P\}
are the only solutions to the condition

m_{i}+m_{j}=n_{ij}\bmod 2,\qquad\forall[ij]\in E.

(G.39)

Equations (
G.38
) and (
F.24
) show that (
G.35
) holds.
